# Part I: The argument


## Introduction: The enterprise GenAI stack, layer by layer

*Series introduction, from Post 0 of the series.*

### The post

Over the next twelve weeks I am going to take the enterprise GenAI stack apart, one layer at a time. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Most of us have shared some version of the popular stack diagram: nine layers, rows of product logos. It is a useful map. But by the end of Q3 2026, 39 of its 80 tiles needed a correction. Acquired, renamed, mispositioned, superseded. The logos move faster than any diagram can. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

So I asked a narrower question. What would a regulated asset manager actually need to run GenAI safely in production? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The answer became a review of 140 products across nine layers and eight cross-cutting controls, built on 1,255 logged sources and checked by two independent verifiers. The finding that shaped everything else: the architecture that matters is the control plane around the products, not the products themselves. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The series follows that shape. Each week takes one layer of the stack, from foundation models to evaluation, and then the control that makes it safe. One worked example runs throughout: an agent that drafts the monthly performance-attribution commentary for a multi-asset fund, with a portfolio manager approving every draft. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The posts share judgement, not vendor rankings. No vendor is named in the post itself; sources sit in the first comment. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: parts of this will date. Versions, owners and prices move monthly, so each post is re-checked in the week it goes out, and where I get something wrong I will say so. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

If one of these weeks saves you a quarter of rework, the series will have done its job. The first post is on foundation models. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

![The enterprise GenAI stack, layer by layer](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P00.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 0. Each week, one layer of the stack, L1 to L9, then the control that makes it safe. One worked example throughout: an agent drafting a fund's monthly attribution commentary, approved by a portfolio manager.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

The tension in this introduction is simple to state. The logos move faster than any diagram, and the controls around them are what last [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Everything else in this book is an attempt to show why that is true, and what to do about it.

The starting point was a widely shared picture of the GenAI stack: nine layers and 80 product tiles. It is a good teaching device, and its nine-layer spine still maps the work [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. But when the review checked each tile against primary sources, and two adversarial verifiers re-checked the high-risk claims, 39 of the 80 tiles needed a correction: 9 acquired, 9 mispositioned, 8 renamed, 8 with a wrong version label, 6 not publicly verifiable, 4 duplicated, 3 superseded and 2 deprecated, with some tiles carrying more than one flag [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Nobody did anything wrong in drawing it. The market simply moved.

It moved in a particular direction. Much of the tooling that firms think of as neutral now belongs to a platform vendor. Dynatrace completed its acquisition of Arize on 1 October 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S045: Dynatrace Completes Acquisition of Arize, Extending AI Observability Across the Full Development Lifecycle (Dynatrace, Inc.) <https://ir.dynatrace.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/439/dynatrace-completes-acquisition-of-arize-extending-ai-observability-across-the-full-development-lifecycle> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S005: Dynatrace Completes Acquisition of Arize (IR press release) (Dynatrace, Inc.) <https://ir.dynatrace.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/439/dynatrace-completes-acquisition-of-arize-extending-ai-observability-across-the-full-development-lifecycle> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. ClickHouse announced that it had acquired Langfuse on 16 January 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S021: ClickHouse raises $400M Series D, acquires Langfuse, launches Postgres (ClickHouse, Inc.) <https://clickhouse.com/blog/clickhouse-raises-400-million-series-d-acquires-langfuse-launches-postgres> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S041: ClickHouse welcomes Langfuse (with langfuse.com 'Langfuse joins ClickHouse', Orrick) <https://clickhouse.com/blog/clickhouse-acquires-langfuse-open-source-llm-observability> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Palo Alto Networks bought Portkey and Protect AI, Check Point bought Lakera, Harvey bought Guardrails AI, and Mintlify bought Helicone [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S011: Palo Alto Networks Completes Acquisition of Portkey to Secure AI Agents (and 30 Apr 2026 announcement) <https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/company/press/2026/palo-alto-networks-completes-acquisition-of-portkey-to-secure-ai-agents> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S014: Palo Alto Networks Completes Acquisition of Protect AI <https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/company/press/2025/palo-alto-networks-completes-acquisition-of-protect-ai> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S039: Palo Alto Networks Completes Acquisition of Protect AI <https://investors.paloaltonetworks.com/news-releases/news-release-details/palo-alto-networks-completes-acquisition-protect-ai/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A7-S012: Check Point to Acquire Lakera, Redefining Security for the AI era (Check Point Software) <https://blog.checkpoint.com/security/check-point-to-acquire-lakera-redefining-security-for-the-ai-era/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S028: Guardrails AI Joins Harvey <https://www.harvey.ai/blog/guardrails-ai-joins-harvey> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S112: Helicone Blog: Helicone acquired by Mintlify (and Mintlify announcement) (Helicone / Mintlify) <https://www.helicone.ai/blog> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S043: Helicone Joins Mintlify (with mintlify.com 'Mintlify acquires Helicone'; PostHog comparison) (Helicone / Mintlify) <https://www.helicone.ai/blog/joining-mintlify> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Independence, exit planning and effective challenge can no longer be assumed from a product's origins; they have to be designed in [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The models moved too. Every major vendor now sells a tiered family rather than a single model, the most capable tiers are gated, and lifetimes are short: a Gemini Flash version released on 13 August 2026 retires on 28 January 2027 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S002: GPT-6 Astra: A new generation of intelligence (plus release notes, Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna, GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6 for everyone) (OpenAI) <https://openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S003: OpenAI announces rollout of GPT-6 Astra model (with Axios and OpenRouter results) (CNBC) <https://www.cnbc.com/2026/09/03/open-ai-astra-gpt-6-cyber.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S016: Introducing Claude Fable 5.1 and Claude Mythos 5.1 (Anthropic) <https://www.anthropic.com/claude-fable-and-mythos-5-1> accessed 2026-10-07; A5-S031: Gemini 4 Argon: our next era of frontier intelligence (Google) <https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/gemini-4-argon/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; B-L1-S003: Gemini 3.7 Flash model page and related lifecycle pages (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/models/gemini/3-7-flash> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Model choice has become a portfolio and lifecycle discipline, not a one-off selection [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

And the rules moved. In the United States, SR 26-2 superseded SR 11-7 on 17 April 2026 and places generative and agentic AI outside its scope [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S002: OCC Bulletin 2026-13 Model Risk Management: Revised Guidance (attachment bulletin-2026-13a.pdf) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/bulletins/2026/bulletin-2026-13.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The EU AI Act's enforcement powers over general-purpose models started on 2 August 2026, and Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 moved the Annex III high-risk duties to 2 December 2027 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-EU-OMNIBUS-AI: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 ('Digital Omnibus on AI') amending Regulations (EU) 2024/1689, 2018/1139 and 2023/1230 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S011: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) amending Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (EUR-Lex / Official Journal of the EU) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The DORA critical-provider list and the UK critical third-party designations cover hyperscalers and no model vendor [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-DORA: Regulation (EU) 2022/2554 Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S021: Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) pages and RoI reporting FAQ (ESMA / EBA / EIOPA) <https://www.esma.europa.eu/esmas-activities/digital-finance-and-innovation/digital-operational-resilience-act-dora> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; R-UK-CTP: UK Critical Third Parties regime (FSMA 2023 / FSMA 2000 s312L; PRA/FCA/BoE CTP rules) and the Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S023: The Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777); BoE/FCA/HMT announcements (HM Treasury / legislation.gov.uk / Bank of England) <https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2026/777/made> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. In the UK, PRA PS7/26 and FCA PS26/2 require material third-party notifications from 18 March 2027 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-FCA-SYSC8: FCA Handbook SYSC 8 (Outsourcing); FG16/5 'Guidance for firms outsourcing to the cloud and other third-party IT services'; PS26/2 operational incident and third-party reporting (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S062: PS7/26 Operational incident and third-party reporting (PRA) and FCA PS26/2; revised SS2/21 (March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA; FCA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2026/march/operational-incident-and-third-party-reporting-policy-statement> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. A regulated firm therefore has to write much of its own GenAI standard [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Put those three movements together and the question changes. It stops being "which tools?" and becomes "what has to stay true whichever tools we use?" [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The review's answer is a control plane of eight components: the gateway, guardrails, data protection, identity, configuration, FinOps, AI security, and governance with its evidence store [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The gateway has already become the place where model, tool and agent traffic is governed; LiteLLM, Kong, Azure API Management, Apigee and agentgateway all govern model, MCP and agent-to-agent traffic [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S015: LiteLLM docs: MCP Overview, Agent Gateway (A2A), Caching, Budgets/Rate Limits, v1.89.0 release notes (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/docs/mcp> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S016: Kong AI Gateway 2.0 Is Now GA — And It's Already Moving Faster (Kong Inc.) <https://konghq.com/blog/product-releases/kong-ai-gateway-2-0-ga> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S053: AI gateway capabilities in Azure API Management (Microsoft Learn source, ms.date 29 May 2026) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-docs/main/articles/api-management/genai-gateway-capabilities.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S024: Apigee AI solutions: gateway to enterprise-ready AI (Google Cloud) <https://cloud.google.com/solutions/apigee-ai> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S061: agentgateway README (GitHub) (agentgateway project (Linux Foundation)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/agentgateway/agentgateway/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. The products beneath the plane are replaceable. The plane is not [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The evidence base is worth stating plainly, because the book depends on it. The review covers 140 product records across nine layers and eight controls, of which 138 were scored, and 1,255 logged sources [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Every fact carries a source and an access date, and facts that could not be confirmed were marked "not publicly verified" rather than guessed [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Vendor benchmark figures were never used as decision inputs [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The credit for that discipline belongs to the research streams and the two verifiers whose job was to disagree.

One worked example runs through the book. An agent drafts the monthly Brinson-style attribution commentary for a generic multi-asset fund, covering allocation, selection and currency effects, and a portfolio manager approves every draft [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It is illustrative, not a description of any firm. It was chosen because its one failure that matters, a wrong number in a client document, is easy to state and hard to excuse [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

A word on how the book is arranged. Part II takes the nine layers in order, from foundation models (Chapter 1) to evaluation and observability (Chapter 17), and follows each with the control that pairs with it, so that Chapter 2 on cost follows Chapter 1 on models, and Chapter 4 on the gateway follows Chapter 3 on inference [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Part III, Chapters 19 to 24, puts the pieces together: the worked example end to end, starting small, build versus buy, abstraction, lock-in and a closing selection [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Each chapter stands alone.

Finally, a disclosure that matters for a book about vendors. The review and these chapters were researched and drafted with help from an AI model made by Anthropic [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Anthropic's products were scored on the same rubric as everyone else's, and wherever an Anthropic product or standard is named, an independent alternative is named beside it: OpenAI, Google or Mistral models next to Claude, for example, and OpenAPI-described tools behind the same gateway next to MCP [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The introduction has no single metric of its own, but the whole book rests on one: **control-plane coverage**. It is the share of production GenAI calls (model, tool and agent) that pass through the firm's own gateway, and for which a complete evidence record exists: model and version, route and region, guard outcomes, cost and the human approver [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Measure it by reconciling provider usage reports against the gateway log, scanning code repositories for provider keys held outside the gateway, and sampling regulated outputs to check that each one can be traced to its evidence record [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A direct provider key in an application is a defect, not a shortcut [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

A reasonable starting target is 100% of in-scope production traffic through the gateway, and an evidence record for every regulated output [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Treat these as a starting point, not a benchmark. They are the review's own target guidance, not an industry statistic, and each firm should set its own scope and thresholds [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**The brief.** The use case and its boundary: the agent writes words around numbers it is given; it never produces a number, and a named portfolio manager approves every draft. The design file starts empty; each chapter adds one piece, and Appendix A lists every step [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"Architecture first is slower. The business wants results this quarter."** It is slower for the first use case. It is much faster for the second and the tenth [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A control plane that exists before the first production workload means each later use case inherits the gateway, the evaluation suite and the evidence store rather than rebuilding them [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Some of the work cannot be skipped anyway. PRA SS1/23 expects independent validation and ongoing monitoring, including of vendor models [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S008: SS1/23 Model risk management principles for banks (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2023/may/model-risk-management-principles-for-banks-ss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S037: Bank of England/PRA letter to DSIT and HMT on AI (2024) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/letter/2024/dsit-hmt-letter.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Without an evaluation suite there is nothing for a validator to re-perform, so the first use case cannot go live under the firm's own standard [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Chapter 20 shows how small the first version can be.

**"Any landscape this detailed will be out of date within months."** True, and the post says so [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That is precisely why the book argues from responsibilities and controls rather than from logos. The product named in a chapter may change; the duty it performs, and the evidence a supervisor will ask for, change far more slowly [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Each fact carries its date, so a reader can see what to re-check [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"A book drafted with an AI vendor's model cannot be neutral about AI vendors."** The concern is fair, which is why it is disclosed rather than hidden [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The defence is method, not assurance: one rubric for every product, borderline calls on Anthropic-related items resolved against them, the tier decisions taken by the human reader at checkpoints, and an independent alternative named wherever an Anthropic product appears [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Readers should still test the reasoning for themselves.

### Questions for your team

- If our most-used model vendor withdrew access tomorrow, how long would it take us to move, and have we ever tried? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- What share of our production AI traffic passes through a gateway we control, and how do we know? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which of the tools we treat as "independent" have changed owner in the last year? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- For one regulated output, can we produce the model version, the route, the guard results and the approver within an hour? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Who owns our GenAI standard, given that the US model-risk guidance now excludes generative and agentic AI? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which dates in 2027 does our roadmap already respect, and which has nobody yet put in a plan? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

The products will keep changing; build the control plane that lets you change them safely [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.


# Part II: Nine layers and the controls that make them safe


## 1. Treat foundation models as a portfolio, not a bet

*L1 Foundation models, from Post 1 of the series.*

### The post

Treat foundation models as a portfolio, not a bet. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Every major vendor now sells a tiered family rather than a single model, and lifetimes are short. One generally available model version released in August 2026 is already scheduled to retire in January 2027. Earlier this year, one leading vendor's top tier was withdrawn for nearly three weeks before being restored. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S002: GPT-6 Astra: A new generation of intelligence (plus release notes, Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna, GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6 for everyone) (OpenAI) <https://openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S004: OpenAI API Pricing (and Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna pricing) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S010: Models overview - Claude API Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/models/overview> accessed 2026-10-07; A5-S019: Introducing Claude Opus 5.5 (Anthropic) <https://www.anthropic.com/claude-opus-5-5> accessed 2026-10-07; B-L1-S003: Gemini 3.7 Flash model page and related lifecycle pages (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/models/gemini/3-7-flash> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; V2-S004: Claude Fable - Anthropic <https://www.anthropic.com/claude/fable> accessed 2026-10-08]

A firm that hard-codes one vendor cannot respond to either. Nor can a firm whose "fallback model" is a line in a design document that was never tested on the real workload. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

A portfolio does not need to be large. Two mid-tier models from unrelated vendors, a small model for high-volume classification, and one open-weight model the firm can run itself. All of them called through the gateway, pinned to exact versions rather than "latest", and each qualified on the same evaluation suite. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal is qualified-alternative coverage: the share of production use cases with a second model, from a different vendor, that passed the same evaluation within the last quarter. For an important business service the target is 100%. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to keep the frontier tier switched off until an evaluation shows the mid tier fails a named use case. And to remember that no model, at any tier, is fit to produce authoritative figures. Numbers come from the system of record. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: a portfolio multiplies validation and contract work. The answer is to keep it small and let the evaluation suite do the re-qualification, not to give up the second vendor. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Capability changes every quarter. The ability to change your mind safely is the asset worth building. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

![Treat foundation models as a portfolio, not a bet](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P01.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 1. Four model roles, each pinned, each qualified on the same evaluation suite, all behind one gateway. Generic and illustrative: no vendor names.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

An enterprise does not own the foundation-model layer by building models. It owns the choice, the contract, the configuration and the qualification of models it buys or runs [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That is a different job from picking a winner, and the market has made the difference sharper during 2025 and 2026.

**Families, not models.** Every major vendor now sells a tiered family. OpenAI's GPT-6 comes as Astra, Sol and Luna, with GPT-6.1 Sol alongside [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S002: GPT-6 Astra: A new generation of intelligence (plus release notes, Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna, GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6 for everyone) (OpenAI) <https://openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S004: OpenAI API Pricing (and Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna pricing) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Anthropic sells Claude Fable 5.1 above Opus, Sonnet and Haiku 5.5 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S010: Models overview - Claude API Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/models/overview> accessed 2026-10-07; A5-S019: Introducing Claude Opus 5.5 (Anthropic) <https://www.anthropic.com/claude-opus-5-5> accessed 2026-10-07]. Google runs Gemini 3.x alongside a restricted Gemini 4 Argon [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S030: Gemini API Release notes (with Models, Deprecations, 'What's new in Gemini 3.8 Flash' pages) (Google) <https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/changelog> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S031: Gemini 4 Argon: our next era of frontier intelligence (Google) <https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/gemini-4-argon/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Within one family, list prices span two orders of magnitude, from US$0.10 to US$10 per million input tokens in both the OpenAI and Anthropic ranges [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S004: OpenAI API Pricing (and Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna pricing) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S011: Pricing - Claude API Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07]. The tier is a design decision, not a badge.

**The top is gated.** The most capable tiers are no longer simply for sale. OpenAI rates Astra "Critical" for cybersecurity and requires enterprise administrators to enable it; Anthropic restricts Mythos 5.1; Google released Argon first to cyber defenders [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S002: GPT-6 Astra: A new generation of intelligence (plus release notes, Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna, GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6 for everyone) (OpenAI) <https://openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S003: OpenAI announces rollout of GPT-6 Astra model (with Axios and OpenRouter results) (CNBC) <https://www.cnbc.com/2026/09/03/open-ai-astra-gpt-6-cyber.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S016: Introducing Claude Fable 5.1 and Claude Mythos 5.1 (Anthropic) <https://www.anthropic.com/claude-fable-and-mythos-5-1> accessed 2026-10-07; A5-S031: Gemini 4 Argon: our next era of frontier intelligence (Google) <https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/gemini-4-argon/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Gated tiers are off by default for a reason, and they should stay off unless a named use case needs them [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Lifetimes are short and notice periods vary.** The two events in the post are both on the record. A Gemini Flash version released on 13 August 2026 retires on 28 January 2027 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L1-S003: Gemini 3.7 Flash model page and related lifecycle pages (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/models/gemini/3-7-flash> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Anthropic's top tier, Claude Fable 5, was unavailable from 12 June 2026 and restored from 1 July 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V2-S004: Claude Fable - Anthropic <https://www.anthropic.com/claude/fable> accessed 2026-10-08]. The lesson is about every vendor, not one. OpenAI gives at least six months' notice for generally available models and as little as about two weeks for previews; Mistral's Labs models may go with one month's notice [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L1-S001: Deprecations (OpenAI API docs) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/deprecations> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; B-L1-S002: Models overview: deprecated and retired models (Mistral Docs) (Mistral AI) <https://docs.mistral.ai/models> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Mistral Medium 3.1 retired on 31 August 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L1-S002: Models overview: deprecated and retired models (Mistral Docs) (Mistral AI) <https://docs.mistral.ai/models> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. A version change is a model change, and a forced retirement is a forced re-validation [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The route matters as much as the model.** A model can be reached through the vendor's own API, through a hyperscaler, or as open weights the firm runs itself [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Each route has different data terms. Anthropic's first-party API offers only global or US inference geography, while the same models run in EU regions on Google Cloud and Bedrock [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S013: Data residency - Claude Platform Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/manage-claude/data-residency> accessed 2026-10-07; V2-S075: Data residency - Claude Platform Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/data-residency> accessed 2026-10-08; A5-S027: Generative AI pricing - Google Cloud (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform, formerly Vertex AI) <https://cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/generative-ai/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07]. OpenAI and Mistral both offer first-party EU endpoints [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S006: Data controls in the OpenAI platform (with Enterprise privacy, EU/UK data residency announcements) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/your-data> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S075: Mistral Help Centre: SOC 2 / ISO 27001; data storage location; ZDR; Regional inference docs; Trust Center (Mistral AI) <https://help.mistral.ai/en/articles/347638-do-you-have-soc-2-or-iso-27001-certification> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Residency also has a price: about 10% on several regional routes [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S011: Pricing - Claude API Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07; A5-S027: Generative AI pricing - Google Cloud (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform, formerly Vertex AI) <https://cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/generative-ai/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07; A5-S075: Mistral Help Centre: SOC 2 / ISO 27001; data storage location; ZDR; Regional inference docs; Trust Center (Mistral AI) <https://help.mistral.ai/en/articles/347638-do-you-have-soc-2-or-iso-27001-certification> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. For a regulated firm, the residency premium is the real price [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What the portfolio looks like.** The review's answer is deliberately small. Two mid-tier models from unrelated vendors, a small model for classification and routing, and one open-weight model the firm can run in its own estate, all reached through the gateway and qualified on the firm's own evaluation suite [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Pin exact versions; Google's own documentation recommends versioned model IDs for predictable behaviour [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L1-S003: Gemini 3.7 Flash model page and related lifecycle pages (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/models/gemini/3-7-flash> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. In the review's scoring, OpenAI and Mistral were Strategic, Anthropic Strategic on condition that it is consumed through a hyperscaler UK or EU region with a qualified non-Anthropic fallback, Gemini Strategic where Google Cloud is the primary cloud, and Gemma 4 Strategic as the small self-hosted tier [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Wherever a Claude model is the primary, the independent alternatives are GPT-6.1 Sol, Gemini 3.8 Flash or Mistral Medium 3.5 [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. No vendor benchmark, Anthropic's included, was used as a decision input [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Open weights split two decisions.** Open weights are now a serious option from US and EU vendors (gpt-oss, Gemma 4, Mistral) and from Chinese-origin vendors (DeepSeek, Qwen, Kimi, GLM) [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S005: Introducing gpt-oss (and gpt-oss-safeguard; help-centre open-weight article) (OpenAI) <https://openai.com/index/introducing-gpt-oss/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S034: Gemma 4: Byte for byte, the most capable open models (with Gemma releases page, Gemma 4 12B post) (Google) <https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/developers-tools/gemma-4/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S074: Mistral Medium 3.5 model card; Introducing Mistral 3 (Large 3, Ministral 3); Mistral-Medium-3.5-128B (Hugging Face) (Mistral AI) <https://docs.mistral.ai/models/model-cards/mistral-medium-3-5-26-04> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S063: DeepSeek V4 Preview Release (24 April 2026) and deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4-Pro model card (MIT) <https://api-docs.deepseek.com/news/news260424/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. That makes *where the model runs* and *who made it* separate questions [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. DeepSeek's own API stores data in the PRC [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S062: DeepSeek Privacy Policy; DeepSeek Open Platform Privacy Policy and Terms of Service <https://cdn.deepseek.com/policies/en-US/deepseek-privacy-policy.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The defensible position for a regulated firm is no Chinese-origin vendor APIs for client data, and Chinese-origin weights only by explicit policy, self-hosted and without tool access [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why no model produces numbers.** In the worked example, the attribution engine produces every figure through a read-only tool, and the model only writes narrative around it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A deterministic check compares every figure, sign and direction word in the draft with the engine's output, and a mismatch fails the draft whatever the model tier [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A fluent model is not a source of record.

**The regulatory frame.** PRA SS1/23 applies to all models used to inform business decisions, in-house or vendor, and is technology-agnostic [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S008: SS1/23 Model risk management principles for banks (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2023/may/model-risk-management-principles-for-banks-ss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S037: Bank of England/PRA letter to DSIT and HMT on AI (2024) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/letter/2024/dsit-hmt-letter.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. For an FCA solo-regulated manager it is not binding, but it is the natural benchmark [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. SR 26-2 places generative and agentic AI outside its scope, so US firms have to set their own standard [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S002: OCC Bulletin 2026-13 Model Risk Management: Revised Guidance (attachment bulletin-2026-13a.pdf) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/bulletins/2026/bulletin-2026-13.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The DORA and UK critical-provider regimes designate hyperscalers and no model vendor, so the oversight burden for a direct model API stays with the firm [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-DORA: Regulation (EU) 2022/2554 Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S021: Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) pages and RoI reporting FAQ (ESMA / EBA / EIOPA) <https://www.esma.europa.eu/esmas-activities/digital-finance-and-innovation/digital-operational-resilience-act-dora> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; R-UK-CTP: UK Critical Third Parties regime (FSMA 2023 / FSMA 2000 s312L; PRA/FCA/BoE CTP rules) and the Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S023: The Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777); BoE/FCA/HMT announcements (HM Treasury / legislation.gov.uk / Bank of England) <https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2026/777/made> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. From 18 March 2027, UK firms must notify material third-party arrangements before entering them, so qualifying a second vendor needs lead time [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-FCA-SYSC8: FCA Handbook SYSC 8 (Outsourcing); FG16/5 'Guidance for firms outsourcing to the cloud and other third-party IT services'; PS26/2 operational incident and third-party reporting (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S062: PS7/26 Operational incident and third-party reporting (PRA) and FCA PS26/2; revised SS2/21 (March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA; FCA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2026/march/operational-incident-and-third-party-reporting-policy-statement> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].

**The trade-off.** A portfolio multiplies validation and contract work [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Two vendors means two data-protection assessments, two contracts and two sets of retirement dates. The answer is to keep the portfolio small and make the evaluation suite do the re-qualification, not to give up the second vendor [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That work is unglamorous. It belongs to the platform, validation and procurement teams who keep the fallback real, and it is the reason a withdrawal becomes a configuration change rather than a crisis.

### What good looks like

The signal is **qualified-alternative coverage**: the share of production use cases that have a second model, from a different vendor, which has passed the same evaluation suite within the last quarter [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Measure it by joining the model inventory to the evaluation results. For each production use case, the inventory names the primary and the fallback; the evaluation platform shows the date and outcome of the fallback's last run on that use case's suite [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A fallback whose last pass is older than a quarter does not count.

The review's target guidance is 100% for important business services [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Two companion measures keep it honest. The version pinning rate, the share of production calls that name an exact model version rather than an alias, should be 100%, read from the gateway log [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Retirement runway, the days until the earliest announced retirement of any pinned model, should never fall below the re-qualification lead time, for example 90 days [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

These are a starting point, not a benchmark. They come from the review's KPI tables, not from an industry survey [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 1: models.** Four model roles, each pinned to an exact version: a primary drafting model, a fallback from a different vendor, a small classifier for reviewer edits, and a self-hosted open-weight model for anything touching unmasked client data. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** The agent has a primary and a qualified fallback in the approved region. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** No alias or preview model, and no model ever generates, rounds or corrects a figure. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** Model ID, version, route and region for every draft. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Qualified-alternative coverage: 100% for this use case. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"Two vendors doubles the work for a marginal gain."** It does add work: contracts, assessments, a second evaluation run [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The gain is not marginal, though. A three-week withdrawal of a vendor's top tier has already happened [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V2-S004: Claude Fable - Anthropic <https://www.anthropic.com/claude/fable> accessed 2026-10-08], and short retirement windows are now normal [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L1-S003: Gemini 3.7 Flash model page and related lifecycle pages (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/models/gemini/3-7-flash> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The work stays manageable if the portfolio stays small and the same evaluation suite qualifies every member [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. There is also a simpler test. If the firm's operational-resilience self-assessment already claims a multi-vendor fallback for an important business service, the second vendor is not optional; it is a statement the firm has made and must be able to evidence [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"The frontier model is better. Why default to the mid tier?"** Sometimes it is better, and the evaluation will say so. But the gated tiers carry cyber-capability ratings that keep them off by default [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S002: GPT-6 Astra: A new generation of intelligence (plus release notes, Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna, GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6 for everyone) (OpenAI) <https://openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A5-S016: Introducing Claude Fable 5.1 and Claude Mythos 5.1 (Anthropic) <https://www.anthropic.com/claude-fable-and-mythos-5-1> accessed 2026-10-07], and the frontier list price is several times the mid tier's: US$10 against US$2 per million input tokens in OpenAI's range [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S004: OpenAI API Pricing (and Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna pricing) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Switch it on for a named use case where the mid tier has failed, not as a habit [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Our cloud already offers several vendors' models, so we are diversified."** Partly. Consuming models through one hyperscaler concentrates on one designated provider [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-UK-CTP: UK Critical Third Parties regime (FSMA 2023 / FSMA 2000 s312L; PRA/FCA/BoE CTP rules) and the Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S023: The Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777); BoE/FCA/HMT announcements (HM Treasury / legislation.gov.uk / Bank of England) <https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2026/777/made> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], and the catalogue differs by cloud and region: Gemini is on Google Cloud only [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S027: Generative AI pricing - Google Cloud (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform, formerly Vertex AI) <https://cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/generative-ai/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07]. Keep a second route, such as a self-hosted open-weight model, for the stressed-exit case [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It may be lower quality than the primary. That is acceptable, because its job is continuity under stress, not first place in an evaluation [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- For each production use case, which second-vendor model has passed our evaluation suite in the last quarter? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Do any production calls use an alias such as "latest" rather than a pinned version? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- What is the earliest retirement date among our pinned models, and is re-qualification already scheduled? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Where, geographically, is each model route actually processing prompts, and is that written into the contract? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which use cases run on a frontier tier, and what evaluation result justified it? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Could any model in our estate produce a figure that reaches a client without being checked against the system of record? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

Capability will change every quarter, so build the ability to change models safely, and treat it as the asset [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.


## 2. Cost per task, not cost per token

*C6 AI FinOps, from Post 2 of the series.*

### The post

Cost per token is the number on the price list. Cost per task is the number executives understand, and the one that tells you whether a change saved anything. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

A cheaper token that needs three retries and a longer human review is not cheaper. Teams that optimise price per token can move to a smaller model and lose more in regeneration and review time than they saved. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

A portfolio of models needs a fair way to choose between them on cost. Meter every call at the gateway, tagged by use case and run. Join those records to the traces so that failed and regenerated attempts count. Reconcile monthly against provider bills. Keep the result in a dataset the firm owns, shaped to the open billing standard. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

In an illustrative cost model for a monthly fund commentary, at current list prices, tokens come to well under a dollar per approved commentary. Reviewer time is the real cost. So the signal to report is cost per approved output, counting every failed and regenerated draft, alongside the regeneration rate. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S011: Pricing - Claude API Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07; A5-S004: OpenAI API Pricing (and Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna pricing) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Budgets need teeth. The FinOps Foundation puts it plainly: a budget without a quota is a number, not a control. Every cap needs an enforcement point that fails closed, so a runaway agent loop stops itself long before the invoice arrives. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S115: FinOps Framework 2026; State of FinOps 2026; FinOps for AI technology category; Tokenomics working group; Who sets the AI budget? (FinOps Foundation) <https://www.finops.org/insights/2026-finops-framework/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to send cost optimisations through the same change control and evaluation gate as any other change. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: the open billing standard does not yet have a first-class column for input and output tokens, so part of the mapping is still the firm's own. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V2-S046: FOCUS 1.5 Release Scope: Confirmed and Stretch Features (with Introducing FOCUS 1.4; FOCUS for AI) (FinOps Foundation (FOCUS)) <https://focus.finops.org/focus-1-5-release-scope/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A7-S116: FOCUS 1.5 Release Scope; FOCUS for AI; Introducing FOCUS 1.2; Introducing FOCUS 1.4 (FinOps Foundation (FOCUS)) <https://focus.finops.org/focus-1-5-release-scope/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]

What a task costs is a design question. The invoice only reports the answer. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

![Cost per task, not cost per token](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P02.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 2. Illustrative arithmetic for one approved monthly fund commentary at list prices: tokens come to well under a dollar, and reviewer time is the real cost. Reviewer bar not to scale. Generic: no vendor names.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

GenAI spend behaves differently from the cloud spend most finance teams already understand. It scales with usage and with agent behaviour rather than with provisioned capacity, it can grow by orders of magnitude when an agent loops, and prices change quickly [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The discipline has caught up with that. The FinOps Foundation's State of FinOps 2026 reports that 98% of respondents now manage AI spend, and that FinOps for AI is the top forward-looking priority [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S115: FinOps Framework 2026; State of FinOps 2026; FinOps for AI technology category; Tokenomics working group; Who sets the AI budget? (FinOps Foundation) <https://www.finops.org/insights/2026-finops-framework/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].

**The unit is the task.** For a multi-step agent, the cost of one task is the sum of every planning call, tool-result summary, retry, evaluation-judge call and regeneration after a reviewer rejects a draft [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A price list cannot see any of that. Only the trace, which groups the calls that make up one task and records its outcome, can assemble the true cost [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That is why a cheaper token that needs three retries and a longer review is not cheaper.

**The mechanics have five stages: meter, attribute, reconcile, enforce and optimise** [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

*Meter at the gateway.* The measurement point has settled on the gateway, because every model call passes through it. LiteLLM, Cloudflare AI Gateway, Azure API Management, Apigee and Kong all now ship budgets, rate limits or spend tracking per key, team or user [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S015: LiteLLM docs: MCP Overview, Agent Gateway (A2A), Caching, Budgets/Rate Limits, v1.89.0 release notes (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/docs/mcp> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S019: Cloudflare AI Gateway docs: Pricing, Features, Changelog; Cloudflare blog spend limits (Cloudflare, Inc.) <https://developers.cloudflare.com/ai-gateway/reference/pricing/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S053: AI gateway capabilities in Azure API Management (Microsoft Learn source, ms.date 29 May 2026) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-docs/main/articles/api-management/genai-gateway-capabilities.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S024: Apigee AI solutions: gateway to enterprise-ready AI (Google Cloud) <https://cloud.google.com/solutions/apigee-ai> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S016: Kong AI Gateway 2.0 Is Now GA — And It's Already Moving Faster (Kong Inc.) <https://konghq.com/blog/product-releases/kong-ai-gateway-2-0-ga> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Give each use case its own virtual key, and tag each request with the run ID [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Shared keys destroy attribution [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

*Attribute to tasks.* The gateway knows the tokens per call; the evaluation and observability platform knows which calls form a task and whether the task was approved, edited or rejected [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Join them on the run ID and you have cost per completed task and cost per approved output [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

*Reconcile with the provider and the cloud.* Gateway prices are estimates from a price table, and invoices are the truth [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Each route has its own reconciliation source. Anthropic's Usage and Cost Admin API reports usage by key, workspace and model and separates cached from uncached input [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S070: Usage and Cost Admin API (Claude Platform docs) (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/manage-claude/usage-cost-api> accessed 2026-10-07]. Amazon Bedrock carries cost-allocation tags through application inference profiles, though only per usage type per day [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-C6-S004: Amazon Bedrock user guide: Application inference profiles; Best practices for cost attribution (Amazon Web Services) <https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-mgmt-application-inference-profiles.html> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Microsoft Foundry tags each project's usage automatically, in preview [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-C6-S005: Plan and manage costs - Microsoft Foundry <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/concepts/manage-costs> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Whichever routes a firm uses, one adapter per provider is enough [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

*Normalise to an open schema.* FOCUS, the FinOps Foundation's open billing specification, is the natural shape for the firm's own dataset. FOCUS 1.4 was ratified on 4 June 2026; FOCUS 1.5 adds model-identity properties, and a first-class token-type column has been deferred [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V2-S046: FOCUS 1.5 Release Scope: Confirmed and Stretch Features (with Introducing FOCUS 1.4; FOCUS for AI) (FinOps Foundation (FOCUS)) <https://focus.finops.org/focus-1-5-release-scope/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A7-S116: FOCUS 1.5 Release Scope; FOCUS for AI; Introducing FOCUS 1.2; Introducing FOCUS 1.4 (FinOps Foundation (FOCUS)) <https://focus.finops.org/focus-1-5-release-scope/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. That deferral is the caveat in the post: part of the mapping is still the firm's own [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

*Enforce, at three levels.* Per run, as step and token ceilings in the workflow; per key, as gateway budgets and rate limits; and per month, as a financial budget with alerts [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The FinOps Foundation's guidance is to measure for 30 to 60 days, budget at 110% to 120% of that baseline, and alert at 80% and 100%; it adds that "a budget without a quota is a number, not a control" [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S115: FinOps Framework 2026; State of FinOps 2026; FinOps for AI technology category; Tokenomics working group; Who sets the AI budget? (FinOps Foundation) <https://www.finops.org/insights/2026-finops-framework/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The fail-closed point is not academic. In LiteLLM, `max_budget` does not enforce without a database, and the proxy admin key bypasses budget checks [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S015: LiteLLM docs: MCP Overview, Agent Gateway (A2A), Caching, Budgets/Rate Limits, v1.89.0 release notes (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/docs/mcp> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Every gateway has details like these. Test the budget by exceeding it in a test environment and confirming that requests are refused [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The worked example's arithmetic.** The review's illustrative cost model assumes about 40,000 input tokens and 3,000 output tokens per draft commentary [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. At US$2 and US$10 per million tokens, which as of 7 October 2026 was the list price of both Claude Sonnet 5.5 and OpenAI's gpt-6.1-sol, one draft costs about US$0.11 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S011: Pricing - Claude API Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07; A5-S004: OpenAI API Pricing (and Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna pricing) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. With an average of 2.5 drafts per approved commentary and a few small-model judge calls, the token cost is roughly US$0.30 per approved commentary, or about US$12 a month for 40 funds [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The assumptions are the review's own. The conclusion is robust to them: tokens are not the cost driver here, reviewer time is, so the regeneration and edit rate is the number to watch [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Optimisation is a model change.** The levers are real. Batch APIs from OpenAI and Anthropic are 50% off list price, and month-end drafting reviewed the next morning is a batch workload [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S011: Pricing - Claude API Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07; A5-S004: OpenAI API Pricing (and Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna pricing) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Within one range, OpenAI's small Luna tier lists at US$0.10 per million input tokens against US$10 for Astra [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S004: OpenAI API Pricing (and Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna pricing) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. But routing a step to a cheaper model, trimming the context or turning on a cache changes the system's behaviour [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. PRA SS1/23 covers vendor models used to inform business decisions [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S008: SS1/23 Model risk management principles for banks (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2023/may/model-risk-management-principles-for-banks-ss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S037: Bank of England/PRA letter to DSIT and HMT on AI (2024) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/letter/2024/dsit-hmt-letter.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], so a cost-driven change belongs in the same change control and evaluation gate as any other change [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. One lever is ruled out: a semantic cache that returns an answer to a *similar* question can serve last month's narrative, which is unacceptable for regulated outputs [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Budgets are also a resilience control.** A runaway agent can exhaust a shared provider rate limit and starve a client-facing service on the same account [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Separate keys and limits per important business service prevent that [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Spend per provider also feeds the DORA register of information and, from 18 March 2027, the UK's material third-party notifications [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-DORA: Regulation (EU) 2022/2554 Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S021: Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) pages and RoI reporting FAQ (ESMA / EBA / EIOPA) <https://www.esma.europa.eu/esmas-activities/digital-finance-and-innovation/digital-operational-resilience-act-dora> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-FCA-SYSC8: FCA Handbook SYSC 8 (Outsourcing); FG16/5 'Guidance for firms outsourcing to the cloud and other third-party IT services'; PS26/2 operational incident and third-party reporting (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S062: PS7/26 Operational incident and third-party reporting (PRA) and FCA PS26/2; revised SS2/21 (March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA; FCA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2026/march/operational-incident-and-third-party-reporting-policy-statement> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].

**Tools, in their place.** Vantage and CloudZero have added AI-provider integrations, described mainly in their own blog posts [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S113: Vantage blog: Managed AI Tags; Support for Anthropic Costs; Support for Claude Enterprise Costs; OpenAI cost tools <https://www.vantage.sh/blog/managed-ai-tags> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S114: CloudZero blog posts on AI cost (token-based pricing, Bedrock, Anthropic, Azure OpenAI) <https://www.cloudzero.com/blog/token-based-pricing/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. They are useful reporting layers, fed from the firm's dataset, not the record itself [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Helicone, which combined a gateway with cost tracking, was acquired by Mintlify and is in maintenance mode [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S112: Helicone Blog: Helicone acquired by Mintlify (and Mintlify announcement) (Helicone / Mintlify) <https://www.helicone.ai/blog> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S043: Helicone Joins Mintlify (with mintlify.com 'Mintlify acquires Helicone'; PostHog comparison) (Helicone / Mintlify) <https://www.helicone.ai/blog/joining-mintlify> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. This is quiet work, and it rewards the platform and finance colleagues who build the joins, because without them nobody can say what a task costs.

### What good looks like

The signal is **cost per approved output**: all model, judge and tool-call cost for a task, including every failed and regenerated attempt, divided by the number of outputs a human approved [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Report it alongside the **regeneration rate**, the number of drafts per approved output, because that is where the real cost hides [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Measure it by joining gateway records to traces on the run ID, and reconcile the total each month to provider usage data and cloud bills [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The review's target guidance is a figure set per use case, compared with the cost of the manual process it replaces, and falling at constant quality [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Three supporting measures make the number trustworthy: attribution coverage of at least 98% of AI spend; reconciliation to invoices within plus or minus 2% a month; and 100% of production keys with a budget and rate limit tested to fail closed [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. These are a starting point, not a benchmark [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 2: cost.** A run ID on every call, tagged with use case and fund, joined to the trace; a monthly budget set from two month-end cycles; a per-run ceiling on model calls and tokens. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** Cost is reported per approved commentary and per fund, not per token. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** A runaway loop fails the run instead of the budget. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** Token counts and cost per draft, including regenerations. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Cost per approved commentary, with the regeneration rate beside it. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"The token bill is tiny. Why build all this?"** In the worked example it is tiny, and that is the point [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The machinery is not there to save tokens. It is there to show where the cost actually sits, to stop a runaway loop within an hour rather than at the invoice, and to give the risk committee spend by provider for concentration and exit sizing [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A small bill with no enforcement is still an unbounded one. The same data also exposes waste early: the FinOps Foundation lists retry rate and tool calls per task as diagnostics, and both are signs of a workflow going wrong before they are signs of a cost problem [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S115: FinOps Framework 2026; State of FinOps 2026; FinOps for AI technology category; Tokenomics working group; Who sets the AI budget? (FinOps Foundation) <https://www.finops.org/insights/2026-finops-framework/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"We already have a FinOps platform. Let it do this."** Use it, as a reporting layer fed from the firm's own FOCUS-shaped dataset, so that replacing the tool never means losing the history [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Two cautions apply. Allocation rules held only in a proprietary engine are finance policy the firm cannot export [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. And a cost tool that holds a provider admin key with read-write access is a privileged-access risk, so grant the least privilege the provider allows [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S113: Vantage blog: Managed AI Tags; Support for Anthropic Costs; Support for Claude Enterprise Costs; OpenAI cost tools <https://www.vantage.sh/blog/managed-ai-tags> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"A fail-closed budget could stop a critical service at month-end."** It could, if it is sized badly or shared [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The answer is separate keys and limits per service, budgets sized from measured peaks, alerts at 80% so humans act first, and a defined degraded mode [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The alternative, a budget that alerts but never blocks, protects nothing when it matters [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- Can we state the fully loaded cost of one approved output for our largest GenAI use case, including regenerations? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Does every production use case have its own key, or do several share one? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- When did we last test that a gateway budget actually refuses requests once exceeded? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Where is the step or token ceiling that would stop an agent loop within an hour? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Does our last cost-saving change have an evaluation result attached to it? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Who reconciles gateway-metered cost against provider invoices, and what was last month's variance? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

What a task costs is decided in the design, and the invoice only reports the answer [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.


## 3. Self-hosting is a capacity decision, not just cost

*L2 Inference, serving and model access, from Post 3 of the series.*

### The post

Self-hosting a model is a capacity and operating-model decision. It is only partly a cost decision. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The usual case for it is a spreadsheet: GPU hours against per-token prices. What the spreadsheet leaves out is the team that patches inference engines monthly, runs on-call, keeps weights scanned and pinned, and re-qualifies every change of engine version or quantisation, because each of those can change outputs. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S009: PyPI JSON metadata: vllm (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/vllm/json> accessed 2026-10-07; B-L2-S004: Security Advisories - vllm-project/vllm (vLLM project (GitHub)) <https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/security/advisories> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Inference is really three jobs: serving engines that run the model, an optional optimisation layer for firms with their own GPU fleets, and model access through managed services. For regulated workloads the sensible default is managed access in an approved region, through the cloud estate already in place, called only through the firm's gateway. A private open-weight route earns its place where capacity, residency or exit planning justify it. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S090: NVIDIA Dynamo README (GitHub) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ai-dynamo/dynamo/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S089: llm-d README (GitHub) (llm-d project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/llm-d/llm-d/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07] [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Two details catch people out. Where a prompt is processed is a property of the endpoint, not the model: the same model can run in one geography or anywhere, depending on the deployment type. And overflow from reserved capacity can silently cross a residency boundary if the default allows it. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L2-S006: Understanding deployment types in Microsoft Foundry Models <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/foundry-models/concepts/deployment-types> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; B-L2-S008: Data residency (with Deployments and endpoints; Single Zone Provisioned Throughput) - Vertex AI (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/generative-ai/docs/learn/data-residency> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]

The signal is capacity headroom at peak: reserved throughput minus observed demand in the month-end window, when every fund's draft is requested at once. It should be positive, with a margin, before go-live. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to write the peak, the reserved amount, the overflow behaviour and the degraded mode into one capacity plan. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: managed access concentrates dependence on one cloud. The answer is a small portfolio, the primary cloud's service plus one qualified second route, not a retreat into running everything yourself. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The cheapest token is irrelevant if it arrives late, or in the wrong country. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

![Self-hosting is a capacity decision, not just cost](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P03.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 3. Inference is three jobs, with the gateway lifted out into the control plane. Size reserved capacity for the month-end peak and keep overflow in region. The demand curve is illustrative, not real volumes.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

The popular picture of the stack draws inference as one box. The review found three distinct jobs inside it, each with its own risks and its own owner [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Three jobs, not one.** *Serving engines* run the model: vLLM, which is Apache-2.0 and has been hosted by the PyTorch Foundation since May 2025, and SGLang, also Apache-2.0 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S009: PyPI JSON metadata: vllm (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/vllm/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S146: PyTorch Foundation Welcomes vLLM as a Hosted Project (and umbrella-foundation press release, 7 May 2025) (PyTorch Foundation (Linux Foundation)) <https://pytorch.org/blog/pytorch-foundation-welcomes-vllm/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A4-S010: PyPI JSON metadata: sglang (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/sglang/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S147: RadixArk Launches with $100 Million in Seed Funding Led by Accel to Grow SGLang (Business Wire, May 2026) (RadixArk (Business Wire)) <https://www.morningstar.com/news/business-wire/20260505077157/radixark-launches-with-100-million-in-seed-funding-led-by-accel-to-grow-sglang-and-democratize-frontier-ai-infrastructure> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. *Inference optimisation* sits above the engines and coordinates fleets of them: NVIDIA Dynamo calls itself "the orchestration layer above inference engines", and llm-d provides orchestration "above model servers" [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S090: NVIDIA Dynamo README (GitHub) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ai-dynamo/dynamo/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S089: llm-d README (GitHub) (llm-d project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/llm-d/llm-d/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. *Model access* is someone else's served model under a contract: managed inference clouds, routers and the hyperscalers' own model services [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S131: Pricing - Together AI docs (serverless, dedicated endpoints, GPU clusters) <https://docs.together.ai/docs/inference/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A4-S135: Fireworks - Pricing (and Serverless Pricing docs) (Fireworks AI) <https://fireworks.ai/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A4-S139: Pricing - Build with Cerebras Inference (Cerebras Systems) <https://inference-docs.cerebras.ai/support/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A4-S081: About Inference Endpoints (Hugging Face) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/huggingface/hf-endpoints-documentation/main/docs/source/about.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S111: OpenRouter Docs: Guardrails <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OpenRouterTeam/docs/main/guides/features/guardrails.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S075: Hugging Face Inference Providers <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/huggingface/hub-docs/main/docs/inference-providers/index.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A5-S010: Models overview - Claude API Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/models/overview> accessed 2026-10-07]. The review's verdict was that model access is the sub-layer most firms will actually operate; serving engines matter only where a private route is justified, and the optimisation layer is relevant only to firms running their own GPU fleets [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Routing, fallback, budgets and the request log leave this layer altogether and move to the gateway, the subject of Chapter 4 [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What the spreadsheet leaves out.** The case for self-hosting is usually a comparison of GPU hours with token prices. It omits the operating model. vLLM shipped 28 releases in 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S009: PyPI JSON metadata: vllm (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/vllm/json> accessed 2026-10-07]. It also published advisories in which a malicious model repository or crafted input could execute code, including a critical one fixed from version 0.14.1 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L2-S004: Security Advisories - vllm-project/vllm (vLLM project (GitHub)) <https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/security/advisories> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. SGLang's critical CVE-2026-3059 allowed unauthenticated code execution in versions up to 0.5.9 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L2-S009: SGLang multimodal generation module unauthenticated RCE via ZMQ broker (CVE-2026-3059) - GitHub Advisory Database <https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-rgq9-fqf5-fv58> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; B-REVC-S002: NVD CVE-2026-3059 (with OSV and GitHub Advisory Database GHSA-rgq9-fqf5-fv58) (NIST NVD) <https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-3059> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Someone has to patch, scan weights, run on-call for accelerators and forecast peaks [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. And every change of engine version or quantisation level is a change to the numerical system that produced the validated output, so it needs re-qualification [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. None of that is a reason not to self-host. It is the reason to call self-hosting what it is: a capacity and operating-model decision [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Two less obvious duties come with a private route. Running an open-weight model at FP8 or INT4 on vLLM [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S063: vLLM README (GitHub) (vLLM project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/vllm-project/vllm/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07] produces a different numerical system from the provider's reference, so the quantisation level belongs in the model inventory [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. And the caches that make engines fast hold prompt state. Shared prefix caches and tiered key-value stores, such as those in LMCache, Dynamo and llm-d, can carry one request's context into another's memory or storage [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S091: LMCache README (GitHub) (LMCache project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/LMCache/LMCache/dev/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S090: NVIDIA Dynamo README (GitHub) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ai-dynamo/dynamo/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S089: llm-d README (GitHub) (llm-d project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/llm-d/llm-d/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. Partition them by tenant or data class, encrypt the offload tiers, and bring them into retention scope [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Processing location belongs to the endpoint.** This is the detail that catches people out. On Microsoft Foundry, Global deployments may process prompts in any geography, while Data Zone deployments process only within the US or EU zone [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L2-S006: Understanding deployment types in Microsoft Foundry Models <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/foundry-models/concepts/deployment-types> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. On Amazon Bedrock, a geographic profile keeps EU-originated requests within EU regions, and CloudTrail records the region that actually processed each request [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L2-S005: Geographic cross-Region inference - Amazon Bedrock (Amazon Web Services) <https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/geographic-cross-region-inference.html> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. On Google Cloud, regional endpoints keep processing within a jurisdiction, while the global endpoint gives no residency guarantee [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L2-S008: Data residency (with Deployments and endpoints; Single Zone Provisioned Throughput) - Vertex AI (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/generative-ai/docs/learn/data-residency> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Storage residency and processing residency are different promises: OpenAI's UK endpoint, for instance, provides storage only, with no UK processing [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V2-S079: Data controls in the OpenAI platform (with help article 'Data residency for the OpenAI API'; 'Expanding data residency access') <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/your-data> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Contracts and configurations should name processing location, not only storage location [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

There is a UK-specific wrinkle.
