Harvey announced its acquisition of Guardrails AI on 9 September 2026, after the project had retired hosted remote inference [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S028: Guardrails AI Joins Harvey <https://www.harvey.ai/blog/guardrails-ai-joins-harvey> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S026: Guardrails AI Joins Harvey (with Artificial Lawyer 9 Sep 2026, The Next Web, unite.ai) <https://www.harvey.ai/blog/guardrails-ai-joins-harvey> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; V2-S071: Guardrails HUB_UPDATE.md (Guardrails AI (Harvey)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/guardrails-ai/guardrails/main/HUB_UPDATE.md> accessed 2026-10-08]. Security vendors are consolidating the field: Check Point bought Lakera and Palo Alto Networks bought Protect AI [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S012: Check Point to Acquire Lakera, Redefining Security for the AI era (Check Point Software) <https://blog.checkpoint.com/security/check-point-to-acquire-lakera-redefining-security-for-the-ai-era/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S013: Check Point Software Technologies Completes Acquisition of Lakera to Secure the World's AI Transformation (CXOtoday (republished Check Point press release)) <https://cxotoday.com/press-release/check-point-software-technologies-completes-acquisition-of-lakera-to-secure-the-worlds-ai-transformation/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S014: Palo Alto Networks Completes Acquisition of Protect AI <https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/company/press/2025/palo-alto-networks-completes-acquisition-of-protect-ai> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. A two-detector design from different owners is therefore a concentration control as well as a security one [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The newest features target agent behaviour, checking whether a tool call fits the task, and those are in preview or unmaintained [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S055: What's new in Azure AI Content Safety? (Microsoft Learn source) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-ai-docs/main/articles/ai-services/content-safety/whats-new.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S039: LlamaFirewall README (GitHub) (Meta) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/meta-llama/PurpleLlama/main/LlamaFirewall/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S107: Llama Guard 4 model card; Llama Prompt Guard 2 model card; Llama Protections (Meta) <https://developer.meta.com/ai/docs/model-cards-and-prompt-formats/llama-guard-4/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. That supports the order of operations in the post: decide autonomy first, and treat guardrails as the second line, not the reason an agent is allowed to act [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The worked example's inline guard.** Every retrieved chunk is screened for prompt attacks before it enters the prompt, and a flagged chunk is dropped and logged, not sanitised [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A deterministic comparator extracts every figure, sign and direction word ("added", "detracted", "overweight") from the draft and checks each against the attribution engine output for that fund and period. Any mismatch blocks the draft, and no model judge can override it [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. If any guard times out, the draft is held and the analyst is told [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The regulatory reading.** ESMA expects "ex-ante input controls and frequent ex-post output controls" for AI in investment services [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-INTL-AI-ASSETMGMT: IOSCO Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (FR/02/2026) and ESMA Public Statement on AI and investment services (30 May 2024) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S059: ESMA Public Statement on AI and investment services (30 May 2024) and 2026 briefings <https://www.esma.europa.eu/document/public-statement-ai-and-investment-services> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Input guardrails are the ex-ante control; output guardrails plus online evaluation are the ex-post one [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. PRA SS1/23 includes model risk mitigants and ongoing performance monitoring [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S008: SS1/23 Model risk management principles for banks (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2023/may/model-risk-management-principles-for-banks-ss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. A classifier or judge used as a guardrail is itself a model, and its false-positive and false-negative rates deserve monitoring like any other [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Policy is the asset.** Keep the policy (what is forbidden, on which route) separate from the detector (which product detects it), and a vendor change swaps detectors without rewriting policy [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The teams who curate the attack and benign test sets do the work that makes that swap safe, and they deserve the credit when it goes quietly [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is the **false-positive rate**: the share of a labelled benign set, made of real, approved finance text, that the guardrails block [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Measure it by running the benign regression set on every threshold or detector-version change, alongside an attack set mapped to the OWASP LLM and Agentic lists [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A starting target for a drafting route is below 1%, set per route rather than firm-wide [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Three companion measures stop the number being gamed. *Fail-closed conformance*: zero guard failures (timeouts, errors) that let a request through on a regulated route, tested with chaos tests. *Indirect-injection coverage*: 100% of external or third-party retrieved content screened before it enters a prompt. *Numeric grounding*: every figure and direction word matches the authoritative source before release [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The 1% figure is an example target from the review, not a benchmark [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A firm should set its own threshold from its own benign set, and record every threshold change, with approver and reason, as a configuration change [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 6: guardrails.** A deterministic numeric comparator on every draft, injection screening of retrieved text, a PII check on output and denied topics (forecasts, advice). [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** A draft with any figure, sign or direction word that disagrees with the engine is blocked before a human sees it. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** Guardrails back up the design; they never license more autonomy. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** Guard results for every draft. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** False-positive rate of the guards on a labelled set. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"Our cloud's managed guardrail service covers this."** It covers a great deal: content, prompt attacks, PII and grounding [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S072: AWS API model: Amazon Bedrock control plane (botocore service-2.json) - guardrail shapes (Amazon Web Services (via boto/botocore)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/boto/botocore/develop/botocore/data/bedrock/2023-04-20/service-2.json> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S054: What is Azure AI Content Safety? (Microsoft Learn source) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-ai-docs/main/articles/ai-services/content-safety/overview.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. It does not know the firm's business invariants. A polite, harmless, PII-free draft with a reversed currency effect passes every content check [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The deterministic business rules have to be the firm's own code [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The service also processes the same client data as the model, so it belongs in the same residency and third-party analysis [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. And a managed service on the request path is an ICT third-party dependency for the DORA register; if it is down, the route fails closed and the business service degrades [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-DORA: Regulation (EU) 2022/2554 Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S021: Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) pages and RoI reporting FAQ (ESMA / EBA / EIOPA) <https://www.esma.europa.eu/esmas-activities/digital-finance-and-innovation/digital-operational-resilience-act-dora> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Any blocking slows people down; set thresholds loose."** Loose thresholds and over-sensitive thresholds fail the same way: people stop trusting the control [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The answer is a governed override path, in which an analyst requests release of a blocked draft, a second person approves, and a confirmed false positive joins the benign set [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Without that path, users find their own bypass [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Layered guards add latency and cost."** They do, and the costs stack [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Running cheap checks first and stopping early on a block keeps most requests fast [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Give each guard a latency budget, a timeout and a defined outcome on timeout [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Judges cost a model call each, which is why they belong only where nothing simpler can decide [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Emitting each guard decision as a span on the request trace makes the cost and latency visible per route, so the trade-off is argued with data rather than instinct [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- Before we discuss guardrails for this use case, have we decided its autonomy and removed every write tool it does not need? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Do we screen retrieved documents and tool results, or only the user's prompt? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which of our business invariants (figures, signs, forbidden phrases) are checked by deterministic code, and which are left to a classifier? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- What happens when a guard times out, and when did we last test it? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Who owns the guardrail policy and the benign and attack test sets, and are they held in our version control rather than a vendor console? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- If one detector vendor changed hands, could we swap it without rewriting policy? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

Decide what the system may do first; guardrails then catch what slips through, and only if they fail closed and you own their tests. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}


## 7. Easy tool connections make governance the architecture

*L4 Tools, protocols and agent connectivity, from Post 7 of the series.*

### The post

Open tool protocols have made it easy to connect an agent to almost anything. That is exactly why tool governance now matters. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

A badly governed tool layer turns every weakness of a language model into an action. Prompt injection becomes exfiltration when the agent holds a token that can send email. A hallucinated argument becomes a wrong instruction when a write tool is exposed. A tool description becomes an attack vector when the client trusts whatever a third-party server says about itself. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

This is not theoretical. A benchmark published at AAAI 2026 tested tool-poisoning attacks against 45 live tool servers and 20 models. The average attack success rate was 36.5%, and more capable models were often more susceptible. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S023: MCPTox: A Benchmark for Tool Poisoning Attack on Real-World MCP Servers (arXiv (academic preprint; AAAI 2026)) <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2508.14925> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]

The protocols have made room for enterprise controls, but authorisation is still optional in the leading specification. So enforcement has to live outside it: one firm-owned tool gateway with mandatory authorisation, a private allow-listed registry, and every reviewed tool definition pinned by hash. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S055: MCP Specification 2026-07-28: Authorization (Model Context Protocol project (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol/main/docs/specification/2026-07-28/basic/authorization/index.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S015: The 2026-07-28 Specification (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2026-07-28/> accessed 2026-10-07] [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal is tool-definition drift: descriptions or schemas that changed since review. Zero unreviewed changes should reach production. A server that quietly updates its own description is a supply-chain change. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to expose authoritative data through read-only tools owned by the system's team, and to make every write a separate tool, a separate approval and a human confirmation. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: a gateway in front of every tool adds latency and a new critical dependency. It has to be run like one, with the same resilience as anything else on the request path. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Connecting tools is now the easy part. Deciding what each one may do, and on whose behalf, is the architecture. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

![Easy tool connections make governance the architecture](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P07.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 7. No tool is reachable except through a firm-owned governance sub-layer between the agent workflow and its tools; read-only tools by default, every write separate. Generic: no vendor or protocol names.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

This layer is where an agent stops talking and starts acting. It calls tools, reads enterprise systems, browses, runs code and talks to other agents [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Its jobs are tool invocation, enterprise-system connectivity, execution sandboxes and protocol interoperability. The review adds a fifth that the protocols leave open: deciding which agent, acting for which person, may call which tool with which arguments and which credential, and recording that decision [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The evidence that the risk is real.** The MCPTox benchmark, published at AAAI 2026, ran tool-poisoning attacks against 45 live servers and 20 models, with an average attack success rate of 36.5% and a peak of 72.8% [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S023: MCPTox: A Benchmark for Tool Poisoning Attack on Real-World MCP Servers (arXiv (academic preprint; AAAI 2026)) <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2508.14925> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Cloud Security Alliance research notes describe high-severity issues between mid-2025 and June 2026 in several developer tools that launched project-defined tool servers with developer privileges and no isolation, though CSA's attribution and dates are inconsistent across its notes [R]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Reported — A3-S022: MCP Attack Surface: Tool Poisoning and IDE Auto-Execution (CSA research note, Jul 2026) (Cloud Security Alliance Labs) <https://labs.cloudsecurityalliance.org/research/csa-research-note-mcp-tool-poisoning-auto-execution-20260701/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. In May 2026 Composio, a broker that holds end users' OAuth tokens for third-party applications, disclosed unauthorised access to internal systems; by its own account about 0.3% of active connections leaked and 5,241 API keys were flagged as possibly exposed [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L4-S007: Composio May 2026 security incident update <https://composio.dev/blog/composio-may-2026-security-incident> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. A broker that holds every user's tokens is a concentrated breach target and hard to exit [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What changed in 2025–26: the protocols found neutral homes.** The Model Context Protocol (MCP) originated at Anthropic and was donated to the Agentic AI Foundation, under the Linux Foundation, on 9 December 2025 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S018: MCP joins the Agentic AI Foundation (9 Dec 2025) (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2025-12-09-mcp-joins-agentic-ai-foundation/> accessed 2026-10-07; V1-S038: Linux Foundation Announces the Formation of the Agentic AI Foundation (AAIF) (The Linux Foundation) <https://www.linuxfoundation.org/press/linux-foundation-announces-the-formation-of-the-agentic-ai-foundation> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. A2A, the agent-to-agent protocol, reached 1.0.0 on 12 March 2026 and joined the same foundation in August 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S079: A2A CHANGELOG (A2A Project (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/a2aproject/A2A/main/CHANGELOG.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S116: A2A joins AAIF's open agentic stack (17 Aug 2026) (Agentic AI Foundation (AAIF)) <https://aaif.io/blog/a2a-joins-aaif> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A3-S117: A New Chapter for A2A: Joining the Agentic AI Foundation (27 Aug 2026) (A2A Project) <https://a2a-protocol.org/latest/blog/2026/08/27/a-new-chapter-for-a2a-joining-the-agentic-ai-foundation/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. A disclosure is due here: this book was prepared with the help of an Anthropic model, and both of MCP's Lead Maintainers are Anthropic staff [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S082: Expanding the MCP Maintainer Team (8 Apr 2026) (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2026-04-08-maintainer-update/> accessed 2026-10-07]. The review scored MCP on the same rubric as everything else and names an independent alternative wherever it recommends it: OpenAPI-described tools exposed through the same gateway, with A2A for agent-to-agent delegation [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The protocol now makes room for governance, but does not supply it.** MCP's 2026-07-28 revision is stateless, puts method and tool names in HTTP headers so that gateways can authorise them without parsing bodies, and deprecates Dynamic Client Registration [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S015: The 2026-07-28 Specification (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2026-07-28/> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S057: MCP Specification 2026-07-28: Key Changes (Model Context Protocol project (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol/main/docs/specification/2026-07-28/changelog.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07]. The Enterprise-Managed Authorization extension has been stable since June 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S017: Enterprise-Managed Authorization: Zero-touch OAuth for MCP (18 Jun 2026) (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/enterprise-managed-auth/> accessed 2026-10-07]. Yet authorisation itself remains optional in the specification [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S055: MCP Specification 2026-07-28: Authorization (Model Context Protocol project (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol/main/docs/specification/2026-07-28/basic/authorization/index.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07], and tool annotations such as "read-only" or "destructive" are hints that clients must treat as untrusted unless the server is trusted [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S020: Tool Annotations as Risk Vocabulary (16 Mar 2026) (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2026-03-16-tool-annotations/> accessed 2026-10-07]. MCP's own roadmap says the current model is "built around a person approving access in a browser" [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S016: The New MCP Roadmap (22 Aug 2026) (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/mcp-roadmap/> accessed 2026-10-07]. A2A leaves the same gap: Agent Card signing is optional and delegated-authority semantics are undefined [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S078: A2A Protocol Specification (source, latest released version 1.0.0) (A2A Project (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/a2aproject/A2A/main/docs/specification.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. Enforcement therefore has to live somewhere outside the protocol [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The tool-governance sub-layer.** The review's verdict is to draw a sub-layer between the workflow and its tools, so that no tool is reachable except through it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It has five parts [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

- *A tool gateway with mandatory authorisation*, accepting only audience-bound tokens. This is usually the same gateway product that fronts model calls [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *A private, allow-listed registry.* The official MCP Registry is still in preview, its API frozen at v0.1, and it moderates by denylisting [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S019: Introducing the MCP Registry (8 Sep 2025) (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2025-09-08-mcp-registry-preview/> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S042: modelcontextprotocol/registry (GitHub README) (Model Context Protocol project) <https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/registry> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A3-S036: Official MCP Registry API v0.1 sample response (v0.2 and v1 paths returned HTTP 404 on 7 Oct 2026) (Model Context Protocol project) <https://registry.modelcontextprotocol.io/v0.1/servers?limit=1> accessed 2026-10-07]. GitHub Copilot can be pointed at an organisation's own registry, and Microsoft documents a private registry on Azure that is enforced in Copilot and VS Code [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S043: Configure an MCP registry for your organization or enterprise (GitHub Docs) <https://docs.github.com/en/copilot/how-tos/administer-copilot/manage-mcp-usage/configure-mcp-registry> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A3-S044: Locking Down MCP: Create a Private Registry on Azure API Center and Enforce It in GitHub Copilot And VS Code (Microsoft (All things Azure devblog)) <https://devblogs.microsoft.com/all-things-azure/locking-down-mcp-create-a-private-registry-on-azure-api-center-and-enforce-it-in-github-copilot-and-vs-code/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *Hash-pinned definitions.* OWASP's MCP Top 10 recommends signing manifests and hash-pinning reviewed definitions against "rug pulls" [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S045: OWASP MCP Top 10: MCP03:2025 Tool Poisoning <https://owasp.org/www-project-mcp-top-10/2025/MCP03-2025%E2%80%93Tool-Poisoning> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *A policy decision per call.* Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Policy evaluates every agent-to-tool call against Cedar policies before execution and filters denied tools out of the tool list; OPA is the vendor-neutral alternative [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S026: Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is now generally available; AgentCore Policy docs; Why Policy in AgentCore chose Cedar (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/03/policy-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-generally-available/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S046: Open Policy Agent README (GitHub) (Open Policy Agent project (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-policy-agent/opa/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07].
- *An audit event per call*, recording subject, agent, tool, definition hash, arguments or their hash, decision and timestamp [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Read-only by default.** Classify each tool's effect yourself rather than trusting the server's annotations [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S020: Tool Annotations as Risk Vocabulary (16 Mar 2026) (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2026-03-16-tool-annotations/> accessed 2026-10-07] [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. In the worked example, an agent drafting monthly attribution commentary needs exactly four tools: read-only access to the attribution engine's published results, read-only fund reference data, a sandboxed calculator with no network egress and no credentials, and retrieval of approved prior commentary [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It holds no write, publish or email tool; publication happens after human approval, outside the agent [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. There is a model-risk reason too. PRA SS1/23 allows model risk management to apply to complex deterministic methods that are not models [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S008: SS1/23 Model risk management principles for banks (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2023/may/model-risk-management-principles-for-banks-ss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. An agent that recomputes what a validated engine already produces has created an unvalidated shadow model [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Every SaaS tool is a third party.** DORA requires a register covering all ICT third-party arrangements [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-DORA: Regulation (EU) 2022/2554 Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S021: Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) pages and RoI reporting FAQ (ESMA / EBA / EIOPA) <https://www.esma.europa.eu/esmas-activities/digital-finance-and-innovation/digital-operational-resilience-act-dora> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], and search, browser, sandbox and integration services each belong in it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Ownership moves: Nebius closed its acquisition of Tavily on 19 February 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S084: Tavily is Joining Nebius (10 Feb 2026) <https://www.tavily.com/blog/tavily-is-joining-nebius> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S041: Nebius Group N.V. Form 20-F FY2025 (subsequent event: Tavily acquisition) (Nebius Group N.V. (SEC EDGAR)) <https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0001513845/000110465926052948/nbis-20251231x20f.htm> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. No EU processing region was found for Exa or Tavily [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S121: Exa Security Overview (and exa.ai/enterprise, exa.ai/dpa) <https://exa.ai/docs/reference/security> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A3-S088: Tavily pricing, FAQ and help-centre security pages <https://www.tavily.com/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. A search query can disclose confidential intent, such as a planned trade or a client's concern, even with no personal data in it, so the control is "no client context in outbound queries", enforced at an egress proxy [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The credit for this work goes to the platform teams who build the unglamorous enforcement point, and to the system owners who expose their data through tools they are willing to stand behind [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is **tool-definition drift**: tool descriptions or schemas that have changed since review, detected as a hash mismatch against the pinned definition [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Measure it by pinning every reviewed definition by hash in the private registry, alerting on any diff, and blocking the changed tool at the gateway until it is re-reviewed [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The starting target is zero unreviewed changes reaching production [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Three companion measures cover the rest of the sub-layer. *Authorised-call coverage*: 100% of remote tool calls carry an audience-bound token validated at the gateway. *Write-scope exposure*: zero write or destructive tools exposed to a read-only use case, with each exception named and approved. *Time to revoke*: under an hour from the decision to block a server, tool or credential until it is enforced everywhere, proven by drill [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

These are starting points from the review's guidance, not industry benchmarks [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The drill matters more than the number: a firm that has never revoked a tool does not know how long it takes [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 7: tools.** Four tools behind the tool gateway: read-only attribution results with a snapshot hash, read-only fund reference data, a sandboxed calculator for derived figures, and retrieval of approved commentary. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** Every figure in the draft traces to a snapshot ID and hash. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** No write, publish or e-mail tool exists, so none can be misused. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** Every tool call with its arguments hash and decision. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Write tools in a read-only use case: zero. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"The specification is improving. Wait until it makes authorisation mandatory."** The direction is encouraging, and the 2026-07-28 revision tightened issuer validation [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S057: MCP Specification 2026-07-28: Key Changes (Model Context Protocol project (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol/main/docs/specification/2026-07-28/changelog.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07]. But the roadmap still describes agent identity as a priority rather than a feature [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S016: The New MCP Roadmap (22 Aug 2026) (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/mcp-roadmap/> accessed 2026-10-07]. A gateway works whatever the next revision says, and pinning the revision at the gateway turns each protocol change into a tested upgrade rather than a surprise [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"A gateway in front of every tool is a single point of failure."** It is a critical dependency, and the post says so [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The stateless 2026-07-28 revision removes session affinity, so tool servers can sit behind ordinary load balancers [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S015: The 2026-07-28 Specification (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2026-07-28/> accessed 2026-10-07]. Run the gateway with the same resilience, monitoring and break-glass procedure as anything else on the request path, and give each workflow a graceful "tool unavailable" route [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The alternative is worse: without a gateway, every agent host becomes its own policy engine, and the firm has as many security models as it has agent hosts [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. An agent that silently answers from model memory when a data tool fails is worse than one that stops [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"A private registry slows developers down."** It slows the first connection, briefly [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. In return it makes approved tools discoverable and gives every tool a named owner and risk class [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The slower path is retrofitting governance after a community server has quietly changed its own description [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The registry can also carry the review itself: an owner, a risk class, a definition hash and a change process for each tool, so approval becomes a routine step rather than a negotiation each time [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- Can any agent in our estate reach a tool except through a gateway we own? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which remote tool servers accept calls without an audience-bound token today? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Do we pin reviewed tool definitions by hash, and what happens when one changes? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which of our agents can reach a write, publish or email tool, and was each one approved? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Who classifies a tool's risk: the server's own annotations, or a named owner on our side? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Is every search, browser, sandbox and integration service in our third-party register, with its data location known? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- How long would it take us to revoke one tool everywhere, and have we tested it? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

Connecting a tool now takes minutes; governing what it may do, for whom, is the part of the architecture that has to be designed. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}


## 8. "Who did this?" needs an answer for agents

*C4 Identity and access for agents, from Post 8 of the series.*

### The post

"Who did this?" is the first question in every incident review. When the actor is an agent, many logs can answer only with the name of a shared service account. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Agents turn identity mistakes into actions. A person with excessive access usually does nothing with it. An agent with excessive access can be steered into using it by text it reads, which is why the OWASP list for agentic applications names identity and privilege abuse alongside goal hijack and tool misuse. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-C4-S005: OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications (release post and related OWASP pages) (OWASP GenAI Security Project) <https://genai.owasp.org/2025/12/09/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-the-benchmark-for-agentic-security-in-the-age-of-autonomous-ai/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; R-OWASP-AGENTIC: OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications for 2026 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S042: OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications for 2026 (launched 9-10 December 2025) (OWASP GenAI Security Project) <https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

A tool gateway decides what can be called. Identity decides who is allowed through it. Five properties hold up. The agent has its own registered identity with a named sponsor. Where a person started the work, it acts on that person's behalf. Its permissions are the intersection of that person's and the agent's own ceiling. It holds no standing secrets, only short-lived tokens scoped to the task. And every action is attributable to both. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal is attribution completeness: the share of audit events recording user, agent, tool, an argument hash, the policy decision and a trace ID. Aim for 99.9% or better. A gap is a question you cannot answer later. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to carry segregation of duties across unchanged. An agent may draft; it may not approve. The approver is a different, entitled person. And a write tool the agent never calls should still not be reachable. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: no rule yet names an accountable person for an agent. Mapping every agent to a sponsor, and through the business to a senior manager, is a judgement worth making before anyone asks. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-UK-AI-STATEMENTS: FCA, Bank of England and PRA statements and initiatives on AI, 2024-2026 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S055: FCA announces second cohort for AI Live Testing (April 2026); FS25/5; Mills Review (Financial Conduct Authority) <https://www.fca.org.uk/news/press-releases/fca-announces-second-cohort-ai-live-testing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Accountability does not disappear when work is automated. It needs a name, and the system has to record it. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

!["Who did this?" needs an answer for agents](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P08.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 8. One attribution chain: the agent acts for the analyst on a short-lived, read-only token; a different, entitled person approves. Generic worked example, no vendor names.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

This control answers the question every incident review and every regulator will ask: who did this, on whose authority, and with what permission [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}? For people, firms have decades of practice in joiners, movers and leavers, access reviews and segregation of duties. For agents, much of that practice has to be rebuilt, because an agent is neither a person nor a conventional batch job [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why agents change the risk.** A person with excessive access usually does nothing with it. An agent with excessive access may be steered into using it by text it reads [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications for 2026 lists Identity and Privilege Abuse (ASI03), where leaked or excessive credentials let agents operate beyond their intended scope, next to Agent Goal Hijack (ASI01) and Tool Misuse and Exploitation (ASI02) [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-C4-S005: OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications (release post and related OWASP pages) (OWASP GenAI Security Project) <https://genai.owasp.org/2025/12/09/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-the-benchmark-for-agentic-security-in-the-age-of-autonomous-ai/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; R-OWASP-AGENTIC: OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications for 2026 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S042: OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications for 2026 (launched 9-10 December 2025) (OWASP GenAI Security Project) <https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Four failures recur [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}:

- *Shared service accounts*, so logs cannot say which person started an action.
- *Standing credentials*: API keys and refresh tokens in agent configuration or prompts that outlive the task.
- *Token passthrough*, where a tool server forwards the caller's token downstream and becomes a confused deputy. The MCP specification forbids it [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S034: MCP specification 2026-07-28: Authorization security considerations (Model Context Protocol project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol/main/docs/specification/2026-07-28/basic/authorization/security-considerations.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07].
- *Ungoverned agents*, created by developers with no owner, review or revocation path.

**Three identity patterns.** Microsoft's Entra Agent ID documentation draws the distinction clearly: a client-credentials flow for autonomous agents, an on-behalf-of flow for delegation, and a refresh-token flow for long-running user-delegated work, with no interactive sign-in for agent entities [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S060: Authentication protocols for agents (Microsoft Learn source) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/entra-docs/main/docs/agent-id/agent-oauth-protocols.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. Delegated, on-behalf-of access carries the lowest risk, because the agent's effective permission is at most the user's and attribution is native [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Autonomous access makes the agent's own grants the ceiling, so they must be narrow and reviewed [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. An agent given a user account carries the highest risk: it looks like a person in logs and in access reviews [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Intersection, not union.** The right mental model is that effective permission is the intersection of the user's entitlements and the agent's ceiling [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. HashiCorp Vault Enterprise 2.1, generally available from 1 September 2026, enforces exactly that, alongside request-scoped authorisation details, and records both user and agent in its audit logs [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S033: Vault release notes 2.x; IBM Vault Self-Managed 2.x.x lifecycle page (HashiCorp (IBM)) <https://developer.hashicorp.com/vault/docs/updates/release-notes> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S034: HashiCorp Vault agentic IAM is now generally available (HashiCorp (IBM)) <https://www.hashicorp.com/en/blog/hashicorp-vault-agentic-iam-is-now-generally-available> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. In the worked example, the agent cannot read a fund the analyst cannot, and a write tool is not merely unused but absent from its tool list [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What changed in 2025–26: the products became generally available.** Auth0 for AI Agents reached general availability on 19 November 2025 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S097: Auth0 for AI Agents is Now Generally Available (GA); Auth0 May 2026 product innovations (Auth0 (Okta)) <https://auth0.com/blog/auth0-for-ai-agents-generally-available/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Microsoft Entra Agent ID followed in April 2026, with its security features tied to Microsoft Agent 365 licences [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S058: What's new in Microsoft Entra Agent ID (Microsoft Learn source) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/entra-docs/main/docs/agent-id/whats-new-agent-id.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S059: Microsoft Entra Agent ID licensing (include file) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/entra-docs/main/docs/includes/licensing-agent-id.md> accessed 2026-10-07; V2-S032: Microsoft Entra releases and announcements (with What's new in Entra Agent ID; Entra blog 'Build AI agents for production with secure identities from day one';  <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/fundamentals/whats-new> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Okta for AI Agents reached general availability on 30 April 2026 and Okta Agent SSO (Cross App Access) on 24 August 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S100: Okta for AI Agents is Now Generally Available; Okta announces new blueprint for the secure agentic enterprise (Okta, Inc.) <https://www.okta.com/blog/ai/okta-for-ai-agents-general-availability/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S099: Okta brings first-class identity to AI agents with Agent SSO; Okta Introduces Agent SSO for Cross App Access (XAA) (Okta, Inc.) <https://www.okta.com/newsroom/press-releases/okta-brings-first-class-identity-to-ai-agents-with-agent-sso/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S035: Okta brings first-class identity to AI agents with Agent SSO (with Okta support article, OIE release notes, Oktane 2026 release) <https://www.okta.com/newsroom/press-releases/okta-brings-first-class-identity-to-ai-agents-with-agent-sso/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Policy engines matured too. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Policy, built on Cedar, became generally available on 3 March 2026, while OPA and SPIFFE/SPIRE (for workload identity) are graduated CNCF projects [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S026: Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is now generally available; AgentCore Policy docs; Why Policy in AgentCore chose Cedar (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/03/policy-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-generally-available/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S033: Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is now generally available (with AgentCore policy authoring guide and FAQ) (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/03/policy-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-generally-available/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A6-S046: Open Policy Agent README (GitHub) (Open Policy Agent project (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-policy-agent/opa/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S087: SPIRE README (GitHub) (SPIFFE project (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/spiffe/spire/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07].

**The protocol piece.** Okta states that Cross App Access is the MCP Enterprise-Managed Authorization extension [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S099: Okta brings first-class identity to AI agents with Agent SSO; Okta Introduces Agent SSO for Cross App Access (XAA) (Okta, Inc.) <https://www.okta.com/newsroom/press-releases/okta-brings-first-class-identity-to-ai-agents-with-agent-sso/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S035: Okta brings first-class identity to AI agents with Agent SSO (with Okta support article, OIE release notes, Oktane 2026 release) <https://www.okta.com/newsroom/press-releases/okta-brings-first-class-identity-to-ai-agents-with-agent-sso/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. In that pattern the corporate identity provider issues an assertion during single sign-on, which the client exchanges for an access token using the IETF token-exchange standards RFC 8693 and RFC 7523 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S035: MCP extension: Enterprise-Managed Authorization (overview) (Model Context Protocol project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol/main/docs/extensions/auth/enterprise-managed-authorization.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S079: MCP ext-auth: Enterprise-Managed Authorization specification (Stable) (Model Context Protocol project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/modelcontextprotocol/ext-auth/main/specification/stable/enterprise-managed-authorization.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07]. That puts the decision about which tool servers an employee can use in the identity provider [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S035: MCP extension: Enterprise-Managed Authorization (overview) (Model Context Protocol project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol/main/docs/extensions/auth/enterprise-managed-authorization.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07]. MCP originated at Anthropic, and this book was prepared with the help of an Anthropic model, so the disclosure applies [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. MCP authorisation remains optional, and OAuth 2.1 is still an IETF draft [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S033: MCP specification 2026-07-28: Authorization (Model Context Protocol project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol/main/docs/specification/2026-07-28/basic/authorization/index.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07]. The independent route is to apply the same token-exchange standards to OpenAPI-described tools behind the same gateway [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Segregation of duties carries over unchanged.** An agent may draft but not approve; the approver must be a different, entitled person; a policy change needs a second person [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. OpenID CIBA lets an agent request asynchronous approval from a person out of band, and Auth0's AI SDKs implement it [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S078: Auth0 AI for JavaScript README (GitHub) (Auth0 (Okta)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/auth0/auth0-ai-js/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S076: auth0-ai - PyPI JSON metadata (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/auth0-ai/json> accessed 2026-10-07]. In the worked example, the portfolio manager approves through single sign-on with step-up authentication, and the approval records the manager's identity, the draft hash and the time [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The regulatory gap.** The FCA has no AI-specific rules and relies on existing frameworks, including the Senior Managers and Certification Regime and Consumer Duty [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-UK-AI-STATEMENTS: FCA, Bank of England and PRA statements and initiatives on AI, 2024-2026 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S055: FCA announces second cohort for AI Live Testing (April 2026); FS25/5; Mills Review (Financial Conduct Authority) <https://www.fca.org.uk/news/press-releases/fca-announces-second-cohort-ai-live-testing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. No rule names an accountable person for an agent, so mapping each agent to a sponsor and, through the business area, to a Senior Manager is a judgement [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The FPC said in 2026 that agentic AI had not yet been adopted in a way presenting systemic risk, but that risks were likely to increase, potentially rapidly [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-UK-AI-STATEMENTS: FCA, Bank of England and PRA statements and initiatives on AI, 2024-2026 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S056: Financial Policy Committee Record April 2026; FSR July 2026; 2026 AI survey (Bank of England) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/financial-policy-committee-record/2026/april-2026> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Under the EU AI Act, Article 26 requires deployers of high-risk systems to keep logs for at least six months [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S011: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) amending Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (EUR-Lex / Official Journal of the EU) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Logs without user and agent identity cannot show who operated the system [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. In the US, an NCCoE concept paper on software and AI agent identity and authorisation appeared in February 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-NIST-AIRMF: NIST AI Risk Management Framework (AI RMF 1.0, NIST AI 100-1) and Generative AI Profile (NIST AI 600-1) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S044: NIST AI 600-1 Generative AI Profile; NCCoE agent identity concept paper; CAISI agent security <https://www.nist.gov/publications/artificial-intelligence-risk-management-framework-generative-artificial-intelligence> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].

**The root of trust moves.** The workforce identity provider is already a critical dependency. Adding agents makes it the root of trust for automated actions too [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. An agent's permissions are part of its risk tier: the same model with write access is a different risk from one with read-only access [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Identity teams rarely get credit for incidents that never happen; this is a good place to give it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is **attribution completeness**: the share of audit events that record user, agent, tool, an argument hash, the policy decision and a trace ID [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Measure it with a scheduled completeness check on the audit store, and reconcile policy-decision logs against tool calls so that no call escapes evaluation [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A starting target is 99.9% or better, and every missing field is investigated as a defect [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Four companion measures make the chain credible. *Delegated-call share*: 100% of tool calls in user-initiated work carry a user-bound, on-behalf-of token. *Standing credentials*: zero long-lived secrets held by agents, with any exception time-limited and owned. *Time to revoke*: under 15 minutes from a decision to revoke an agent to its last successful call, proven by drill. *Approval integrity*: every approval made by someone other than the requester, and entitled to approve [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

These targets come from the review's guidance. They are a starting point, not an industry benchmark [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 8: identity.** A registered agent identity with a named sponsor; it acts on behalf of the analyst with a read-only, minutes-long token; a different portfolio manager approves with step-up authentication. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** Every action answers 'who did this, on whose behalf, under which policy'. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** The agent holds no standing credentials and can never approve. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** Audit events linking user, agent, tool, decision and trace ID. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Attribution completeness of audit events. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"Our identity provider is already critical. Agents add load, cost and risk to it."** True, and the costs are real: Entra's agent security features need Agent 365, Okta for AI Agents is a separate subscription, and AgentCore Policy charges per authorisation request [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S059: Microsoft Entra Agent ID licensing (include file) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/entra-docs/main/docs/includes/licensing-agent-id.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S099: Okta brings first-class identity to AI agents with Agent SSO; Okta Introduces Agent SSO for Cross App Access (XAA) (Okta, Inc.) <https://www.okta.com/newsroom/press-releases/okta-brings-first-class-identity-to-ai-agents-with-agent-sso/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S021: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Pricing; AgentCore GA announcement (Oct 2025); AgentCore Gateway docs (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/pricing/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Budget for it explicitly [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. On load, cache decisions briefly, run the policy decision point as a sidecar where possible, and reuse short-lived tokens within their lifetime rather than lengthening it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The alternative, shared accounts, is cheaper only until the first incident review [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Scheduled agents have no person to act on behalf of."** Then they use the autonomous pattern, with their own narrow ceiling, a named sponsor and a review cycle [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Their runtimes should authenticate with workload identity, such as SPIFFE/SPIRE or the cloud equivalent, rather than stored secrets [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S087: SPIRE README (GitHub) (SPIFFE project (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/spiffe/spire/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07] [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The point is not that every call carries a person; it is that every call carries a name somebody answers for [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Why 99.9%? Surely attribution must be 100%."** For regulated releases, approval integrity should be 100% [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Attribution completeness is measured across all events, including telemetry gaps outside the agent's control, so 99.9% is a realistic floor to start from [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Each gap is still traced to a cause and closed, and a firm should tighten the target as its pipeline matures [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. What matters is that the number is measured at all. A firm that cannot state its attribution completeness is, in effect, unable to say how many of yesterday's agent actions it could explain [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- For each agent in production, who is its named owner and sponsor, and when was it last certified? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which agents still run under a shared service account or a developer's personal token? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- When an analyst starts a run, does every tool call carry that analyst's identity as well as the agent's? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Is an agent's effective permission the intersection of the user's entitlements and its own ceiling, or the union? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Can any agent reach a write tool it never calls, and why? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- How long does it take to revoke an agent everywhere, and when did we last drill it? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which senior manager would answer for each agent if a regulator asked tomorrow? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

Give every agent a name, act on the requester's behalf with no more than the requester could do, and record both, because accountability does not automate itself. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}


## 9. Build memory last, on purpose

*L5 Memory, from Post 9 of the series.*

### The post

Memory is the layer I would deliberately build last. What an agent remembers is a governance question before it is a technical one. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Memory is the one part of the stack that writes its own inputs. A mistaken "fact" extracted from one conversation can be recalled in hundreds of later ones. A stale preference can override a newer instruction. An injected instruction that lands in memory keeps working long after the original input has gone, which is why the OWASP list for agentic applications now names memory and context poisoning as a risk of its own. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L5-S001: Memory Is a Feature. It Is Also an Attack Surface (OWASP GenAI Security Project) <https://genai.owasp.org/2026/05/13/memory-is-a-feature-it-is-also-an-attack-surface/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

It also creates a regulatory object that did not exist before: a growing store of extracted personal and business information with no natural expiry. Several memory products keep long-term records indefinitely unless the firm sets a limit. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S111: Designing lifecycle policies for AgentCore memory (and AgentCore Memory docs: harness-memory, delete-memory, UpdateMemory, memory-record-streaming) (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/designing-lifecycle-policies-for-agentcore-memory/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A3-S109: Vertex AI Agent Engine Memory Bank overview / generate memories / Memory Bank REST reference (docs.cloud.google.com, now 'Agent Platform Memory Bank') (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/generative-ai/docs/agent-engine/memory-bank/overview> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

So the design starts with a question: does this use case need long-term memory at all? Often session state plus retrieval of approved knowledge is enough. In the worked example, what looks like memory, such as fund terminology and the portfolio manager's preferred phrasing, is better held as a versioned style file, with recurring edits proposed as changes and approved by a person. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal is erasure completion time: from request to deletion confirmed across the store, its indexes, revisions and backups. Within the one-month statutory window, with an internal target well inside it. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L5-S005: Right to erasure (Information Commissioner's Office) <https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/individual-rights/individual-rights/right-to-erasure/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to treat memory as a governed record class with an approved write path. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: the independent memory products are moving fast and in different directions, while the platforms absorb the feature. Owning the memory interface in front of them keeps that churn survivable. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S081: Mem0 docs source: Open Source migration to the new memory algorithm (Mem0 (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mem0ai/mem0/main/docs/migration/oss-v2-to-v3.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S059: getzep/zep README: Zep Cloud Examples & Integrations (Zep AI (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/getzep/zep/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S093: Letta's Next Phase (Mar 2026) <https://www.letta.com/blog/our-next-phase/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A3-S006: PyPI JSON metadata: langmem (Python Package Index (PyPI)) <https://pypi.org/pypi/langmem/json> accessed 2026-10-07] [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Forgetting is a feature. In a regulated firm, it has to be engineered. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

![Build memory last, on purpose](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P09.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 9. Memory comes after everything it depends on, is written only through a policy gate, and is erased from every copy. Generic: no vendor names.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

This layer decides what an agent carries from one interaction to the next, where that state is stored, and who is allowed to change, inspect or erase it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The cognitive labels (conversation, working, episodic, semantic, procedural) are less useful to an architect than one plain question: who writes the record, and how authoritative is it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}? A knowledge base is written by an accountable owner through a controlled ingestion process. Memory is written by the agent, or by an extraction model, as a side effect of conversations. That makes it the least authoritative and least reviewed data the agent reads, even though it reaches the prompt in the same way as approved knowledge [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**How it fails.** A badly designed memory layer fails slowly and invisibly [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A mistaken fact extracted once is recalled many times; a stale preference overrides a newer instruction; a client's name surfaces in another user's session because the scope key was too broad [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications for 2026 names memory and context poisoning (ASI06): attacker-controlled content that the system continues to trust over time, shaping later planning, tool use and behaviour [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L5-S001: Memory Is a Feature. It Is Also an Attack Surface (OWASP GenAI Security Project) <https://genai.owasp.org/2026/05/13/memory-is-a-feature-it-is-also-an-attack-surface/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Memory also breaks reproducibility. A system whose output depends on what it has accumulated cannot be re-run to the same result unless its memory state at the time was captured [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Defaults that run against storage limitation.** Each product's design is defensible for recall quality, and each makes retention an engineering task rather than a default [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Memory's long-term records have no built-in time limit, and AWS recommends a pruner using timestamp filters [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S111: Designing lifecycle policies for AgentCore memory (and AgentCore Memory docs: harness-memory, delete-memory, UpdateMemory, memory-record-streaming) (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/designing-lifecycle-policies-for-agentcore-memory/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Google's Memory Bank has an optional time-to-live that is off by default, and keeps memory revisions for 365 days by default [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S109: Vertex AI Agent Engine Memory Bank overview / generate memories / Memory Bank REST reference (docs.cloud.google.com, now 'Agent Platform Memory Bank') (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/generative-ai/docs/agent-engine/memory-bank/overview> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Mem0's open-source extraction is now add-only, so memories accumulate rather than being overwritten [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S081: Mem0 docs source: Open Source migration to the new memory algorithm (Mem0 (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mem0ai/mem0/main/docs/migration/oss-v2-to-v3.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07]. Graphiti invalidates old facts rather than deleting them, keeping history [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S003: PyPI JSON metadata: graphiti-core (Python Package Index (PyPI)) <https://pypi.org/pypi/graphiti-core/json> accessed 2026-10-07]. Vendor-hosted chat memory behaves differently again: deleting a ChatGPT chat does not delete its saved memories, and deleted memory logs may be kept for up to 30 days [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S110: OpenAI API: Conversation state (and Help Center: Memory FAQ) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/conversation-state> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].

**The regulatory object.** Under UK GDPR the ICO expects erasure from live systems, steps for backups (which may be put "beyond use" until overwritten) and a response within one month; the ICO notes that this guidance is under review following the Data (Use and Access) Act [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L5-S005: Right to erasure (Information Commissioner's Office) <https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/individual-rights/individual-rights/right-to-erasure/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Storage limitation expects personal data to be kept no longer than necessary, with periodic review and deletion or anonymisation [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L5-S006: Principle (e): Storage limitation (Information Commissioner's Office) <https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/data-protection-principles/a-guide-to-the-data-protection-principles/storage-limitation/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. A memory store is personal-data processing by default, because extraction picks up names, preferences and circumstances from conversation [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Some content may also be a business record the firm must keep. Whether a records obligation overrides an erasure request is a legal question per record class; the architectural answer is to keep memory and records apart [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"} [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What changed in 2025–26.** The independent products moved apart. Mem0 removed every external graph store from its open-source build on 14 April 2026; Zep deprecated its Community Edition, leaving Graphiti as the open-source path; Letta pivoted to an agent harness; and LangMem has had no release since 27 October 2025 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S053: Mem0 docs: Platform vs Open Source (and OSS v2-to-v3 migration notes) <https://docs.mem0.ai/platform/platform-vs-oss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S043: Open Source: Migrating to the New Memory Algorithm; SDK changelog; Highlights (Mem0) <https://docs.mem0.ai/migration/oss-v2-to-v3> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A3-S059: getzep/zep README: Zep Cloud Examples & Integrations (Zep AI (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/getzep/zep/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S093: Letta's Next Phase (Mar 2026) <https://www.letta.com/blog/our-next-phase/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A3-S006: PyPI JSON metadata: langmem (Python Package Index (PyPI)) <https://pypi.org/pypi/langmem/json> accessed 2026-10-07]. Meanwhile the platforms absorbed the feature. AgentCore Memory has been generally available since 13 October 2025 and Memory Bank since December 2025 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S047: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is now generally available (Oct 2025) (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/10/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-available/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S087: Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is now generally available; AgentCore FAQs; release notes (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/10/amazon-bedrock-agentcore-available/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; V1-S088: Vertex AI release notes (Agent Engine Sessions and Memory Bank GA; pricing) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/docs/release-notes> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Model vendors ship memory in their own APIs too: Anthropic's memory tool executes file operations on storage the developer controls, and OpenAI persists conversation state through its Conversations API [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S069: Memory tool - Claude Platform Docs (Anthropic) <https://docs.claude.com/en/docs/agents-and-tools/tool-use/memory-tool> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S110: OpenAI API: Conversation state (and Help Center: Memory FAQ) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/conversation-state> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Every product stores memory in ordinary vector, graph, relational or file stores [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S053: Mem0 docs: Platform vs Open Source (and OSS v2-to-v3 migration notes) <https://docs.mem0.ai/platform/platform-vs-oss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A3-S003: PyPI JSON metadata: graphiti-core (Python Package Index (PyPI)) <https://pypi.org/pypi/graphiti-core/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S005: PyPI JSON metadata: cognee (Python Package Index (PyPI)) <https://pypi.org/pypi/cognee/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S006: PyPI JSON metadata: langmem (Python Package Index (PyPI)) <https://pypi.org/pypi/langmem/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S073: letta-ai/letta-code README (Letta (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/letta-ai/letta-code/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. The review's synthesis therefore merges memory physically into the retrieval and data stores, while keeping a thin "memory service" visible as a logical component, because its controls are unique and auditors will look for them [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why last.** The review's build order puts memory in phase 6 of 0 to 7: after governance, evaluation and observability, model access through the gateway, retrieval, agent workflows and tools [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Each step earns its place [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Memory changes behaviour over time, so its effect can only be measured once evaluation and tracing exist. Extraction is a model call, so it should route through the gateway. Memory is stored and recalled with retrieval infrastructure, so that should be settled first. The safe pattern is a workflow that decides when to remember. And authoritative data must already flow through read-only tools, so that memory never becomes the easiest place to find a number [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The governed write path.** The design that holds up puts a policy gate between extraction and storage: a data-loss-prevention screen, an allow-list of what may be remembered ("terminology preferences: yes; client facts: no; figures: never") and, for shared memory, human approval [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Every record carries provenance, a scope and a retention class [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The key design choice is the subject index: every memory that may contain personal data must be findable by data subject, so that one query can erase it across rows, vectors, graph nodes, revisions and caches [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Put a thin, firm-owned memory interface (write, recall, forget-by-subject, snapshot) in front of any product, and keep the canonical record in a store the firm controls [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The worked example.** For an agent drafting monthly attribution commentary, the "memory" asked for is fund terminology and the portfolio manager's past edits [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That is a governed, versioned style file, not free-form agent memory. A monthly job clusters recurring edits and proposes new style rules as a change request; the manager approves, rejects or amends them; each run reads one pinned version, and its hash goes into the evidence pack [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The slice needs no memory product at all [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Credit for that kind of restraint usually goes unrecorded, which is a pity: it saves a team a year of clean-up [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is **erasure completion time**: from an erasure request being received to deletion confirmed across the memory store, its indexes, revisions and backups, with backups put "beyond use" [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Measure it from erasure job logs, with the evidence filed alongside other governance records, and test the whole path end to end before go-live, including revisions and indexes [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The outer bound is the one-month UK GDPR response window [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L5-S005: Right to erasure (Information Commissioner's Office) <https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/individual-rights/individual-rights/right-to-erasure/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The internal target should sit well inside it, set by the firm from its own drills [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A simple drill uses a synthetic data subject: seed memories about them, request erasure, and confirm that nothing about them can be recalled or restored [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Three companion measures keep memory honest. *Retention conformance*: zero records older than their retention class. *Provenance coverage*: 100% of memories carry source interaction, writer, timestamp, approval status and version. *Harmful-recall rate*: zero runs where recalled memory contradicted an authoritative source on a number [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

These targets come from the review's guidance. They are a starting point, not an industry benchmark [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 9: memory.** Deliberately little: a versioned glossary and style rules per fund, changed only by an approved pull request, recalled by version hash. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** Any commentary can be regenerated with exactly the memory it used. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** The agent may propose memory, never write it; no client identifiers in memory. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** The memory version hash in every trace. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Time to complete an erasure request across every copy. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"Users expect assistants to remember them. Building memory last makes us look behind."** Per-user preference memory can be committed automatically, provided it passes the write gate and is visible to and editable by the user [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Mainstream assistants already work that way. Anthropic's Claude app offers a user-editable memory summary and an admin switch to disable memory on its business plans, and ChatGPT's memory can also be switched off [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S071: Bringing memory to Claude (11 Sep 2025; updated 23 Oct 2025) (Anthropic) <https://www.anthropic.com/news/memory> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S110: OpenAI API: Conversation state (and Help Center: Memory FAQ) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/conversation-state> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. What should wait is memory that feeds a regulated output [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"The hyperscaler memory services are generally available. Why not just use one?"** Inside that hyperscaler's own agent runtime, the review rates this acceptable [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. But defaults still need retention settings, and Memory Bank memories are deleted with the Agent Runtime instance, so an export to the firm's own store is part of the design [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S109: Vertex AI Agent Engine Memory Bank overview / generate memories / Memory Bank REST reference (docs.cloud.google.com, now 'Agent Platform Memory Bank') (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/generative-ai/docs/agent-engine/memory-bank/overview> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Check the security settings as well: AgentCore Memory encrypts with an AWS-owned key unless a customer-managed key is set at creation, and Memory Bank lists customer-managed keys but not on its global endpoint [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L5-S002: Encrypt your Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Memory (and AWS Config / Security Hub / SRA pages) (Amazon Web Services) <https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/storage-encryption.html> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; B-L5-S004: Scale your agents / Set up Memory Bank / release notes / data residency terms (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/scale> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Wrapping the service in a firm-owned interface keeps memory movable if the agent moves [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Erasure and record-keeping pull in opposite directions."** Sometimes they do [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Article 17(3) GDPR sets exceptions to erasure, including legal claims [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L5-S007: Judgment C-26/22 (and C-484/24) on GDPR Articles 5(1)(e) and 17 (Court of Justice of the EU (EUR-Lex)) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/HTML/?uri=CELEX%3A62022CJ0026> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The cleaner answer is architectural: business records live in the records archive under the records policy, and memory holds only what the agent needs, under shorter retention [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Then an erasure request touches memory without touching the firm's obligations to keep records [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- For each agent use case, does it need long-term memory, or would session state plus approved retrieval do? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- What may each agent remember, and where is that allow-list written down and enforced? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Could we find every memory about one person with a single query, including revisions, indexes and backups? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which memory products or features in our estate have no retention limit set? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Does any workflow read a figure from memory rather than from the system of record? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Can we reproduce a past output with the memory state it actually used? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line
