Memory is the one layer that writes its own inputs, so build it last, gate every write, and engineer the forgetting before you switch it on. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}


## 10. Multi-vendor AI is becoming close to a requirement

*Regulated reality: the EU AI Act, DORA and the UK third-party regime (with C8 Model risk, governance and auditability), from Post 10 of the series.*

### The post

Concentration risk is turning multi-vendor AI from a preference into something close to a requirement.

Four facts frame it. In the US, SR 26-2 replaced the long-standing model-risk guidance in April 2026 and placed generative and agentic AI outside its scope, so firms write their own standard. In the EU, enforcement of the AI Act's duties on general-purpose model providers began in August 2026, while the high-risk duties for Annex III uses moved to 2 December 2027. For most asset-management uses, today's live duties are literacy and transparency. Under DORA, and the UK's new critical third parties regime, the providers designated for direct oversight are hyperscalers and infrastructure firms; no model vendor is on either list. And from 18 March 2027, UK firms must notify material third-party arrangements before entering or significantly changing them. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S002: OCC Bulletin 2026-13 Model Risk Management: Revised Guidance (attachment bulletin-2026-13a.pdf) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/bulletins/2026/bulletin-2026-13.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-EU-OMNIBUS-AI: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 ('Digital Omnibus on AI') amending Regulations (EU) 2024/1689, 2018/1139 and 2023/1230 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S011: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) amending Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (EUR-Lex / Official Journal of the EU) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; R-DORA: Regulation (EU) 2022/2554 Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S021: Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) pages and RoI reporting FAQ (ESMA / EBA / EIOPA) <https://www.esma.europa.eu/esmas-activities/digital-finance-and-innovation/digital-operational-resilience-act-dora> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S051: European Supervisory Authorities designate critical ICT third-party providers under DORA (EBA/EIOPA/ESMA press release; with Morgan Lewis, TLT) (EBA / EIOPA / ESMA) <https://www.eba.europa.eu/publications-and-media/press-releases/european-supervisory-authorities-designate-critical-ict-third-party-providers-under-digital> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; R-UK-CTP: UK Critical Third Parties regime (FSMA 2023 / FSMA 2000 s312L; PRA/FCA/BoE CTP rules) and the Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S023: The Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777); BoE/FCA/HMT announcements (HM Treasury / legislation.gov.uk / Bank of England) <https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2026/777/made> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S052: The Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777) (with Lewis Silkin, National Law Review, Library of Congress) (UK Government (legislation.gov.uk)) <https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2026/777/made> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-FCA-SYSC8: FCA Handbook SYSC 8 (Outsourcing); FG16/5 'Guidance for firms outsourcing to the cloud and other third-party IT services'; PS26/2 operational incident and third-party reporting (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S062: PS7/26 Operational incident and third-party reporting (PRA) and FCA PS26/2; revised SS2/21 (March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA; FCA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2026/march/operational-incident-and-third-party-reporting-policy-statement> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Read together, oversight of a direct model-vendor contract rests largely on the firm. No rule says "use two model vendors". But a tested stressed-exit plan for a model behind an important business service needs a second route that already works: qualified on the same evaluation suite, through the same gateway, in an approved region. International supervisors now name concentration on a few AI providers as a risk in itself. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S048: SS2/21 Outsourcing and third party risk management (and PS7/26, March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/supervisory-statement/2021/ss221-march-21.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; R-INTL-AI-ASSETMGMT: IOSCO Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (FR/02/2026) and ESMA Public Statement on AI and investment services (30 May 2024) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S058: IOSCO FR/02/2026 Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (May 2026) <https://www.iosco.org/library/pubdocs/pdf/IOSCOPD823.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal is a concentration ratio: the share of production usage, or of important services, served by the largest single model vendor, reported to the risk committee against a ceiling the firm sets. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to use these deadlines as a mandate, building the evidence once.

The honest caveat: the DORA list is updated every year, and nothing guarantees a direct model API stays outside the perimeter.

Regulation rarely tells you what to build. It tells you what you will have to prove.

![Multi-vendor AI is becoming close to a requirement](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P10.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 10. Five dates that frame GenAI in regulated asset management. No model vendor is designated for direct oversight, so that oversight rests on the firm. Not legal advice.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

This chapter describes the regulatory frame as the review found it at the end of Q3 2026. It is an architect's reading, not legal advice, and any firm should take its own legal view before acting on it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The US anchor moved, and left a gap.** SR 26-2, OCC Bulletin 2026-13 and FDIC FIL-15-2026 were issued on 17 April 2026 and replace SR 11-7 and SR 21-8 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S002: OCC Bulletin 2026-13 Model Risk Management: Revised Guidance (attachment bulletin-2026-13a.pdf) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/bulletins/2026/bulletin-2026-13.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S005: Federal Banking Agencies Issue Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (Sullivan & Cromwell LLP (also Orrick, Davis Polk memos)) <https://www.sullcrom.com/insights/memo/2026/April/OCC-Fed-FDIC-Issue-Revised-Guidance-Model-Risk-Management> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S049: Supervisory Letter SR 26-2 on Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management -- April 17, 2026 (with SR2602a1 attachment, OCC Bulletin 2026-13a) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. They keep risk-based, materiality-driven model risk management with effective challenge by independent reviewers. They also expressly place generative and agentic AI models out of scope, leaving the firm's own practices to determine controls [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S002: OCC Bulletin 2026-13 Model Risk Management: Revised Guidance (attachment bulletin-2026-13a.pdf) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/bulletins/2026/bulletin-2026-13.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The agencies said they would issue a request for information on AI; none had been published by 7 October 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-US-AGENCY-AI: US federal banking agency and NIST statements on generative and agentic AI, 2026 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S003: OCC Issues Updated Model Risk Management Guidance (NR 2026-29) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/news-releases/2026/nr-occ-2026-29.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S007: After SR 26-2: Governing Generative and Agentic AI in Banks (and related practitioner summaries) (Lumenova AI / practitioner commentary) <https://www.lumenova.ai/blog/sr-26-2-control-framework/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The carve-out does not remove the governance need. It moves the burden to the firm, which now has to write its own standard. Writing it to the quality of PRA SS1/23 is the safest course, because the planned request for information may bring expectations back [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The EU timetable split in two.** The Commission's enforcement powers over general-purpose AI providers, with fines of up to 3% of global turnover, began on 2 August 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S011: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) amending Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (EUR-Lex / Official Journal of the EU) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S019: General-Purpose AI Models in the AI Act - Questions & Answers (European Commission) <https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/faqs/general-purpose-ai-models-ai-act-questions-answers> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Regulation (EU) 2026/1744, in force from 27 July 2026, moved the Annex III high-risk duties to 2 December 2027 and Annex I to 2 August 2028, leaving the general-purpose, prohibition and transparency timelines unchanged [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-EU-OMNIBUS-AI: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 ('Digital Omnibus on AI') amending Regulations (EU) 2024/1689, 2018/1139 and 2023/1230 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S011: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) amending Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (EUR-Lex / Official Journal of the EU) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S050: Digital Omnibus on AI in the Official Journal: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 is published (with CSA research note, ActuIA, Licentium) (Law and Technology (secondary)) <https://lawandtechnology.eu/en/digital-omnibus-on-ai-official-journal-regulation-2026-1744/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. An asset manager using third-party models is normally a deployer, and attribution commentary, research summaries and operations are not Annex III uses [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The live duties are therefore AI literacy, which Article 4 now frames as taking "measures to support" it [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S060: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 amended Article 4 text; Commission AI Literacy Q&A (EUR-Lex / European Commission) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/HTML/?uri=OJ%3AL_202601744> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], and Article 50 transparency, which has applied since 2 August 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S018: AI Act Article 50 Transparency obligations; Commission Article 50 guidelines C(2026) 5054 (European Commission) <https://ai-act-service-desk.ec.europa.eu/en/ai-act/article-50> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S078: Not Delayed, Not Deferred: EU AI Act Transparency Obligations Are Now in Force (Goodwin; with Orrick, Freshfields on final Digital Omnibus) (Goodwin Procter) <https://www.goodwinlaw.com/en/insights/publications/2026/08/alerts-technology-dpc-eu-ai-act-transparency-obligations-now-in-force> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Two traps sit nearby. HR uses, creditworthiness and life and health insurance pricing are Annex III points [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S017: AI Act Annex III; Article 27 FRIA; Article 72 post-market monitoring (European Commission AI Act Service Desk) <https://ai-act-service-desk.ec.europa.eu/en/ai-act/annex-3> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. And under Article 25 a deployer becomes a provider if it changes a system's intended purpose so that it becomes high-risk [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S016: AI Act Article 26 Obligations of deployers; Article 25 Responsibilities along the AI value chain (European Commission AI Act Service Desk) <https://ai-act-service-desk.ec.europa.eu/en/ai-act/article-26> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. A system prompt is the easiest place to make that change by accident (Chapter 14) [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The third-party perimeter stops at the cloud.** DORA's first list of critical ICT third-party providers, published on 18 November 2025, named 19 providers, including AWS, Google Cloud, Microsoft, Oracle, IBM, SAP, Bloomberg and LSEG, and no AI model provider; the list is updated every year [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-DORA: Regulation (EU) 2022/2554 Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S020: The ESAs designate critical ICT third-party providers under DORA (with List_of_designated_CTPPs.pdf, 18 Nov 2025) (EBA / ESMA / EIOPA (Joint ESAs)) <https://www.eba.europa.eu/publications-and-media/press-releases/european-supervisory-authorities-designate-critical-ict-third-party-providers-under-digital> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S021: Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) pages and RoI reporting FAQ (ESMA / EBA / EIOPA) <https://www.esma.europa.eu/esmas-activities/digital-finance-and-innovation/digital-operational-resilience-act-dora> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S051: European Supervisory Authorities designate critical ICT third-party providers under DORA (EBA/EIOPA/ESMA press release; with Morgan Lewis, TLT) (EBA / EIOPA / ESMA) <https://www.eba.europa.eu/publications-and-media/press-releases/european-supervisory-authorities-designate-critical-ict-third-party-providers-under-digital> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The UK designated AWS, Google Cloud, Microsoft and Oracle as critical third parties with effect from 13 July 2026, again with no model vendor, and firms keep their own due-diligence and contingency duties [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-UK-CTP: UK Critical Third Parties regime (FSMA 2023 / FSMA 2000 s312L; PRA/FCA/BoE CTP rules) and the Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S023: The Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777); BoE/FCA/HMT announcements (HM Treasury / legislation.gov.uk / Bank of England) <https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2026/777/made> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S024: HM Treasury makes first designations under UK CTP regime (with Lewis Silkin, S&C) (Herbert Smith Freehills Kramer) <https://www.hsfkramer.com/notes/fsrandcorpcrime/2026-posts/hm-treasury-makes-first-designations-under-uk-ctp-regime> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S052: The Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777) (with Lewis Silkin, National Law Review, Library of Congress) (UK Government (legislation.gov.uk)) <https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2026/777/made> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The mechanism matters more than the lists. A model consumed through a designated hyperscaler sits inside a relationship that is already overseen and already contracted. A direct contract with a model vendor does not, so more of the oversight falls on the firm [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That is the strongest regulatory argument for the hyperscaler's model service as the default route [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The UK adds a clock.** From 18 March 2027, PRA PS7/26 and FCA PS26/2 require firms to notify before entering into or significantly changing a material third-party arrangement, and to submit an annual register [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-FCA-SYSC8: FCA Handbook SYSC 8 (Outsourcing); FG16/5 'Guidance for firms outsourcing to the cloud and other third-party IT services'; PS26/2 operational incident and third-party reporting (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S062: PS7/26 Operational incident and third-party reporting (PRA) and FCA PS26/2; revised SS2/21 (March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA; FCA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2026/march/operational-incident-and-third-party-reporting-policy-statement> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S053: PS7/26 - Operational resilience: Operational incident and third-party reporting (with SS2/21 March 2026 update; FCA PS26/2 and FG26/4) (Bank of England (PRA) / FCA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2026/march/operational-incident-and-third-party-reporting-policy-statement> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. SS2/21 already expects documented and tested exit plans, including stressed exit [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S048: SS2/21 Outsourcing and third party risk management (and PS7/26, March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/supervisory-statement/2021/ss221-march-21.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. A hosted model behind an important business service is likely to be a material arrangement, so adding a second vendor in a hurry will need notification lead time [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Concentration is now named, and it is not hypothetical.** IOSCO's Supervisory Toolkit (FR/02/2026) flags concentration risk from reliance on a few AI technology providers [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-INTL-AI-ASSETMGMT: IOSCO Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (FR/02/2026) and ESMA Public Statement on AI and investment services (30 May 2024) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S058: IOSCO FR/02/2026 Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (May 2026) <https://www.iosco.org/library/pubdocs/pdf/IOSCOPD823.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S077: IOSCO public reports list: FR/02/2026 Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (with A&O Shearman FinReg, Regulation Tomorrow May 2026) <https://www.iosco.org/publications/_public_reports.cfm> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The review's evidence shows why. Anthropic's top tier, Claude Fable 5, was made unavailable on 12 June 2026 and restored from 1 July 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V2-S004: Claude Fable - Anthropic <https://www.anthropic.com/claude/fable> accessed 2026-10-08]. Google's recent Gemini Flash versions live about five to six months, Mistral's Labs models may go with a month's notice, and OpenAI gives as little as about two weeks for previews [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L1-S003: Gemini 3.7 Flash model page and related lifecycle pages (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/models/gemini/3-7-flash> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; B-L1-S002: Models overview: deprecated and retired models (Mistral Docs) (Mistral AI) <https://docs.mistral.ai/models> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; B-L1-S001: Deprecations (OpenAI API docs) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/deprecations> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. None of this is a criticism of any one vendor. It is the normal life of a fast-moving product line, and it is why the review classifies a single proprietary frontier vendor behind an important service as unacceptable lock-in [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What the second route actually is.** A credible stressed exit is not a contract in a drawer. It is a second model from an unrelated vendor, qualified on the same evaluation suite, reached through the same gateway, in an approved region, and switched by configuration [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. For the deepest stress, the review adds an open-weight model already qualified on vLLM, because that route survives a vendor's commercial or legal event entirely [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The review is equally clear about where multi-vendor only adds complexity: two orchestration frameworks, two vector stores for one corpus or two observability platforms of record bring cost without resilience [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Build the evidence once.** The useful insight from setting the regimes side by side is how much of the evidence overlaps. A gateway request log naming model, version, region and fallback serves the firm's model-risk standard, Article 26-style logging, exit drills and data-location questions at once. A use-case inventory entry with its configuration bundle serves model risk, AI Act classification and board accountability [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The team that builds that register first will answer most of the next question from regulators without a new project [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is the **concentration ratio**: the share of production tokens, or of important business services, served by the largest single model vendor [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Measure it from gateway metering, because the gateway sees every call with its vendor, model and route [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Report it to the risk committee against a ceiling the firm sets; the review deliberately does not suggest a number [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Two companion measures make the ratio honest. **Qualified-alternative coverage** is the share of production use cases with a second model from a different vendor that has passed the same evaluation suite within the last quarter; the starting target is 100% for important business services [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. **Failover drill success** is the share of scheduled drills in which traffic moved to the alternative with quality inside threshold; the starting target is 100% [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Spend gives a cross-check: the share of AI spend with the largest provider, reported quarterly to the third-party risk committee [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

These targets are starting points for a firm to calibrate, not industry benchmarks [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A low ratio with an untested alternative is worse than a high ratio the board has knowingly accepted [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 10: regulation.** The regulatory mapping: not an Annex III use; transparency and literacy duties; model vendors as material outsourcing with exit plans; the agent recorded in the model inventory. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** The design is defensible to a regulator, not only to an architect. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** Never assume a vendor's oversight covers the firm's duties. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** The regulatory assessment attached to the use-case record. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Concentration ratio by provider for important services. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"No rule requires two vendors, so this is gold-plating."** The premise is right: no instrument in the review's fact base says so [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. But SS2/21 expects tested exit plans, including stressed exit [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S048: SS2/21 Outsourcing and third party risk management (and PS7/26, March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/supervisory-statement/2021/ss221-march-21.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. An exit that has never been run through the gateway is not tested. For a model behind an important service, a qualified second route is the cheapest way to make the plan real. Where a use is not important, a documented acceptance of single-vendor risk may be the right answer [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The discipline is in deciding which is which, and writing the decision down [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Route everything through the hyperscaler and the problem goes away."** It helps, because the hyperscaler is overseen and already contracted [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It does not remove the firm's own due-diligence and contingency duties [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-UK-CTP: UK Critical Third Parties regime (FSMA 2023 / FSMA 2000 s312L; PRA/FCA/BoE CTP rules) and the Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S023: The Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777); BoE/FCA/HMT announcements (HM Treasury / legislation.gov.uk / Bank of England) <https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2026/777/made> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. And it moves concentration rather than ending it: the model, gateway, guardrail and data services of one cloud then sit under a single designated provider [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Accept that consciously for the primary cloud, and keep a second route for important services [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"The rules are still moving. Wait for the request for information and the next DORA list."** The dates in this chapter will change, and the DORA list is updated every year [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-DORA: Regulation (EU) 2022/2554 Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S021: Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) pages and RoI reporting FAQ (ESMA / EBA / EIOPA) <https://www.esma.europa.eu/esmas-activities/digital-finance-and-innovation/digital-operational-resilience-act-dora> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The evidence will not change much. Inventory, logs, version bundles and exit drills serve every regime in the frame, and they are cheap to build once and expensive to retrofit [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. If a model vendor is ever designated, the firm that already measures its concentration will have the shortest conversation with its supervisor [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- Which of our GenAI use cases support an important business service, and who decided?
- For each one, what share of traffic goes to the largest model vendor, and what ceiling have we agreed?
- Which models do we reach directly rather than through a designated hyperscaler, and who carries that oversight?
- When did we last move live traffic to the second route, and did quality stay inside threshold?
- Which of our arrangements will need notification before a significant change from 18 March 2027?
- Could any prompt or configuration change turn a current use into an Annex III use without anyone noticing?
- Is our own GenAI model-risk standard written to SS1/23 quality, whatever our home regulator? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

Regulation rarely names the vendors you must use, but it will ask you to prove you can leave one, so build that proof before you need it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.


## 11. You may not need a vector database

*L6 Retrieval and knowledge stores, from Post 11 of the series.*

### The post

You may not need a vector database. You do need retrieval you can measure.

The category has quietly turned into a feature. General-purpose databases, search engines and even object storage now offer vector search, and hybrid retrieval, lexical plus semantic, is standard rather than advanced. For most regulated asset-management corpora, the database or search platform already in place will do the job. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S061: pgvector 0.8.7 Released (PostgreSQL Global Development Group (news post by pgvector p) <https://www.postgresql.org/about/news/pgvector-087-released-3392/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S137: MongoDB Search and Vector Search Now Run Anywhere <https://www.mongodb.com/company/blog/product-release-announcements/mongodb-search-vector-search-now-run-anywhere> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S133: Elastic 9.5: Columnar, VectorDB index mode & auto-calibration, and AI-driven alert triage <https://www.elastic.co/blog/whats-new-elastic-9-5-0> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S081: Amazon S3 Vectors is now generally available with 40 times the scale of preview (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/12/amazon-s3-vectors-generally-available/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S056: turbopuffer (Python SDK) - PyPI (PyPI / turbopuffer) <https://pypi.org/project/turbopuffer/> accessed 2026-10-07; A2-S103: Qdrant 1.17 - Relevance Feedback & Search Latency Improvements <https://qdrant.tech/blog/qdrant-1.17.x/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S101: Introducing cascading retrieval: Unifying dense and sparse with reranking (Pinecone) <https://www.pinecone.io/blog/cascading-retrieval/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

That matters because every extra store is another copy of confidential content to secure, retain, back up and eventually exit. So the first question is not which engine; it is whether a load test on your own data gives you a reason for one. Three reasons hold up: filtered latency or recall fails the budget, tenants must be isolated at a scale schemas cannot manage, or the corpus makes the existing platform's cost disproportionate. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal is filtered recall@k: recall measured with production-like entitlement filters applied, not on the open index. A store that looks excellent unfiltered can collapse once a restrictive filter leaves too few candidates. The tempting fix, retrying without the filter, is how another client's material reaches a draft. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to treat the store as a derived index, never the system of record. If you can rebuild it from approved sources, with a pinned embedding model, inside a tested time, it is also your backup and your exit plan. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: dedicated engines still earn their place on filtered search, tenant isolation and scale. The point is to arrive at one with evidence, not by default. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S103: Qdrant 1.17 - Relevance Feedback & Search Latency Improvements <https://qdrant.tech/blog/qdrant-1.17.x/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S124: Architecture - turbopuffer <https://turbopuffer.com/docs/architecture> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S117: Release Notes - Milvus (Milvus project) <https://milvus.io/docs/release_notes.md> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Retrieval is judged by what it returns under real permissions. An open-index benchmark answers a different question.

![You may not need a vector database](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P11.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 11. Use the platform you already run until a load test shows a trigger, and filter inside the search with no fallback. Generic; the leakage path is illustrative.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

**A category became a feature.** Three movements in 2025–26 changed this layer. First, hybrid retrieval became standard: turbopuffer ranks by vectors and BM25, Chroma Cloud offers vector, hybrid and full-text search, Milvus ships BM25, and Elasticsearch, MongoDB, Qdrant and Pinecone all fuse lexical and dense results [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S056: turbopuffer (Python SDK) - PyPI (PyPI / turbopuffer) <https://pypi.org/project/turbopuffer/> accessed 2026-10-07; A2-S060: chromadb - PyPI (PyPI / Chroma) <https://pypi.org/project/chromadb/> accessed 2026-10-07; A2-S054: milvus-lite - PyPI (PyPI / Milvus contributors) <https://pypi.org/project/milvus-lite/> accessed 2026-10-07; A2-S133: Elastic 9.5: Columnar, VectorDB index mode & auto-calibration, and AI-driven alert triage <https://www.elastic.co/blog/whats-new-elastic-9-5-0> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S141: Context Engineering Has a Retrieval Issue: Introducing rerank-3 and Native Reranking (MongoDB) <https://www.mongodb.com/company/blog/product-release-announcements/introducing-rerank-3-and-native-rerank> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S103: Qdrant 1.17 - Relevance Feedback & Search Latency Improvements <https://qdrant.tech/blog/qdrant-1.17.x/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S101: Introducing cascading retrieval: Unifying dense and sparse with reranking (Pinecone) <https://www.pinecone.io/blog/cascading-retrieval/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Second, general-purpose databases absorbed vector search. pgvector reached 0.8.7 on 1 October 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V1-S020: pgvector CHANGELOG.md (pgvector (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pgvector/pgvector/master/CHANGELOG.md> accessed 2026-10-08; V1-S022: pgvector 0.8.7 Released (PostgreSQL Global Development Group) <https://www.postgresql.org/about/news/pgvector-087-released-3392/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]], and MongoDB Vector Search is generally available on self-managed editions as well as Atlas [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S137: MongoDB Search and Vector Search Now Run Anywhere <https://www.mongodb.com/company/blog/product-release-announcements/mongodb-search-vector-search-now-run-anywhere> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S035: MongoDB Delivers Accurate AI Retrieval Wherever Enterprise Data Lives; Community Edition product page (MongoDB, Inc.) <https://investors.mongodb.com/news-releases/news-release-details/mongodb-delivers-accurate-ai-retrieval-wherever-enterprise-data> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Third, vectors moved onto object storage: Amazon S3 Vectors has been generally available since December 2025, Milvus 3.0 is "lake-native", and turbopuffer keeps all durable state in object storage [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S081: Amazon S3 Vectors is now generally available with 40 times the scale of preview (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/12/amazon-s3-vectors-generally-available/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S030: Amazon S3 Vectors is now generally available with 40 times the scale of preview; S3 Vectors limitations; March 2026 region expansion (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/12/amazon-s3-vectors-generally-available/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A2-S118: Zilliz Announces Milvus 3.0, Making the World's Most Adopted Open-Source Vector Database Lake-Native <https://zilliz.com/news/milvus-3-0-lake-native-vector-database> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S124: Architecture - turbopuffer <https://turbopuffer.com/docs/architecture> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Meanwhile some vendors repositioned above the store. Pinecone now sells Nexus, a "knowledge engine for agents" [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S073: General Availability of Pinecone Nexus Proves Knowledge Drives Real Outcomes for Agentic AI <https://www.pinecone.io/newsroom/general-availability-of-pinecone-nexus-proves-knowledge-drives-real-outcomes-for-agentic-ai/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S029: General Availability of Pinecone Nexus (press release); Nexus GA blog <https://www.pinecone.io/newsroom/general-availability-of-pinecone-nexus-proves-knowledge-drives-real-outcomes-for-agentic-ai/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The term "vector database" now describes a feature more than a product category [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why another store is a liability before it is an asset.** A retrieval store holds a second representation of the firm's most sensitive unstructured content [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Embeddings are derived from that content and should carry the same classification, residency and erasure duties as the text they came from [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Residency has to cover vectors, raw text, caches, replicas and backups, and an erasure request has to reach the index as well as the source [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. For an EU entity, a vector-database SaaS is an ICT third-party service that belongs in the DORA register of information [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-DORA: Regulation (EU) 2022/2554 Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S021: Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) pages and RoI reporting FAQ (ESMA / EBA / EIOPA) <https://www.esma.europa.eu/esmas-activities/digital-finance-and-innovation/digital-operational-resilience-act-dora> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. And because the specialist SaaS stores all run on the major hyperscalers, their outages correlate with the cloud's [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S069: Bring Your Own Cloud (BYOC) - Pinecone Docs <https://docs.pinecone.io/guides/production/bring-your-own-cloud> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S121: Zilliz Cloud Now Available in AWS Europe (Ireland) <https://zilliz.com/blog/zilliz-cloud-now-available-in-aws-europe-ireland> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S105: Qdrant Security: Compliance, Isolation, and Encryption Across Every Deployment <https://qdrant.tech/security/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S111: Vector Database Pricing - Weaviate <https://weaviate.io/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S125: turbopuffer BYOC <https://turbopuffer.com/docs/byoc> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S140: Search Node Costs - Atlas (MongoDB) <https://www.mongodb.com/docs/atlas/billing/search-node/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S135: Elastic Cloud Hosted regions <https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/cloud/cloud-hosted/regions> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S129: Chroma Cloud - Chroma Docs <https://docs.trychroma.com/cloud/getting-started> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. None of that argues against vectors. It argues against an extra copy that nobody can justify.

**The three triggers.** The review's decision tree starts from the database or search platform the firm already operates: PostgreSQL with pgvector, Elasticsearch where lexical matching on fund codes and share-class names matters, or MongoDB where it is already the system of record [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It moves to a dedicated engine only when a load test on the firm's own data shows at least one of three triggers: filtered p95 latency or filtered recall fails the budget; tenants must be isolated at a scale schemas cannot manage cleanly; or corpus size or write rate makes the existing platform's cost disproportionate [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. If a trigger is real, the choice of engine follows where it must run. Qdrant or Milvus suit the firm's own estate; Pinecone, turbopuffer or Zilliz suit a data plane in the firm's cloud account [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. turbopuffer reports Anthropic as a customer [R]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Reported — A2-S126: Ex-Shopify engineers raise fresh financing to scale Turbopuffer's AI search (BetaKit) <https://betakit.com/ex-shopify-engineers-raise-fresh-financing-to-scale-turbopuffers-ai-search/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], so the review names Zilliz Cloud BYOC as the independent alternative for that profile [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Weaviate stays Tactical while its open-core licence position settles [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S115: weaviate/weaviate (README and licence files; PR #13360) (Weaviate) <https://github.com/weaviate/weaviate> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S070: weaviate/weaviate README (Community vs Enterprise Edition), releases, PR #13360, PR #12942 (Weaviate (GitHub)) <https://github.com/weaviate/weaviate> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]] [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Where leakage comes from.** There are three ways to apply a filter [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Post-filtering runs the nearest-neighbour search first and discards disallowed results. It is cheap, and it is the cause of filter collapse: a restrictive filter leaves too few results, so recall drops or the system quietly widens the filter [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Pre-filtering, or filter-aware traversal, restricts the search to permitted candidates as it runs; Qdrant added ACORN-based filtered search in 1.16, and pgvector 0.8.0 added iterative index scans for filtered queries [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S103: Qdrant 1.17 - Relevance Feedback & Search Latency Improvements <https://qdrant.tech/blog/qdrant-1.17.x/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S020: pgvector CHANGELOG.md (pgvector (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pgvector/pgvector/master/CHANGELOG.md> accessed 2026-10-08]. Partitioning gives each tenant its own namespace or collection, so the filter becomes an address [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The review's illustrative scenario shows the failure in practice. A shared index post-filters by fund; a small segregated mandate's query returns nothing permitted; a fallback retries without the filter "to avoid an empty context"; and another client's hedging rationale reaches the draft [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Three choices would each have prevented it: filtering inside the search, a hard empty result instead of a fallback, and a cross-fund leakage test in the evaluation suite [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The engines provide the mechanism; the firm provides the policy. turbopuffer, for example, states that permissions must be implemented with filters because it has no built-in row- or document-level access control [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L6-S002: turbopuffer Regions; Security; Permissions; Roadmap & Changelog; Private Networking <https://turbopuffer.com/docs/regions> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Most engines are in the same position [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. So the entitlement logic belongs in the firm's identity control and should be compiled into whichever store is used. Held only in one store's proprietary syntax, it becomes lock-in the review classifies as unacceptable [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Derived, rebuildable, patched.** The review's defining rule is that the store is a derived index over approved content, never the system of record [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Keep the raw text and the embedding-model version beside every vector, and the index can be rebuilt without re-parsing (Chapter 13) [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That rebuild is then the primary backup and the exit route. SS2/21 expects documented and tested exit plans [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S048: SS2/21 Outsourcing and third party risk management (and PS7/26, March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/supervisory-statement/2021/ss221-march-21.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], so rebuild time belongs in the exit plan and should be tested twice a year [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A "knowledge engine" adopted as the only home of curated knowledge breaks the rule; adopted as a disposable derived layer, it does not [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Finally, patch the store like a database, because it is one. pgvector 0.8.7 fixed an index-build buffer overflow rated CVSS 8.8 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V1-S022: pgvector 0.8.7 Released (PostgreSQL Global Development Group) <https://www.postgresql.org/about/news/pgvector-087-released-3392/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A2-S061: pgvector 0.8.7 Released (PostgreSQL Global Development Group (news post by pgvector p) <https://www.postgresql.org/about/news/pgvector-087-released-3392/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Managed hosts lag upstream: the latest Amazon RDS release seen carried pgvector 0.8.2, while Cloud SQL moved to 0.8.5 in August 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L6-S004: Extension versions for Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL; RDS PostgreSQL release notes; Aurora pgvector announcements (Amazon Web Services) <https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/PostgreSQLReleaseNotes/postgresql-extensions.html> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; B-L6-S005: Vector search in Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server; Cloud SQL vector embeddings; AlloyDB vector search overview (Microsoft; Google Cloud) <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/postgresql/extensions/how-to-use-pgvector> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Self-hosted defaults need care too. Chroma removed its built-in authentication in 1.0, and Milvus Lite has no authentication or TLS [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S131: Migration - Chroma Docs <https://docs.trychroma.com/docs/overview/migration> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S054: milvus-lite - PyPI (PyPI / Milvus contributors) <https://pypi.org/project/milvus-lite/> accessed 2026-10-07]. A prototype store promoted to production without returning to the decision tree is one of the review's named anti-patterns [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is **filtered recall@k**: recall@k measured on a labelled query set with production-like entitlement filters applied, not on the open index [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Run the same queries with and without filters. The starting target is filtered recall within an agreed tolerance of unfiltered recall for the same queries [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A large gap means the store is collapsing under selective filters, which is exactly when teams are tempted to add a fallback [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Three companion measures keep it honest. **Entitlement leakage**, tested with adversarial queries per tenant in CI, has a target of zero, and any occurrence is a severity-1 incident [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. **p95 latency under filter** is measured with the most selective realistic filter on tenant-skewed data, against a budget per use case [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. **Rebuild time** from the approved source with the pinned model should sit inside the exit-plan tolerance, tested twice a year [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

These are starting points to calibrate on the firm's own corpus, not industry benchmarks [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 11: retrieval store.** One store already run by the firm, with three collections (prior commentaries, style guide, approved market notes) and fund-level entitlement filters applied inside the search. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** Context arrives only from documents the analyst is entitled to see. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** No unfiltered fallback: no permitted result means an empty result. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** Retrieved chunk IDs and versions per draft. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Filtered recall against unfiltered recall. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"Dedicated engines are faster. The benchmarks say so."** Some are, on some workloads. But vendor latency figures are context, not decision inputs, and unfiltered benchmarks overstate both recall and speed [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The honest test is a load test with the firm's filters and tenant skew on its own corpus. If that shows a trigger, the dedicated engine has earned its place, and the review recommends one [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Qdrant and Milvus are rated Strategic for exactly that case, which is hardly a bias against the category [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Our database team does not want vector workloads on the operational estate."** That is a fair operational concern, not a prejudice. Memory-resident indexes are expensive, and a team can reasonably refuse to carry them on a critical database [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That concern is trigger (c), disproportionate cost, and it should be evidenced in the same load test. A search platform the firm already runs, or a separate instance of the same database, may answer it without a new vendor [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"An empty result is a poor experience for the analyst."** It is less poor than another client's material in a draft. The review's worked example returns nothing when no permitted passage meets the relevance threshold. The workflow then drafts from the attribution output and the style guide alone, flagged "no comparable commentary" [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A visible gap is a prompt to add content. A silent fallback is an incident waiting for a reviewer to miss it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Segregated mandates should also get their own partition, so that isolation never depends on a filter clause alone [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- How many retrieval stores hold copies of client content today, and who owns each one?
- Is our recall measured with production entitlement filters applied, or on the open index?
- Does any code path retry a query without its filter when results come back empty?
- Can we rebuild each index from approved sources with a pinned model, and how long did it last take?
- Which load-test result, if any, justified each dedicated engine we run?
- Which pgvector or engine version is our managed host actually running against current CVEs?
- Where do our entitlement rules live, and would they survive a change of store? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

Choose the store you already run until your own filtered load test gives you a reason not to, and make sure you could rebuild it tomorrow [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.


## 12. Every retrieved document is untrusted input

*C7 AI security, from Post 12 of the series.*

### The post

A language model follows instructions it finds in any text it reads. Give it tools, and those instructions become actions.

That is why every retrieved document is untrusted input. Retrieval is about finding the right passages; security is about passages someone else wrote for you. A broker note, a web page or an email can carry hidden text, and it arrives through the same pipeline as the firm's own knowledge. Indirect prompt injection is a supply-chain problem, not a chat problem. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The supply chain is literal too. In March 2026, malicious releases of a widely used open-source model gateway were published to a public package index using stolen release credentials. The component that holds every provider key was itself the target. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S008: Security Update: Suspected Supply Chain Incident (and Security Townhall Updates) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/blog/security-update-march-2026> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S009: v1.83.0 - Official Release (Post Supply Chain Incident) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/release_notes/v1.83.0/v1-83-0> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S010: Your AI Gateway Was a Backdoor: Inside the LiteLLM Supply Chain Compromise (Trend Micro Research) <https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/26/c/inside-litellm-supply-chain-compromise.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S027: LiteLLM PyPI supply chain attack (with NetSPI, Comet, Snyk/Datadog reporting) (FutureSearch) <https://futuresearch.ai/blog/litellm-pypi-supply-chain-attack/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The first design rule needs no product: no agent should hold untrusted input, sensitive data and an outbound channel at the same time. Remove any one of the three and an injected instruction has nowhere to go. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal is simple to count: the number of agents that combine all three. The target is zero, unless there is a documented exception with a named owner. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Then layer the rest so no single detector has to be right: secrets brokered outside the model, short-lived credentials per request, pinned and hashed dependencies, retrieved text marked as data, and a runtime detector behind the gateway as a replaceable part, not the foundation. The leadership move is to buy detection last. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: marking retrieved text as data and screening it reduces the risk; it does not remove it. Capability separation is what turns a successful injection into a non-event. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

A design that depends on a filter catching everything is a hope. One that leaves an attacker nothing to call is an architecture.

![Every retrieved document is untrusted input](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P12.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 12. No agent should hold all three at once. Around that rule, six layers defend the worked example, so no single detector has to be right. Generic; the injected note is illustrative.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

**Code and data are no longer separate.** Conventional software keeps instructions in code and treats content as data. A language model does not make that distinction: it follows instructions wherever it finds them, and an agent with tools turns them into actions [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Every retrieved document, web page, email, tool result and memory entry is therefore input that an attacker may have written [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The industry taxonomy has caught up. The OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications for 2026 opens with ASI01 Agent Goal Hijack [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-OWASP-AGENTIC: OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications for 2026 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S042: OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications for 2026 (launched 9-10 December 2025) (OWASP GenAI Security Project) <https://genai.owasp.org/resource/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-for-2026/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Later entries include tool misuse, agentic supply-chain vulnerabilities, unexpected code execution, and memory and context poisoning [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-C4-S005: OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications (release post and related OWASP pages) (OWASP GenAI Security Project) <https://genai.owasp.org/2025/12/09/owasp-top-10-for-agentic-applications-the-benchmark-for-agentic-security-in-the-age-of-autonomous-ai/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Retrieval (Chapter 11) is one of the main routes in, which is why indirect injection is best understood as a supply-chain problem: the payload arrives through the same pipeline as the firm's own knowledge [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The supply chain is not a metaphor.** On 24 March 2026, malicious LiteLLM releases 1.82.7 and 1.82.8 were published to PyPI using stolen release credentials [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S008: Security Update: Suspected Supply Chain Incident (and Security Townhall Updates) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/blog/security-update-march-2026> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S009: v1.83.0 - Official Release (Post Supply Chain Incident) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/release_notes/v1.83.0/v1-83-0> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S010: Your AI Gateway Was a Backdoor: Inside the LiteLLM Supply Chain Compromise (Trend Micro Research) <https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/26/c/inside-litellm-supply-chain-compromise.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S027: LiteLLM PyPI supply chain attack (with NetSPI, Comet, Snyk/Datadog reporting) (FutureSearch) <https://futuresearch.ai/blog/litellm-pypi-supply-chain-attack/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. They were live for about 40 minutes according to LiteLLM, or about three hours according to Snyk, before quarantine [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S008: Security Update: Suspected Supply Chain Incident (and Security Townhall Updates) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/blog/security-update-march-2026> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S009: v1.83.0 - Official Release (Post Supply Chain Incident) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/release_notes/v1.83.0/v1-83-0> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S010: Your AI Gateway Was a Backdoor: Inside the LiteLLM Supply Chain Compromise (Trend Micro Research) <https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/26/c/inside-litellm-supply-chain-compromise.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S027: LiteLLM PyPI supply chain attack (with NetSPI, Comet, Snyk/Datadog reporting) (FutureSearch) <https://futuresearch.ai/blog/litellm-pypi-supply-chain-attack/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The route in is reported two ways. LiteLLM attributes it to a compromised scanner in its build pipeline; other reports describe a hijacked maintainer account [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S009: v1.83.0 - Official Release (Post Supply Chain Incident) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/release_notes/v1.83.0/v1-83-0> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S027: LiteLLM PyPI supply chain attack (with NetSPI, Comet, Snyk/Datadog reporting) (FutureSearch) <https://futuresearch.ai/blog/litellm-pypi-supply-chain-attack/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The first clean build from a rebuilt pipeline, 1.83.0, followed on 31 March after a forensic review, and container images have been signed since [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S009: v1.83.0 - Official Release (Post Supply Chain Incident) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/release_notes/v1.83.0/v1-83-0> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S027: LiteLLM PyPI supply chain attack (with NetSPI, Comet, Snyk/Datadog reporting) (FutureSearch) <https://futuresearch.ai/blog/litellm-pypi-supply-chain-attack/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; V2-S028: PyPI JSON API: litellm, guardrails-ai, presidio-analyzer, nemoguardrails, llamafirewall, langfuse, promptfoo, modelscan, picklescan, fickling, safetensors, mode <https://pypi.org/pypi/litellm/json> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A6-S051: LiteLLM README (GitHub) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/BerriAI/litellm/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. The lesson for an architect is uncomfortable and simple. The gateway, the component that holds every provider key, was itself the target [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A dependency pinned by hash from a private mirror would not have resolved to the malicious versions [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Model files carry a parallel risk. Pickle-based formats can execute code on load, and the safetensors project describes pickle as "Unsafe, runs arbitrary code" [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S082: ModelScan README (Protect AI (Palo Alto Networks)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/protectai/modelscan/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; B-C7-S006: safetensors README (Hugging Face) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/huggingface/safetensors/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-08]. Scanning helps, but Hugging Face itself notes that it reduces risk rather than removing it [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S037: Hugging Face Hub Security docs (malware scanning, pickle scanning, third-party scanners Protect AI and JFrog) <https://huggingface.co/docs/hub/security> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].

**The rule that needs no product.** The single most effective injection control is architectural: an agent that reads untrusted content should not also hold sensitive data and an outbound channel [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Remove any one of the three and an injected instruction has nowhere to go. In practice that means reader agents that cannot act, and actor agents that never read untrusted content. A read-only tool, a deterministic workflow and a human approval gate do most of the work [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The review's illustrative scenario shows the opposite design: a research assistant with URL fetch, internal search and email, and an API key in its system prompt. A planted line in a broker PDF sends holdings out in a URL query string and puts the key in a footnote [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Nothing in that design was exotic. It combined all three capabilities in one context, with a secret inside the prompt [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Then the layers.** Once the capability rule holds, the review layers the rest so that no single control has to be right [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

- *Secrets brokered outside the model.* The agent authenticates as a workload and receives a short-lived credential for one downstream system; the model never sees it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. HashiCorp Vault's agentic IAM enforces the intersection of the user's permissions, an agent ceiling policy and request-scoped details, and records both user and agent in its audit log [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S034: HashiCorp Vault agentic IAM is now generally available (HashiCorp (IBM)) <https://www.hashicorp.com/en/blog/hashicorp-vault-agentic-iam-is-now-generally-available> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Vault is IBM-owned and under BUSL 1.1 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S032: IBM Completes Acquisition of HashiCorp, Creates Comprehensive, End-to-End Hybrid Cloud Platform <https://newsroom.ibm.com/2025-02-27-ibm-completes-acquisition-of-hashicorp,-creates-comprehensive,-end-to-end-hybrid-cloud-platform> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S060: HashiCorp Vault LICENSE (BUSL 1.1) (HashiCorp / IBM) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hashicorp/vault/main/LICENSE> accessed 2026-10-07]; a cloud's native secrets service with workload identity is the alternative for a single-cloud estate [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Pinned and hashed dependencies* from a private mirror, with a cooling-off period before production and a software bill of materials for each image [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Retrieved text marked as data.* Each chunk carries its source and trust tier, and the prompt template wraps it as quoted reference material [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *A runtime detector behind the gateway*, screening retrieved chunks and outputs as well as user input, with its verdicts sent to the SIEM [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why detection comes last.** The specialist market has largely been bought. Check Point acquired Lakera, completed on 22 October 2025. Palo Alto Networks acquired Protect AI in July 2025. Prompt Security, CalypsoAI and Pangea went to SentinelOne, F5 and CrowdStrike, all closing in September 2025 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S012: Check Point to Acquire Lakera, Redefining Security for the AI era (Check Point Software) <https://blog.checkpoint.com/security/check-point-to-acquire-lakera-redefining-security-for-the-ai-era/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S038: Check Point Software Reports 2025 Third Quarter Financial Results (with 'Check Point Completes Acquisition of Lakera' release via cxotoday) (Check Point Software Technologies) <https://www.checkpoint.com/press-releases/check-point-software-reports-2025-third-quarter-financial-results/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A7-S014: Palo Alto Networks Completes Acquisition of Protect AI <https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/company/press/2025/palo-alto-networks-completes-acquisition-of-protect-ai> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S039: Palo Alto Networks Completes Acquisition of Protect AI <https://investors.paloaltonetworks.com/news-releases/news-release-details/palo-alto-networks-completes-acquisition-protect-ai/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A7-S018: SentinelOne Form 10-Q (FY2026) and completion press release for Prompt Security (SentinelOne / US SEC) <https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0001583708/000158370826000041/s-20260430.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S019: F5, Inc. Form 10-Q (quarter ended 31 Dec 2025) and F5 blog on CalypsoAI (F5 / US SEC) <https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1048695/000104869526000023/ffiv-20251231.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S020: CrowdStrike to Acquire Pangea to Secure Every Layer of Enterprise AI; CrowdStrike FY2026 Form 10-K <https://ir.crowdstrike.com/news-releases/news-release-details/crowdstrike-acquire-pangea-secure-every-layer-enterprise-ai> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S040: SEC filings and announcements: SentinelOne completes Prompt Security (8-K, 10-Q); F5 completes CalypsoAI (F5 blog, 10-Q); CrowdStrike completes Pangea (X post,  (SEC EDGAR / SentinelOne / F5 / CrowdStrike) <https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1535527/000153552726000010/crwd-20260131.htm> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. HiddenLayer is the main independent left in that set [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S017: HiddenLayer Raises $100M Series B to Advance AI Security <https://www.hiddenlayer.com/news/hiddenlayer-100m-series-b-ai-security> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S047: HiddenLayer Raises $100 Million Series B Led By Delta-v Capital (with unite.ai, AI Weekly) (Pulse 2.0) <https://pulse2.com/hiddenlayer-raises-100-million-series-b-led-by-delta-v-capital/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. AI security is consolidating into network and endpoint security platforms, not into model vendors [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That is a reason to buy from an existing security supplier if it fits, and a reason to keep the detector behind one thin interface at the gateway so the choice stays reversible [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Detectors also create a new copy of client data: Lakera's dashboard records all prompts and model outputs by default [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-C7-S001: AI Guardrails Dashboard (Lakera / Check Point docs) (Lakera (Check Point)) <https://docs.lakera.ai/docs/platform> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Treat the detector's own store as a client-data store [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What it looks like in the worked example.** The commentary agent faces an approved third-party market note with hidden text telling it to invent a currency effect and send the draft elsewhere [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Six layers answer it. The workflow has no email, HTTP or file-write tool. A deterministic check fails any figure that does not match the attribution engine. Retrieved text is marked as data. A detector screens chunks and quarantines the source. Canary documents in staging fail the release if they change an output. A portfolio manager approves every draft [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. For an EU entity, a successful exfiltration would also be an ICT-related incident to classify under DORA [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-DORA: Regulation (EU) 2022/2554 Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S021: Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) pages and RoI reporting FAQ (ESMA / EBA / EIOPA) <https://www.esma.europa.eu/esmas-activities/digital-finance-and-innovation/digital-operational-resilience-act-dora> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is **agent capability exposure**: the number of agents that combine untrusted input, sensitive data and an outbound channel [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Count it by reviewing the tool inventory against each agent's data access and input sources. The starting target is zero, unless an exception is documented with a named owner [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Four companion measures show whether the layers behind the rule work. The **indirect-injection canary rate** is the share of planted canary instructions in a test corpus that cause any unapproved tool call; target zero [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. **Secrets exposure**, from scanning traces, prompt registries and repositories, should also be zero [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. **Credential lifetime** for agents should be measured in minutes to hours, not months [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. **Time to revoke** an agent's or package's credentials after a compromise signal should be under an hour, proven in drills [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

These are starting points for a firm to calibrate, not industry benchmarks [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The count matters most when it is rising, because that usually means agents are gaining tools faster than anyone is reviewing them [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 12: ai security.** Defence in depth against a poisoned market note: nothing to hijack, numbers that cannot be rewritten, retrieved text marked as data, chunk screening, canary documents and human approval. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** No single control has to catch an injected instruction. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** Untrusted input, sensitive data and an outbound channel never meet in one agent. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** Screening and canary results per run. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Agents combining all three risk factors: zero. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"An agent without web access and email is not much use."** Sometimes that is true. The answer is to split the work, not to drop the rule [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. One agent reads and summarises untrusted material with no outbound channel. A separate step, or a person, decides what is sent and where. Where a single agent genuinely needs all three, record the exception, name its owner and add compensating controls such as an egress allowlist and human approval [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The split usually costs less than expected, because the outbound step was rarely the part that needed a model [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"A good detector will catch injection. Why design around it?"** Detectors reduce the risk; they do not remove it, and the first bypass becomes a breach if the detector is the control [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Five specialist vendors also changed hands in 2025, so a design that depends on one detector depends on its owner's roadmap [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S012: Check Point to Acquire Lakera, Redefining Security for the AI era (Check Point Software) <https://blog.checkpoint.com/security/check-point-to-acquire-lakera-redefining-security-for-the-ai-era/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S014: Palo Alto Networks Completes Acquisition of Protect AI <https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/company/press/2025/palo-alto-networks-completes-acquisition-of-protect-ai> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S018: SentinelOne Form 10-Q (FY2026) and completion press release for Prompt Security (SentinelOne / US SEC) <https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0001583708/000158370826000041/s-20260430.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S019: F5, Inc. Form 10-Q (quarter ended 31 Dec 2025) and F5 blog on CalypsoAI (F5 / US SEC) <https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1048695/000104869526000023/ffiv-20251231.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S020: CrowdStrike to Acquire Pangea to Secure Every Layer of Enterprise AI; CrowdStrike FY2026 Form 10-K <https://ir.crowdstrike.com/news-releases/news-release-details/crowdstrike-acquire-pangea-secure-every-layer-enterprise-ai> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Use detectors, ideally two from different owners on untrusted input, but as the second line [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"We only use hosted model APIs, so supply chain is the provider's problem."** For model weights, largely yes [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. But the SDKs, the gateway, the agent framework and any tool servers run in the firm's estate. The March 2026 compromise hit a gateway package, not a model [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S008: Security Update: Suspected Supply Chain Incident (and Security Townhall Updates) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/blog/security-update-march-2026> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S027: LiteLLM PyPI supply chain attack (with NetSPI, Comet, Snyk/Datadog reporting) (FutureSearch) <https://futuresearch.ai/blog/litellm-pypi-supply-chain-attack/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Pin those by hash, as for any other code [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. If the firm later fine-tunes its own small model, store it as safetensors, scan it and sign it before it is loaded [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- How many of our agents combine untrusted input, sensitive data and an outbound channel today?
- For each exception, who owns it and what compensates for it?
- Could any credential, key or token appear in a prompt, a trace or an agent's memory?
- Are our AI dependencies pinned by hash from a private mirror, including the gateway and its plug-ins?
- Do we screen retrieved chunks and tool outputs, or only what users type?
- If our runtime detector's owner changed tomorrow, how long would replacing it take?
- Where does the detector vendor keep our prompts, and for how long? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

Design so that a successful injection has nothing to call, and treat every detector as a replaceable second line [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.


## 13. Retrieval quality is a two-stage problem

*L7 Embeddings and reranking (retrieval optimisation), from Post 13 of the series.*

### The post

Retrieval quality is a two-stage problem, and it is easy to tune only one stage.

The first stage casts a wide net: embed the question and pull back fifty or a hundred candidates. The second stage reorders them, so the handful the model actually reads are the right ones. Teams swap the embedding model to fix answers a reranker should have fixed, or add a reranker to rescue a first stage that never found the right passage. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

So measure the stages separately. For the first, the signal is recall@k: the share of questions in an in-domain golden set whose relevant passage appears anywhere in the candidates. A starting target is 0.95. Ranking quality after the reranker is a second number, and only then worth tuning. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

There is a quieter point underneath. The embedding model version is production configuration. Change it and the whole corpus must be re-embedded. Mix two versions in one index and the scores stop being comparable, with no error to tell you. Tag every vector with its model version, refuse queries that would mix versions, and migrate by building a second index behind a regression gate. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"} [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to choose models by your own evaluation, not by leaderboard or vendor claim. One to three hundred labelled questions, written by the analysts who will use the system, will tell you more than any published benchmark. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: dual-index migration means running two indexes for a while, at roughly twice the storage. That is small next to an unplanned re-embed under time pressure. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Most retrieval problems are measurement problems first. Once both stages are visible, the fixes are usually plain.
