Tokenisation needs key management and an entitled re-identification path, but those are known patterns: Sensitive Data Protection allows its de-identification key to be wrapped by a Cloud KMS key [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-C3-S002: De-identify and re-identify sensitive data (Sensitive Data Protection) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/sensitive-data-protection/docs/inspect-sensitive-text-de-identify> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]], and keys can equally sit in the firm's own KMS, HSM or vault [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Detectors miss things, so the control is theatre."** Detectors do miss things; Presidio's documentation says it is not guaranteed to find all sensitive data [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S068: Presidio README (GitHub, data-privacy-stack) (Presidio / Data Privacy Stack) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/data-privacy-stack/presidio/main/README.MD> accessed 2026-10-07]. That is an argument for layering, not abandonment. Classify at source so detection is not the only line, run a second detector on samples, fail closed on external egress if the service is down, and measure residual leakage as a standing number [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- For one typical request, can we list every store that ends up holding a client identifier, and its region? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- How many different detectors and policies do we run today, and who owns their consistency? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Do we measure detection recall on our own identifiers, per class, or rely on a vendor's generic figures? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Where are our token keys held, and who can re-identify, with what record of purpose? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- What happens to an external model call if the privacy service is unavailable: does it fail open or closed? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Is any evaluation dataset or trace store holding raw identifiers "because it is internal"? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Could we complete an erasure request across prompts, traces, memory and evaluation sets within the statutory deadline? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

Write the privacy policy once, enforce it wherever data moves, and give the model placeholders rather than people. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}


## 17. Evaluation is a plane, not the last box

*L9 Evaluation and observability, from Post 17 of the series.*

### The post

Most diagrams of the AI stack put evaluation in the last box. It is the layer that ages slowest — and the one most teams build last. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

That ordering is the trap. Evals added after go-live measure the damage, not the quality. When a drafted figure turns out to be wrong, the question becomes which outputs were affected, and nobody can answer it if the prompt version was never recorded and the traces expired on a vendor's free tier. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

This series walks the enterprise GenAI stack in layer order, each layer paired with the control that makes it safe in production. Evaluation is last in the numbering and first in the build order. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Evaluation and observability are not a box at the end. They are a plane across the whole stack. Instrument once, on an open telemetry standard, and keep the evaluation harness and the evidence store in your own hands. The products on top are replaceable: five of the eleven assessed in this space now belong to, or are being bought by, larger platform vendors. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S045: Dynatrace Completes Acquisition of Arize, Extending AI Observability Across the Full Development Lifecycle (Dynatrace, Inc.) <https://ir.dynatrace.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/439/dynatrace-completes-acquisition-of-arize-extending-ai-observability-across-the-full-development-lifecycle> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A1-S021: ClickHouse raises $400M Series D, acquires Langfuse, launches Postgres (ClickHouse, Inc.) <https://clickhouse.com/blog/clickhouse-raises-400-million-series-d-acquires-langfuse-launches-postgres> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A1-S024: OpenAI to acquire Promptfoo <https://openai.com/index/openai-to-acquire-promptfoo/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A1-S131: CoreWeave Completes Acquisition of Weights & Biases (CoreWeave, Inc.) <https://investors.coreweave.com/news/news-details/2025/CoreWeave-Completes-Acquisition-of-Weights--Biases/default.aspx> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The one signal worth starting with is numeric faithfulness: the share of figures in a generated draft that match the authoritative source under an agreed rounding rule. The target is 100%, and any miss blocks the release. A language-model judge does not get a vote on numbers. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is sequencing. Fund the eval suite before the first feature, and run it on every change of model, prompt or index. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: the shared telemetry conventions for GenAI are still marked as in development. That is an argument for owning the harness, not for waiting. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S058: Semantic conventions for generative AI systems (status: Development) (OpenTelemetry (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-telemetry/semantic-conventions-genai/main/docs/gen-ai/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Diagrams keep changing. What you measured, and can still prove, is what lasts. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

![Evaluation is a plane, not the last box](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P17.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 17. Instrument once on an open standard, own the eval harness and evidence store, and treat the products as plug-ins. The left-hand stack is generic.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

This layer answers two questions for every generative AI system: is the output good enough to use, and what actually happened when it ran [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}? It owns three jobs. Evaluation covers offline and CI suites, online scoring, human review, language-model judges and red-teaming. Observability gives each request one trace covering prompt, model and version, retrieved context, tool calls, latency, tokens, cost and errors. Production feedback sends reviewer and user signals, drift and regressions back into the datasets that gate the next release [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Where the layer sits now.** The popular stack diagram drew evaluation as the last box. In the view at the end of Q3 2026, the review repositions it: L9 is the evaluation and observability plane, drawn alongside the control plane, on which every other layer produces evidence [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It forms one evidence plane with model-risk governance (Chapter 18): this layer produces the evidence; governance sets thresholds, approves and retains it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why "plane" is the accurate word.** Three pieces of evidence support it. First, the instrumentation standard spans the stack. The OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions cover client inference, agents, tool execution, retrieval and evaluation [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S061: OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions (repository docs index) (OpenTelemetry (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-telemetry/semantic-conventions-genai/main/docs/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07], and define an evaluation-result event with name, score, label and explanation [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S059: GenAI events incl. gen_ai.evaluation.result (OpenTelemetry (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-telemetry/semantic-conventions-genai/main/docs/gen-ai/gen-ai-events.md> accessed 2026-10-07], so a score can travel on the same pipe as the trace it judges [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. OpenInference, Apache-2.0, is the alternative format used by Arize's tools [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S049: OpenInference LICENSE (Apache-2.0) (Arize AI (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Arize-ai/openinference/main/LICENSE> accessed 2026-10-07; A1-S066: Arize Phoenix README (Arize AI (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Arize-ai/phoenix/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. Second, the tools couple CI to production. Six of the evaluation products assessed document a CI path, from pytest plugins for LangSmith, Phoenix and Opik to DeepEval's test framework, Promptfoo's pipeline integrations and Braintrust's pull-request action [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S108: LangSmith Python SDK README (LangChain, Inc. (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/langchain-ai/langsmith-sdk/main/python/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A1-S106: Phoenix: Eval CI with pytest (docs source) (Arize AI (GitHub docs source)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Arize-ai/phoenix/main/docs/phoenix/datasets-and-experiments/how-to-experiments/eval-ci-with-pytest.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07; A1-S067: Opik README (Comet ML (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/comet-ml/opik/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A1-S068: DeepEval README (Confident AI (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/confident-ai/deepeval/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A1-S065: Promptfoo CI/CD integration docs (Promptfoo (OpenAI) (GitHub docs source)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/promptfoo/promptfoo/main/site/docs/integrations/ci-cd.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A1-S109: Braintrust Eval Action README (Braintrust Data, Inc. (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/braintrustdata/eval-action/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. The platforms also run evaluators over production traces [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S072: Opik Online Evaluation rules (Comet ML (Opik docs source)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/comet-ml/opik/main/apps/opik-documentation/documentation/fern/docs-v2/production/online-evaluation/rules.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07; A1-S073: Langfuse README (Langfuse / ClickHouse (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/langfuse/langfuse/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A1-S099: Datadog LLM Observability: Evaluations (Datadog, Inc.) <https://docs.datadoghq.com/llm_observability/evaluations/> accessed 2026-10-07]. Third, governance products now consume evaluation evidence directly: IBM watsonx.governance retrieves agent evaluation metrics against thresholds, and ValidMind logs tests to the governance record [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S111: IBM announcements 2026: Enforcement Tracking; AI Asset Discovery; security metrics and agent monitoring; Think 2026 AI assurance <https://www.ibm.com/new/announcements/from-governance-policies-to-governance-proof-with-enforcement-tracking-for-watsonx-orchestrate> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S003: validmind on PyPI (JSON API) (PyPI / ValidMind) <https://pypi.org/pypi/validmind/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A7-S056: ValidMind docs: ongoing monitoring <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/validmind/documentation/main/site/guide/monitoring/ongoing-monitoring.qmd> accessed 2026-10-07].

**Three loops, one store.** The mechanics are three loops sharing one trace and dataset store [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Every layer emits spans. Versioned datasets of golden cases, past failures and red-team cases run on every change to model, prompt, retrieval configuration or tool, and the results gate the merge. Evaluators score a sample of production traces, reviewer edits attach as scores, and failing cases are promoted into the CI dataset, which closes the loop [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The key design choice is a firm-owned OpenTelemetry Collector. It redacts before data leaves the estate, fans out to more than one back end, and makes the platform of record replaceable [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why own the spine.** Ownership in this market consolidated sharply. Dynatrace completed its acquisition of Arize, covering Phoenix and AX, on 1 October 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S045: Dynatrace Completes Acquisition of Arize, Extending AI Observability Across the Full Development Lifecycle (Dynatrace, Inc.) <https://ir.dynatrace.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/439/dynatrace-completes-acquisition-of-arize-extending-ai-observability-across-the-full-development-lifecycle> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S005: Dynatrace Completes Acquisition of Arize (IR press release) (Dynatrace, Inc.) <https://ir.dynatrace.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/439/dynatrace-completes-acquisition-of-arize-extending-ai-observability-across-the-full-development-lifecycle> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. ClickHouse announced its acquisition of Langfuse on 16 January 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S021: ClickHouse raises $400M Series D, acquires Langfuse, launches Postgres (ClickHouse, Inc.) <https://clickhouse.com/blog/clickhouse-raises-400-million-series-d-acquires-langfuse-launches-postgres> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S041: ClickHouse welcomes Langfuse (with langfuse.com 'Langfuse joins ClickHouse', Orrick) <https://clickhouse.com/blog/clickhouse-acquires-langfuse-open-source-llm-observability> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. OpenAI announced its acquisition of Promptfoo on 9 March 2026, with no closing date published [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S024: OpenAI to acquire Promptfoo <https://openai.com/index/openai-to-acquire-promptfoo/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S006: OpenAI to acquire Promptfoo <https://openai.com/blog/openai-to-acquire-promptfoo> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. W&B Weave has been part of CoreWeave since 5 May 2025 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S131: CoreWeave Completes Acquisition of Weights & Biases (CoreWeave, Inc.) <https://investors.coreweave.com/news/news-details/2025/CoreWeave-Completes-Acquisition-of-Weights--Biases/default.aspx> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The new owners are an APM vendor, a database vendor, a model vendor and a GPU cloud [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. None of this makes the products worse. It does mean the roadmap of the tool holding your evidence may change within a contract term, which is why the instrumentation, datasets and metric code should live in the firm's repository and the products should sit on top as replaceable back ends [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The model-vendor case deserves a separate word. A test tool owned by a model vendor is a weaker basis for the independent challenge that validation expects when the model under test is that vendor's [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The same rule applies to any vendor's tooling, including an Anthropic-owned evaluation tool; the author of this review is an Anthropic model, and the rule is stated for that reason [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The architectural answer is two red-team tools in CI, one independent of any model vendor [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Confident AI's red-teaming, from DeepEval's maintainer, is one independent option [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S068: DeepEval README (Confident AI (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/confident-ai/deepeval/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07].

**Numbers are not a matter of opinion.** In the review's worked example, a code scorer extracts every figure, sign and direction word ("added", "detracted", "overweight") from a draft and compares it with the attribution-engine output recorded in the same trace; any mismatch outside the rounding rule fails the run, and no language-model judge is involved [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Judges have a proper role in groundedness and tone, but only once calibrated against expert labels [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Retention is a design decision.** Free tiers keep data for 15 days on Arize AX, 30 days on Langfuse's hobby tier and 60 days on Opik [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S047: Arize AX Pricing: AI agent observability and evaluation (Arize AI) <https://arize.com/pricing/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A1-S031: Pricing - Langfuse (Langfuse (ClickHouse)) <https://langfuse.com/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A1-S123: Comet pricing (Opik plans) (Comet ML, Inc.) <https://www.comet.com/site/pricing/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. A firm's records policy, not a vendor tier, should set retention [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The regulated reading.** PRA SS1/23 covers vendor models and requires independent validation and ongoing performance monitoring [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S008: SS1/23 Model risk management principles for banks (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2023/may/model-risk-management-principles-for-banks-ss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S037: Bank of England/PRA letter to DSIT and HMT on AI (2024) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/letter/2024/dsit-hmt-letter.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. SR 26-2, which superseded SR 11-7 on 17 April 2026, places generative and agentic AI outside its scope [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S002: OCC Bulletin 2026-13 Model Risk Management: Revised Guidance (attachment bulletin-2026-13a.pdf) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/bulletins/2026/bulletin-2026-13.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. No regulator defines adequate evaluation for an LLM agent, so the firm sets its own metrics, thresholds and re-validation triggers [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. IOSCO's supervisory toolkit names indicators that include the level and frequency of human intervention in AI-driven investment processes [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-INTL-AI-ASSETMGMT: IOSCO Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (FR/02/2026) and ESMA Public Statement on AI and investment services (30 May 2024) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S058: IOSCO FR/02/2026 Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (May 2026) <https://www.iosco.org/library/pubdocs/pdf/IOSCOPD823.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. This layer is where that evidence is produced [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The practical pattern.** One platform of record for traces and evaluations, chosen to fit the estate: self-hosted Langfuse, MLflow where an ML platform already exists, or LangSmith for LangGraph estates [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Two CI red-team tools. A collector the firm controls [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is **numeric faithfulness**: the share of figures in a generated draft that match the authoritative source value under an agreed rounding rule [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Measure it with a deterministic comparator that reads the draft and the tool output recorded in the same trace, and run it on every release and every change of model, prompt, index or judge [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The target is 100% at release, and any miss blocks [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That is a starting point for use cases whose outputs carry figures, not a universal benchmark; a use case without numbers needs a different lead metric.

Three companion measures make the plane trustworthy [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Trace completeness, requests with a full trace reconciled against gateway logs, should start at 99.9% or better for in-scope systems. Judge–human agreement should reach a Cohen's kappa of about 0.7 before any judge gates a release. And time to detect a regression, from a change to a failing evaluation, should be the same day for gated systems [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 17: evaluation.** The evaluation plane: numeric faithfulness (blocking), groundedness, style, trajectory, and a regression suite of 24 to 36 months of approved commentaries, all on one firm-owned telemetry spine. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** Every change of model, prompt, index or judge is tested before it reaches a client. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** Any numeric miss blocks; no human is asked to catch what code can check. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** Eval results for every draft and every release. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Numeric faithfulness: 100%. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"We will add evaluation once the pilot proves value."** That is the ordering the post warns against. Evaluations added after go-live measure the damage, not the quality [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The review's illustrative scenario has a minor model change alter how effects are rounded and described, and three monthly cycles pass before anyone notices, with no recorded prompt version and traces kept for only 15 days [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The pilot is the cheapest point to build a regression suite, because the cases are few and fresh [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It is also the evidence that decides whether the pilot proved value at all; without it, the decision rests on impressions from a handful of demonstrations [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"The telemetry conventions are still in development. We should wait."** They are, and they recently moved repository [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S058: Semantic conventions for generative AI systems (status: Development) (OpenTelemetry (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-telemetry/semantic-conventions-genai/main/docs/gen-ai/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A1-S060: OpenTelemetry semantic conventions CHANGELOG (v1.42.0 GenAI move) (OpenTelemetry (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-telemetry/semantic-conventions/main/CHANGELOG.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. Waiting does not reduce that risk; it moves it onto every team that instruments in its own way meanwhile [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Pin the convention version in the firm's collector, keep OpenInference as a portable alternative, and translate at the collector if the standard shifts [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"A language-model judge is good enough and cheaper than code."** For tone and some groundedness checks, a calibrated judge is the right tool. For numbers it is the wrong one: a judge can score a draft "faithful" whose figures fail a deterministic check [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The comparator is modest code. The judge model is the main evaluation cost driver, so routing numbers away from it saves money as well [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Judges also drift when their own model changes, so pin and re-calibrate them like any other dependency [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- For each live GenAI system, could we name the dataset, metric code and judge version behind its last release decision? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Does any of our evidence live only inside a vendor tool, or on a tier with a fixed retention period? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Is our instrumentation on an open standard through a collector we control, or hard-wired to one vendor's SDK? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which of our red-team tools is independent of the vendor whose model it tests? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Do any figures in our generated outputs pass on a judge's score rather than a deterministic check? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- How long would it take to say which outputs were affected if a model change introduced an error last quarter? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

Build the evaluation harness first, own the evidence it produces, and let the products on top come and go. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}


## 18. Your eval suite is your validation evidence

*C8 Model risk, governance and audit, from Post 18 of the series.*

### The post

In April, the US supervisory guidance that had shaped model risk management since 2011 was replaced. Its successor, SR 26-2, places generative and agentic AI expressly outside its scope. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S002: OCC Bulletin 2026-13 Model Risk Management: Revised Guidance (attachment bulletin-2026-13a.pdf) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/bulletins/2026/bulletin-2026-13.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]

It is tempting to read that as relief. It is the opposite. The governance burden has not gone; it has moved to the firm. Each firm now has to write its own standard for LLM systems, and the UK's SS1/23, which is technology-agnostic and covers vendor models, is the most complete benchmark to write it against. [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S008: SS1/23 Model risk management principles for banks (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2023/may/model-risk-management-principles-for-banks-ss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Here is what makes it tractable. Much of the validation evidence already exists if the evaluation work is done properly. The eval suite is the validation evidence, on three conditions: someone independent of the developers challenges and extends it; it is versioned with the results it produced; and it is kept beyond any vendor's retention tier. A developer's test suite on its own is development testing, not validation. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal worth putting in front of a risk committee is validation currency: the share of material use cases whose validation covers the model, prompt, index and tool versions running today. Below 100% means an approval that no longer describes production. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is choosing the governed unit. Not the model — the use case, with the foundation model recorded as a vendor component and every version pinned. That one decision makes the inventory, re-validation triggers and audit evidence line up. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: on public evidence, no governance platform yet does this end to end. The inventory schema and the evidence store are things a firm owns, whatever it buys. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Regulation will catch up. The evidence you keep in the meantime is what it will ask for. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

![Your eval suite is your validation evidence](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P18.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 18. One evidence pack per approved commentary, keyed to one trace ID and kept in the firm's own archive. Generic, illustrative worked example.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

This control answers the questions a regulator, auditor or board will ask of any generative AI system. What is it, and who owns it? Was it independently validated for this use? Is it still performing? Who approved this output, on what evidence? Can we reproduce what happened [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}? The jobs group into four: know (inventory, classification, lineage), assure (validation, explainability, monitoring), decide (policy, approval, change control) and prove (evidence, retained under the records policy) [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Most of the evidence is produced elsewhere, by evaluation (Chapter 17), the gateway, prompt versioning and security testing. This control governs it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What changed in the United States.** SR 26-2, with OCC Bulletin 2026-13 and FDIC FIL-15-2026, was issued on 17 April 2026 and superseded SR 11-7 and SR 21-8 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S002: OCC Bulletin 2026-13 Model Risk Management: Revised Guidance (attachment bulletin-2026-13a.pdf) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/bulletins/2026/bulletin-2026-13.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S005: Federal Banking Agencies Issue Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (Sullivan & Cromwell LLP (also Orrick, Davis Polk memos)) <https://www.sullcrom.com/insights/memo/2026/April/OCC-Fed-FDIC-Issue-Revised-Guidance-Model-Risk-Management> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S049: Supervisory Letter SR 26-2 on Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management -- April 17, 2026 (with SR2602a1 attachment, OCC Bulletin 2026-13a) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. It keeps the familiar disciplines for models in its scope: risk-based, materiality-driven management and effective challenge by qualified, independent reviewers [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S002: OCC Bulletin 2026-13 Model Risk Management: Revised Guidance (attachment bulletin-2026-13a.pdf) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/bulletins/2026/bulletin-2026-13.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. It places generative and agentic AI expressly outside that scope, and says the firm's own risk-management practices should determine their governance [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S002: OCC Bulletin 2026-13 Model Risk Management: Revised Guidance (attachment bulletin-2026-13a.pdf) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/bulletins/2026/bulletin-2026-13.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The agencies said they plan a request for information on model risk and AI; none had been published as of 7 October 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-US-AGENCY-AI: US federal banking agency and NIST statements on generative and agentic AI, 2026 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S003: OCC Issues Updated Model Risk Management Guidance (NR 2026-29) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/news-releases/2026/nr-occ-2026-29.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S007: After SR 26-2: Governing Generative and Agentic AI in Banks (and related practitioner summaries) (Lumenova AI / practitioner commentary) <https://www.lumenova.ai/blog/sr-26-2-control-framework/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The carve-out defers the question rather than answering it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A firm that writes its own standard now, to a high enough quality, is unlikely to have to rebuild it when expectations return [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The UK benchmark.** PRA SS1/23 took effect on 17 May 2024. It sets five principles: model identification and classification, governance, development and use, independent validation, and risk mitigants [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S008: SS1/23 Model risk management principles for banks (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2023/may/model-risk-management-principles-for-banks-ss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S037: Bank of England/PRA letter to DSIT and HMT on AI (2024) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/letter/2024/dsit-hmt-letter.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. It is technology-agnostic and covers vendor models [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S008: SS1/23 Model risk management principles for banks (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2023/may/model-risk-management-principles-for-banks-ss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Its formal scope is banks, building societies and PRA-designated investment firms with internal-model approval [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S008: SS1/23 Model risk management principles for banks (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2023/may/model-risk-management-principles-for-banks-ss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S061: SS1/23 model definition and Principle 1.1(b); PS6/23 feedback (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/supervisory-statement/2023/ss123.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], so it does not bind most FCA solo-regulated asset managers [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It is still the most complete benchmark, and bank-affiliated managers will inherit it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Members of the Bank of England's AI Consortium report that generative AI is "often classified as high risk" under SS1/23-type frameworks [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S010: Artificial Intelligence Consortium minutes, 3 June 2026 (Bank of England) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/minutes/2026/june/ai-consortium-minutes-3-june-2026> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The FCA has made no AI-specific rules and relies on existing frameworks such as Consumer Duty and SM&CR [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-UK-AI-STATEMENTS: FCA, Bank of England and PRA statements and initiatives on AI, 2024-2026 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S055: FCA announces second cohort for AI Live Testing (April 2026); FS25/5; Mills Review (Financial Conduct Authority) <https://www.fca.org.uk/news/press-releases/fca-announces-second-cohort-ai-live-testing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], which makes a named senior manager accountable for AI use the operative UK lever [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The EU timetable.** The AI Act's obligations for general-purpose AI became enforceable on 2 August 2026, and the Annex III high-risk duties moved to 2 December 2027 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-EU-OMNIBUS-AI: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 ('Digital Omnibus on AI') amending Regulations (EU) 2024/1689, 2018/1139 and 2023/1230 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S011: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) amending Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (EUR-Lex / Official Journal of the EU) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S019: General-Purpose AI Models in the AI Act - Questions & Answers (European Commission) <https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/faqs/general-purpose-ai-models-ai-act-questions-answers> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Most asset-management uses, including the attribution commentary in this book's worked example, are not Annex III [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Is an LLM a "model"?** The review's answer is practical [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A foundation model is a model by construction. An agent is a system that contains one, wrapped in orchestration, tools and prompts. SS1/23's Principle 1.1(b) already lets firms apply model-risk disciplines to material, complex deterministic methods that are not models [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S061: SS1/23 model definition and Principle 1.1(b); PS6/23 feedback (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/supervisory-statement/2023/ss123.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], which is the cleanest UK hook for governing the whole agent [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. So the governed unit is the use case. Its record binds the foundation model and exact version, the fallback model, the prompt template, the retrieval index and embedding model, the tools and their permissions, the guardrail configuration, the evaluation suite and the judge model [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A change to any of them is a change to the governed system, and triggers re-validation [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That framing works under all three regimes: SS1/23, a firm's own standard under SR 26-2's carve-out, and the AI Act's classification by intended purpose [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**When an eval suite becomes validation.** The same versioned datasets and checks that gate releases are what an independent validator re-performs and what the archive retains [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. In the worked example, the validator first asks whether a language model is appropriate when every number comes from the attribution engine. The validator then re-runs a regression suite of 24 to 36 months of approved commentaries, adds challenge cases such as sign flips, overweight and underweight swaps and injected market notes, and runs a second red-team tool independent of the model vendor [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. For a language model, reproducibility means re-performance of the evidence, not bit-identical text: the inputs, output and check results are retained, and a re-run on the pinned model passes the same thresholds [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The evidence pack.** Each approved output gets one pack, written automatically to the firm's immutable archive at approval and keyed by trace ID [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It holds eight things: the prompt version, the exact model version the gateway returned rather than the alias requested, the data snapshot, the tool calls and identities used, the evaluation results with their thresholds, the approver's authenticated identity and edit, the timestamps, and the final text with its hash [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Retention follows the records policy for client communications, not a vendor tier [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The market.** The vendors are converging on "AI control plane" positioning. Collibra announced its acquisition of trail ML on 5 October 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S101: Collibra acquires trail ML to automate AI governance from policy to production <https://www.collibra.com/company/newsroom/press-releases/collibra-acquires-trail-ml-to-automate-ai-governance-from-policy-to-production> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S105: Collibra acquires trail ML to automate AI governance from policy to production (PR Newswire) <https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/collibra-acquires-trail-ml-to-automate-ai-governance-from-policy-to-production-302897376.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S044: Collibra acquires Munich's trail ML to automate AI governance (with unite.ai, citybiz) (The Next Web) <https://thenextweb.com/news/collibra-trail-ml-eu-ai-act-market> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. IBM added agent Enforcement Tracking to watsonx.governance on 11 August 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S111: IBM announcements 2026: Enforcement Tracking; AI Asset Discovery; security metrics and agent monitoring; Think 2026 AI assurance <https://www.ibm.com/new/announcements/from-governance-policies-to-governance-proof-with-enforcement-tracking-for-watsonx-orchestrate> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Only one vendor assessed, ValidMind, claims a mapping to SS1/23 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S052: ValidMind docs: SS1/23 use case <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/validmind/documentation/main/site/about/use-cases/ss1-23.qmd> accessed 2026-10-07]. Some vendor mappings still point to SR 11-7, a superseded instrument [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S103: IBM watsonx.governance product, pricing and model-governance pages; Compliance Accelerators announcement; IBM Cloud catalog <https://www.ibm.com/products/watsonx-governance/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. No governance platform reaches the review's Strategic tier on public evidence, mainly because product-scoped certification evidence is thin [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The recommendation is to own the inventory schema and an immutable evidence store, use OpenLineage for data lineage, and then pick a workflow tool to fit the estate: ValidMind for model-risk-led banks, watsonx.governance for IBM estates, Collibra where it is already the data catalogue, or Credo AI for policy-led programmes [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The trade-off.** Governing the use case with every version pinned means more re-validations, not fewer. The answer is tiering by materiality, so a tier-3 internal summariser does not need the depth of a client-facing commentary, and automation, so evidence is collected by construction rather than by request [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is **validation currency**: the share of material use cases whose validation covers the model, prompt, index and tool versions running in production today [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Measure it by reconciling the inventory's validated version bundle against the change log and the gateway's record of model versions actually called [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The target is 100% before go-live, with re-validation completed within an agreed window after any trigger [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That is a starting point; the window itself should be set by tier, measured in days rather than months for the most material use cases [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Three companion measures make the number credible [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Version pinning, the share of material use cases calling a pinned model version rather than an alias, should be 100%. Evidence-pack completeness, approved outputs with prompt version, model version, data snapshot, tool calls, evaluation results, approver and timestamps, should be 100%. Reproducibility, tested quarterly by re-performing a sample of past outputs from their packs, should pass on the whole sample [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 18: governance.** The inventory entry for the use case and an evidence pack per approved commentary, keyed by one trace ID, written to an immutable archive. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** Any approved commentary can be explained and re-performed on request. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** The governed unit is the use case; the model is a vendor component inside it. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** The complete eight-part evidence pack. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Validation currency: 100%. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"SR 26-2 excludes generative AI, so model risk management does not apply."** The guidance excludes it from its own scope and hands its governance to the firm's own practices [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A8-S002: OCC Bulletin 2026-13 Model Risk Management: Revised Guidance (attachment bulletin-2026-13a.pdf) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/bulletins/2026/bulletin-2026-13.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. That is a transfer of the burden, not a removal. Other supervisors still expect monitoring and records: ESMA expects regular AI model testing and ex-post output controls [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-INTL-AI-ASSETMGMT: IOSCO Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (FR/02/2026) and ESMA Public Statement on AI and investment services (30 May 2024) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S059: ESMA Public Statement on AI and investment services (30 May 2024) and 2026 briefings <https://www.esma.europa.eu/document/public-statement-ai-and-investment-services> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. And the agencies' planned request for information may reintroduce expectations [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S003: OCC Issues Updated Model Risk Management Guidance (NR 2026-29) (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency) <https://www.occ.gov/news-issuances/news-releases/2026/nr-occ-2026-29.html> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Treating every use case like a model will slow delivery to a halt."** Not if it is tiered. SR 26-2 itself is explicitly risk-based and materiality-driven for the models in its scope [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-US-MRM: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management: Federal Reserve SR 26-2 / OCC Bulletin 2026-13 / FDIC FIL-15-2026 (supersedes SR 11-7 and SR 21-8) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S001: SR 26-2: Revised Guidance on Model Risk Management (17 April 2026) (Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System) <https://www.federalreserve.gov/supervisionreg/srletters/SR2602.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], and the same logic suits a firm's own standard [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Most of the cost is evidence collection, and that should be automatic: the gateway, trace store, prompt registry and approval gate write to the evidence pack under one trace ID [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"We will buy a governance platform and be covered."** A platform helps with workflow, intake and attestation. It is not the evidence. Every regulatory mapping found in these products is a vendor claim, and a policy pack is a starting checklist, not proof of compliance [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Keep the inventory schema and evidence store in firm-controlled formats, so the tool can change hands, as several have, without taking the record with it [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Due diligence matters too: on public evidence, security was capped for four of the five workflow tools assessed because their certification evidence could not be confirmed, which is a question to resolve before any risk records are loaded [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- Which unit do we govern today: the model, the application or the use case? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- For our most material GenAI use case, does the validation report name the model, prompt, index and tool versions running now? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Does any production call go to a model alias rather than a pinned version? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Who outside the development team has challenged and extended our evaluation suite? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Where does our evidence live, and would it survive the end of a vendor contract? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which senior manager is accountable for AI use in client communications? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Have we written our own GenAI model-risk standard, and to which benchmark? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

Govern the use case, pin every version, and keep the evidence yourself, because that is what any future rule will ask to see. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}


# Part III: Putting it together


## 19. What the agent must never do matters more

*Putting it together: the worked example, end to end, from Post 19 of the series.*

### The post

One example has run underneath this series: an agent that drafts the monthly performance-attribution commentary for a multi-asset fund, with a portfolio manager approving every draft. It is generic and illustrative. Here it is in one place. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

What it may do is short. Draft narrative around figures it is given. Explain allocation, selection and currency effects in the house style. Cite approved market context. Propose wording, and propose new style rules for a person to approve. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

What it must never do is longer, and matters more. Never generate, round or "correct" a number. Never treat a figure from retrieved text, memory or a parsed document as data. Never present inference as source. Never publish without a recorded human approval. Never call a write, email or web tool. Never send client identifiers outside the approved route. Never run on an unpinned model. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The useful discipline is that every "never" names the component that enforces it. Read-only tools and a deterministic numeric check stop invented figures. A workflow with no outbound tool stops exfiltration. An approval step that only a named person can release stops unreviewed publication. A "never" without an enforcing component is a hope written into a policy. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal is evidence-pack completeness: the share of approved commentaries with a complete record of prompt version, model version, data snapshot, tool calls, evaluation results, approver, timestamps and final text. The target is 100%. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to write the "never" list first, and let it shape the design. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: this design gives up flexibility on purpose. Each new capability should arrive with its own enforcing control, not a broader prompt. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Capability is what a system can do. Trust comes from what it provably cannot. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

![What the agent must never do matters more](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P19.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 19. Every "never" names the component that enforces it, and every approved draft leaves a complete evidence pack. Generic, illustrative: the attribution-commentary agent.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

The worked example is deliberately ordinary. An agent drafts the monthly performance-attribution commentary for a generic multi-asset fund: Brinson-style allocation and selection effects, currency, and benchmark-relative return. A portfolio manager reviews and approves every draft before release [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It is illustrative and cloud-neutral, not a description of any firm's platform [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The architectural reading.** Strip away the word "agent" and this is a deterministic workflow, not a free agent [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Every number comes from the attribution engine through a read-only tool. The model's only job is to write narrative around figures it is given. A deterministic check proves that every figure in the draft matches the engine. A named person approves [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The language model is therefore governed as a model-dependent use case under the firm's own standard (Chapter 18), and the use case is not an EU AI Act Annex III use [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Article 50 is assessed: it requires disclosure of AI-generated text published to inform the public on matters of public interest, unless the text has been human-reviewed under editorial responsibility [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S018: AI Act Article 50 Transparency obligations; Commission Article 50 guidelines C(2026) 5054 (European Commission) <https://ai-act-service-desk.ec.europa.eu/en/ai-act/article-50> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S078: Not Delayed, Not Deferred: EU AI Act Transparency Obligations Are Now in Force (Goodwin; with Orrick, Freshfields on final Digital Omnibus) (Goodwin Procter) <https://www.goodwinlaw.com/en/insights/publications/2026/08/alerts-technology-dpc-eu-ai-act-transparency-obligations-now-in-force> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Here, human editorial review applies [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**One request, end to end.** The review traces a single run through every layer and control [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Before anything runs, a release manifest pins the prompt set, the drafting model at a dated version, a fallback model, the embedding and reranker versions, the read-only tool list, the guardrail policy and the evaluation thresholds. An analyst signs in, and the agent's own registered identity obtains a short-lived, read-only token to act on the analyst's behalf. The attribution results arrive through a tool gateway that checks an allow-list and the analyst's entitlements, and the snapshot's hash is recorded. Retrieval searches only approved prior commentaries, the style guide and approved market notes, filtered by entitlement, and every chunk carries its source, version and parse manifest (Chapter 15). Retrieved chunks are screened for hidden instructions. Client identifiers are tokenised (Chapter 16). One drafting step runs through the gateway to an in-region primary model, with a qualified fallback from a different vendor; if both are unavailable, it fails closed [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Then the checks. A deterministic comparator tests every figure, sign and direction word against the snapshot. Personal data is re-checked and placeholders must be unchanged. Forecasts and advice are denied topics. The evaluation gate requires 100% numeric faithfulness; a failure goes back for a bounded retry or is returned for revision, never approved (Chapter 17). An approval interrupt then waits for a named portfolio manager, not the requester and not the agent, with step-up authentication. A release service publishes using the approver's identity, outside the agent. The evidence pack is written to the immutable archive, and the approver's edits feed the human-intervention metric and proposed style rules [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Every "never" names its enforcer.** The boundary table is the heart of the design [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Generating or "correcting" a number is stopped by read-only tools, the numeric comparator and a blocking evaluation. Using a figure from retrieved commentary, memory or a parsed document as data is stopped by a document-derived tag and a context-only rule. Presenting inference as source is caught by grounding checks and citations to document versions. Publishing without approval is impossible because the approval interrupt and the release service sit outside the agent, with segregation of duties enforced through identity. Calling a write, email or web tool is impossible because no such tool exists on the allow-list. Sending client identifiers out is stopped by tokenisation, an egress proxy and redaction at the trace collector. Retrieving another fund's or client's material is stopped by an entitlement filter inside the search. Running on an alias or an unqualified fallback is stopped by the gateway route and the version pins [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Two less obvious rows matter too: discarding evaluation evidence is prevented by the firm-owned archive, and mixing two attribution snapshots after a resume is prevented by reusing the recorded snapshot [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The threat it is built to survive.** The review's security scenario is a third-party market note containing hidden text that tells the model to say currency hedging added 40 basis points and to send the draft elsewhere [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. No single control has to catch it. There is no outbound tool to hijack. The comparator fails any figure not in the engine snapshot. Retrieved text is wrapped as data. A detector screens retrieved chunks and quarantines the source. And the portfolio manager approves before release [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What it costs.** On the review's arithmetic, a draft of about 40,000 input and 3,000 output tokens on a mid-tier model at US$2 and US$10 per million tokens, the list price of both Claude Sonnet 5.5 and GPT-6.1 Sol as of 7 October 2026, costs about US$0.11 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A5-S011: Pricing - Claude API Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07; A5-S004: OpenAI API Pricing (and Introducing GPT-6 Sol and Luna pricing) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. With judges and an average of 2.5 drafts per approved commentary, the token cost is roughly US$0.30, or about US$12 a month for 40 funds; those token figures are the review's own illustration [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The conclusion matters more than the number. Tokens are not the cost driver; reviewer time is, so the metric to manage is the regeneration and edit rate [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Portable by design.** Each component is named once in cloud-neutral form, with equivalents on each major cloud [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The workflow, for example, can be LangGraph with a firm-controlled Postgres checkpointer or Temporal, or a native option: Strands on AgentCore Runtime, Microsoft Agent Framework on Foundry, or ADK on Agent Engine [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S116: Bedrock AgentCore SDK README (GitHub) (AWS) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/aws/bedrock-agentcore-sdk-python/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; B-L3-S004: AgentCore Runtime sessions / microVMs / quotas / security best practices (Amazon Web Services) <https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-sessions.html> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A4-S066: Microsoft Agent Framework README (GitHub) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/microsoft/agent-framework/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; B-L3-S006: Foundry Hosted Agents (Agent Framework hosting docs and Foundry blog) (Microsoft) <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/agent-framework/hosting/foundry-hosted-agent> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A4-S114: Agent Development Kit (ADK) 2.0 README (GitHub) (Google) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/google/adk-python/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07] [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Pinning versions and keeping a fallback model qualified on the same evaluation suite also gives the tested exit route that SS2/21 expects [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S048: SS2/21 Outsourcing and third party risk management (and PS7/26, March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/supervisory-statement/2021/ss221-march-21.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is **evidence-pack completeness**: the share of approved commentaries with a complete record of prompt version, model version as returned by the gateway, data snapshot, tool calls, evaluation results, approver identity and edit, timestamps, and final text [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Measure it with an automated check at release that refuses to publish without a complete pack, and confirm it with a periodic sample audit [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The target is 100% of approved commentaries [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Treat it as a starting point for any client-facing use case, not as an industry benchmark.

Two companion measures show whether the pack is useful as well as complete [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Reproducibility, tested quarterly by re-performing a sample of past commentaries from their packs, should pass on the whole sample. The human-intervention rate, the share of drafts edited or rejected and the size of the edits, should be tracked as a trend; it is both the main cost driver and an indicator supervisors have named [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-INTL-AI-ASSETMGMT: IOSCO Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (FR/02/2026) and ESMA Public Statement on AI and investment services (30 May 2024) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S058: IOSCO FR/02/2026 Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (May 2026) <https://www.iosco.org/library/pubdocs/pdf/IOSCOPD823.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]] [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 19: assembled.** All eighteen pieces assembled into one request trace, with a 'may / must never' list in which every 'never' names the component that enforces it. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** The design is complete end to end on paper. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** Every 'must never' has an enforcing component, not a policy sentence. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** The eight-tile evidence pack, keyed to one trace ID. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Evidence-pack completeness: 100%. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"This is too constrained to be called an agent."** That is the point, and the post says so. The design gives up flexibility on purpose [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A client-facing regulated communication is the wrong place to discover what an open-ended agent does with a write tool. If more capability is wanted later, it should arrive with its own enforcing control and its own evaluation cases, not as a broader prompt [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Autonomy is easier to extend from a pinned, well-evidenced baseline than to claw back from a system nobody can fully describe [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"A person approves every draft, so the other controls are redundant."** The approver is the last line, not the only one. People reviewing dozens of fluent drafts at month-end check what they expect to check; the review's ingestion scenario has a reviewer verify the current month's numbers but miss a prior-period figure quoted from a mis-parsed factsheet [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The comparator, the context-only rule and the evidence pack make the approver's job tractable, and make the approval mean something [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Our use case is not attribution commentary."** The specifics will differ. The method transfers [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Write a short "may" list and a longer "never" list, put an enforcing component against every "never", and define the evidence pack before the first line of code. A "never" with no component is the place to start the design conversation [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It is also usually the cheapest conversation the team will have: a boundary agreed before build costs a meeting, while one discovered in production costs a re-review of everything already released [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- For our leading GenAI use case, is there a written "never" list, and does each line name its enforcing component? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Can the model in that use case produce a number that reaches a client without a deterministic check? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Is there any tool on the agent's allow-list that can write, send or browse? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Who can release an output, and is that identity separate from the requester and the agent? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Could we produce a complete evidence pack for an output approved six months ago? [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- What is our edit and regeneration rate, and do we treat it as a cost and quality signal? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### In one line

Decide what the system must never do, name the component that stops it, and keep the evidence that it did not. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}


## 20. The most valuable page: "do not build yet"

*Putting it together: Stack D, minimal start-small, from Post 20 of the series.*

### The post

The most valuable page in a reference architecture is often the "do not build yet" list.

Reference architectures tend to be read as shopping lists. Every box looks like a work package, and a programme that funds them all spends its first year building platform that nobody is using yet. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The right minimum is not a weaker stack. It is the full regulated design with everything the first use case does not need removed. One gateway, deployed twice, with one route and a budget that fails closed. An agent identity acting on the analyst's behalf with read-only scope. A configuration manifest in source control with a second approver. Deterministic output checks and identifier protection. The evaluation suite. One retrieval store already in use. One workflow with one approval step. Two model vendors qualified from day one, because exit must be real. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Then the list of what waits: long-term memory, autonomous agents, agent-to-agent delegation, web search, code sandboxes, a dedicated vector database, a second cloud, fine-tuning, a governance platform, a FinOps product, semantic caching, and anything still experimental. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal is gateway coverage: the share of production model calls that pass through the gateway. The target is 100%; any provider key found in application code is a defect. Without it there is no exit and no log of record. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to publish the "not yet" list with the same authority as the plan, and to revisit it only when evidence shows a real gap. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: a small first release can look unambitious. Present the controls as the deliverable, because every later use case reuses them.

Saying "not yet" clearly is how a platform earns the right to say yes later.

![The most valuable page: “do not build yet”](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P20.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 20. The minimum is the full regulated design with everything the first use case does not need removed, plus a published "not yet" list. Generic and illustrative; revisit triggers are indicative.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

The review sets out four reference stacks. Stack A, the regulated enterprise stack, is the lead; Stacks B (cloud-native managed), C (open-source first) and D (minimal start-small) are alternatives for firms whose constraints differ [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Stack D is easy to misread. It is defined as the minimum viable enterprise platform that can safely run one regulated use case, with an explicit list of what not to build, and it is Stack A with everything the first use case does not need removed [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Every control in Stack A is still present in some form; what shrinks is the number of routes, stores, tools and workflows each control has to cover [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why one use case.** A platform with no user has nothing for a validator to re-perform [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The review names the performance-attribution commentary from Chapter 19 as a good first candidate because its boundaries are clear and its numbers are checkable [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A use case like that forces every control to exist without forcing any of them to be general.

**The minimum, layer by layer.** Read in the house order, the build-now list is short, and each item has a one-line reason [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}:

- *Models and model access (L1, L2).* The primary cloud's model service, with two vendors qualified from day one. On AWS that might be Claude Sonnet 5.5 or a GPT-6 tier on Bedrock, the other or Mistral as fallback; on Azure, GPT-6.1 Sol with Mistral as fallback; on Google Cloud, Gemini 3.8 Flash with Claude as fallback and Mistral as the independent alternative [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Two vendors matter because model lives are short: a Gemini Flash version released on 13 August 2026 retires on 28 January 2027 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L1-S003: Gemini 3.7 Flash model page and related lifecycle pages (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/models/gemini/3-7-flash> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]], and Mistral Medium 3.1 retired on 31 August 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L1-S002: Models overview: deprecated and retired models (Mistral Docs) (Mistral AI) <https://docs.mistral.ai/models> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]].
- *Orchestration (L3).* One graph with one approval gate, on LangGraph with a Postgres checkpointer, or Strands on AgentCore, Microsoft Agent Framework or Google ADK where that cloud is primary [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Tools (L4).* One read-only tool onto authoritative data, and no writes. MCP is the default protocol and originated at Anthropic, so the independent alternative is an OpenAPI-described tool behind the same gateway [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Authorisation is still optional in the MCP specification [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S055: MCP Specification 2026-07-28: Authorization (Model Context Protocol project (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol/main/docs/specification/2026-07-28/basic/authorization/index.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07], which is why the gateway, not the protocol, enforces it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Ingestion and retrieval (L8 to L6).* Docling and pgvector with entitlement filters: one store, already secured [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. pgvector is managed on all three hyperscalers [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V1-S020: pgvector CHANGELOG.md (pgvector (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pgvector/pgvector/master/CHANGELOG.md> accessed 2026-10-08; B-L6-S004: Extension versions for Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL; RDS PostgreSQL release notes; Aurora pgvector announcements (Amazon Web Services) <https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/PostgreSQLReleaseNotes/postgresql-extensions.html> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; B-L6-S005: Vector search in Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server; Cloud SQL vector embeddings; AlloyDB vector search overview (Microsoft; Google Cloud) <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/postgresql/extensions/how-to-use-pgvector> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]].
- *Evaluation (L9).* A firm-owned OpenTelemetry Collector, Langfuse or MLflow, and DeepEval with Promptfoo plus one independent red-team tool [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The second tool is not a luxury: OpenAI announced its acquisition of Promptfoo on 9 March 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S024: OpenAI to acquire Promptfoo <https://openai.com/index/openai-to-acquire-promptfoo/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S006: OpenAI to acquire Promptfoo <https://openai.com/blog/openai-to-acquire-promptfoo> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]], and a red-team tool owned by a model vendor cannot be the only challenge to that vendor's model [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Gateway (C1).* One gateway, two instances, one route, budgets [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Without it there is no exit and no log of record [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Guardrails and privacy (C2, C3).* Deterministic output checks and Presidio, because numbers and client identifiers are the two real risks in this use case [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Identity (C4).* The agent registered in the workforce identity provider, acting on behalf of the analyst with read-only scope [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Entra Agent ID and Okta for AI Agents both reached general availability in 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V2-S032: Microsoft Entra releases and announcements (with What's new in Entra Agent ID; Entra blog 'Build AI agents for production with secure identities from day one';  <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/fundamentals/whats-new> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A6-S100: Okta for AI Agents is Now Generally Available; Okta announces new blueprint for the secure agentic enterprise (Okta, Inc.) <https://www.okta.com/blog/ai/okta-for-ai-agents-general-availability/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *Configuration (C5).* A Git manifest changed by pull request with a second approver; change control is the cheapest control there is [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *FinOps (C6).* A gateway key and a budget that fails closed, to stop runaway loops [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Security (C7).* No outbound tools, and pinned dependencies from a mirror; capability separation costs nothing at this size [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Governance (C8).* An inventory entry and an evidence pack in an archive the firm already runs, because that is what regulators ask for first [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Fails closed" has to be tested, not assumed.** The gateway evidence shows why the budget line carries a qualifier. LiteLLM's `max_budget` fails open when its database is unavailable, and its limits are unset by default [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S015: LiteLLM docs: MCP Overview, Agent Gateway (A2A), Caching, Budgets/Rate Limits, v1.89.0 release notes (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/docs/mcp> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Malicious LiteLLM releases 1.82.7 and 1.82.8 were published to PyPI on 24 March 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S008: Security Update: Suspected Supply Chain Incident (and Security Townhall Updates) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/blog/security-update-march-2026> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S027: LiteLLM PyPI supply chain attack (with NetSPI, Comet, Snyk/Datadog reporting) (FutureSearch) <https://futuresearch.ai/blog/litellm-pypi-supply-chain-attack/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Neither fact rules the product out; clean releases resumed from 1.83.0 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S009: v1.83.0 - Official Release (Post Supply Chain Incident) (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/release_notes/v1.83.0/v1-83-0> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Both make "fails closed" and "pinned" acceptance tests for the first release, the same tests Kong, Azure API Management or Apigee would have to pass [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The not-yet list, and why each item waits.** Stack D's list is longer than the post's. It adds MCP beyond the first read-only tool, self-hosted serving beyond a documented exit option and a runtime AI-security platform [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Each deferral has a reason in the evidence:

- *Memory.* The independent memory layer is thinning: Mem0 removed external graph stores from its open-source build, Zep deprecated its Community Edition, Letta pivoted to an agent harness and LangMem has not released since 27 October 2025 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S053: Mem0 docs: Platform vs Open Source (and OSS v2-to-v3 migration notes) <https://docs.mem0.ai/platform/platform-vs-oss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S043: Open Source: Migrating to the New Memory Algorithm; SDK changelog; Highlights (Mem0) <https://docs.mem0.ai/migration/oss-v2-to-v3> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A3-S059: getzep/zep README: Zep Cloud Examples & Integrations (Zep AI (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/getzep/zep/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S093: Letta's Next Phase (Mar 2026) <https://www.letta.com/blog/our-next-phase/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A3-S006: PyPI JSON metadata: langmem (Python Package Index (PyPI)) <https://pypi.org/pypi/langmem/json> accessed 2026-10-07].
