OWASP lists memory and context poisoning as ASI06 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L5-S001: Memory Is a Feature. It Is Also an Attack Surface (OWASP GenAI Security Project) <https://genai.owasp.org/2026/05/13/memory-is-a-feature-it-is-also-an-attack-surface/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Memory is a regulated record class, and the roadmap builds it last [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *A dedicated vector database.* Only when a load test proves the need [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Fine-tuning.* It adds a model to validate, and under Article 25 of the EU AI Act a deployer becomes a provider if it substantially modifies a high-risk system or changes an AI system's intended purpose so that it becomes high-risk [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S011: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) amending Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (EUR-Lex / Official Journal of the EU) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *A governance platform.* No governance platform reaches Strategic on public evidence, and buying one before the evidence store exists inverts the dependency [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Semantic caching.* LiteLLM's own documentation warns that it "goes badly wrong on agentic traffic" [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S015: LiteLLM docs: MCP Overview, Agent Gateway (A2A), Caching, Budgets/Rate Limits, v1.89.0 release notes (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/docs/mcp> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], and Apigee shipped an SSRF fix in its semantic-cache lookup on 30 September 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S025: Apigee release notes (all; hybrid) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/apigee/docs/release-notes> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].

The figure gives each deferred item an indicative revisit trigger: evidence, a load test, a use case or a review date [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What changed in 2025 and 2026.** Two dates make "start small" a scheduling necessity rather than a temperament. PRA PS7/26 and FCA PS26/2 require material third-party notifications from 18 March 2027 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-FCA-SYSC8: FCA Handbook SYSC 8 (Outsourcing); FG16/5 'Guidance for firms outsourcing to the cloud and other third-party IT services'; PS26/2 operational incident and third-party reporting (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S062: PS7/26 Operational incident and third-party reporting (PRA) and FCA PS26/2; revised SS2/21 (March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA; FCA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2026/march/operational-incident-and-third-party-reporting-policy-statement> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], and EU AI Act Annex III high-risk duties apply from 2 December 2027 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EU-OMNIBUS-AI: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 ('Digital Omnibus on AI') amending Regulations (EU) 2024/1689, 2018/1139 and 2023/1230 (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S011: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) amending Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (EUR-Lex / Official Journal of the EU) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. A firm that spends its first year on breadth reaches those dates with many components and no evidence; one that ships the Stack D minimum reaches them with one use case, one exit drill and one evidence trail [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The roadmap's gateway phase makes that concrete: its exit criterion is a route moved to the alternative vendor and to an open-weight route on vLLM by configuration only [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is **gateway coverage**: the share of production model, tool and agent calls that pass through the gateway of record [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The review's gateway chapter defines it with LLM, MCP and A2A traffic in scope, and treats any direct provider key in an application as a defect [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Measure it in two ways at once. First, reconcile provider usage reports with gateway logs; any usage the gateway did not see is a bypass [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Second, run secret scanning across application repositories and deployment configuration for provider keys; any key outside the gateway's secret store is a finding [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Report the two together, because each catches what the other misses.

The starting target is 100% of production calls in scope, with zero provider keys outside the gateway [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Treat it as a starting point for the first use case, not a benchmark drawn from other firms. As later use cases arrive, the number should stay at 100%; what grows is the volume behind it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A companion check is the exit drill itself: time to move a route to a pre-qualified alternative by configuration, aiming for hours rather than weeks and tested at least annually [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 20: minimum go-live.** The build order for real: governance and evaluation first, then the gateway, retrieval, the workflow and one read-only tool; memory and everything on the 'not yet' list waits for evidence. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** The agent can go live on the minimum platform. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** Nothing is built before the evidence says it is needed. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** The published 'not yet' list with a trigger for each item. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Gateway coverage of production calls: 100%. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"Two model vendors on day one is gold-plating for a pilot."** It looks that way until the first retirement notice or suspension. Model lifetimes are measured in months [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L1-S003: Gemini 3.7 Flash model page and related lifecycle pages (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/models/gemini/3-7-flash> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]], and a single proprietary vendor behind an important business service is classed as unacceptable lock-in in the review [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The cost of qualifying a second vendor is one more run of the evaluation suite the first use case needs anyway [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"If we defer memory and agents, the business will build them without us."** That risk is real. The answer is not to build them early but to publish the not-yet list with its triggers, so that a team with a genuine need has a route to bring evidence [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A deferral that nobody can challenge invites workarounds; a deferral with a stated trigger invites a proposal [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"A minimal platform will need re-platforming later."** Less than it seems. Stack D keeps every Stack A control; what grows later is coverage, not architecture [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The components most likely to change, such as the vector store or the agent runtime, sit behind thin firm-owned interfaces from the start (Chapter 22) [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Publishing a 'not yet' list will read as a lack of ambition to the board."** It can, if the list is presented as a set of refusals. Presented as a sequence, it reads differently: each deferred item has a trigger, and the roadmap shows when evidence is expected [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The controls built for the first use case are reused by every later one, so the first release is the foundation of the programme, not a pilot that will be thrown away [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Boards in regulated firms tend to recognise that argument, because it is the same one they apply to any other critical infrastructure [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- If we published our "not yet" list today, what would be on it, and who has authority to take an item off it? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- What share of our production model calls passes through one gateway, and how do we know?
- Where in our code or configuration do provider keys still live?
- Is our second model vendor qualified on the same evaluation suite, or only contracted?
- Have we tested that our budgets and guards fail closed when their dependencies are down?
- Which first use case has clear boundaries and checkable outputs?
- Are we presenting the controls as the deliverable, or the features?

### In one line

Start with the full regulated design for one use case, publish what waits, and let evidence, not enthusiasm, move items off the list.


## 21. Build where you differentiate, buy where you maintain

*Putting it together: build, buy or hybrid across L1–L9 and C1–C8, from Post 21 of the series.*

### The post

Build where you differentiate. Buy where you would only be maintaining.

For an asset manager adopting GenAI, very little of the stack differentiates. Training a foundation model is out of scope. Gateways, parsers, vector search, identity providers and secrets brokers are commodity infrastructure with a heavy security and maintenance burden. Buy them, or adopt open source. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

What the firm should build is smaller and more important: whatever is its control statement, its evidence or its domain logic. Route definitions and fallback lists. The guardrail policy and its test sets. The numeric check against the attribution engine, which no vendor sells. The evaluation datasets. The release manifest. The evidence store. Read-only tools onto its own systems. The workflows. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Most of the rest lands in a third column: hybrid, meaning a bought or open-source engine behind a firm-owned interface and policy. That column has grown, because so many "neutral" products have changed owner. When a product may be replaced within the planning horizon, the interface and the data around it must already be yours. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal is configuration coverage: the share of production calls whose prompt, model, tool and retrieval settings resolve to an approved version the firm holds. The target is 100% for regulated outputs. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is one build, buy or hybrid decision per component, plus one explicit "do not build yet": fine-tuning, which adds a model to validate and can, in some cases, turn a deployer into a provider under the EU AI Act. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"} [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S011: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) amending Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (EUR-Lex / Official Journal of the EU) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]

The honest caveat: hybrid is the most demanding column. It only pays if the interface stays thin and nobody starts rebuilding the product behind it. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Owning the right small things is what makes everything else replaceable.

![Build where you differentiate, buy where you maintain](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P21.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 21. One decision per component: the firm builds its control statement, evidence and domain logic, and keeps the interface around anything it buys. Generic: no vendor names.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

**The rule.** The review states it in one sentence: build where the capability is the firm's control statement, evidence or domain logic; buy where it is commodity infrastructure with a heavy security, compliance or maintenance burden; and use a hybrid, an open-source or bought engine behind a firm-owned interface and policy, wherever a product may be replaced within the planning horizon [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The test is not "could we build it?" Almost anything in the stack could be built. The test is whether building it creates something only the firm can hold to account [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why the hybrid column grew.** A year ago many teams could treat a well-regarded open-source or independent product as neutral ground. The 2026 evidence makes that harder. Dynatrace completed its acquisition of Arize on 1 October 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S045: Dynatrace Completes Acquisition of Arize, Extending AI Observability Across the Full Development Lifecycle (Dynatrace, Inc.) <https://ir.dynatrace.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/439/dynatrace-completes-acquisition-of-arize-extending-ai-observability-across-the-full-development-lifecycle> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S005: Dynatrace Completes Acquisition of Arize (IR press release) (Dynatrace, Inc.) <https://ir.dynatrace.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/439/dynatrace-completes-acquisition-of-arize-extending-ai-observability-across-the-full-development-lifecycle> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. ClickHouse announced it had acquired Langfuse on 16 January 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S021: ClickHouse raises $400M Series D, acquires Langfuse, launches Postgres (ClickHouse, Inc.) <https://clickhouse.com/blog/clickhouse-raises-400-million-series-d-acquires-langfuse-launches-postgres> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S041: ClickHouse welcomes Langfuse (with langfuse.com 'Langfuse joins ClickHouse', Orrick) <https://clickhouse.com/blog/clickhouse-acquires-langfuse-open-source-llm-observability> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. OpenAI announced its acquisition of Promptfoo on 9 March 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S024: OpenAI to acquire Promptfoo <https://openai.com/index/openai-to-acquire-promptfoo/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S006: OpenAI to acquire Promptfoo <https://openai.com/blog/openai-to-acquire-promptfoo> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Palo Alto Networks bought Portkey, Check Point bought Lakera, Harvey bought Guardrails AI, and Mintlify bought Helicone [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S011: Palo Alto Networks Completes Acquisition of Portkey to Secure AI Agents (and 30 Apr 2026 announcement) <https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/company/press/2026/palo-alto-networks-completes-acquisition-of-portkey-to-secure-ai-agents> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S025: Palo Alto Networks Completes Acquisition of Portkey to Secure AI Agents (with PANW FY2026 10-K and 10-Q, SDxCentral) <https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/company/press/2026/palo-alto-networks-completes-acquisition-of-portkey-to-secure-ai-agents> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A7-S012: Check Point to Acquire Lakera, Redefining Security for the AI era (Check Point Software) <https://blog.checkpoint.com/security/check-point-to-acquire-lakera-redefining-security-for-the-ai-era/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S038: Check Point Software Reports 2025 Third Quarter Financial Results (with 'Check Point Completes Acquisition of Lakera' release via cxotoday) (Check Point Software Technologies) <https://www.checkpoint.com/press-releases/check-point-software-reports-2025-third-quarter-financial-results/> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A6-S028: Guardrails AI Joins Harvey <https://www.harvey.ai/blog/guardrails-ai-joins-harvey> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S112: Helicone Blog: Helicone acquired by Mintlify (and Mintlify announcement) (Helicone / Mintlify) <https://www.helicone.ai/blog> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V2-S043: Helicone Joins Mintlify (with mintlify.com 'Mintlify acquires Helicone'; PostHog comparison) (Helicone / Mintlify) <https://www.helicone.ai/blog/joining-mintlify> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Stripe agreed to acquire OpenRouter, with closing pending [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V1-S059: Stripe agrees to acquire OpenRouter to help businesses optimize token routing and usage <https://stripe.com/newsroom/news/stripe-agrees-to-acquire-openrouter> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; V1-S060: Stripe to buy OpenRouter as fintech expands deeper into AI (and TechCrunch 16 Aug 2026 report) (CNBC / TechCrunch) <https://www.cnbc.com/2026/08/19/stripe-openrouter-fintech-ai-model-marketplace-.html> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. None of these owners is a poor steward by default. The point is that independence, exit planning and effective challenge can no longer be assumed from a product's origins; they have to be designed in [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The hybrid column is how that design shows up in a sourcing decision.

**The buy column.** Four components land here, each for a different reason [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}:

- *Foundation models (L1).* Training a foundation model is out of scope for an asset manager [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The firm buys hosted models through its primary cloud and adopts open weights such as Gemma 4 or Mistral; what it builds is the portfolio policy, the qualification suite and the retirement calendar [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Serving (L2).* Buy access and adopt an open engine, such as vLLM, for the exit route [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Self-hosting is a capacity and operating-model decision, not a cost saving [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Retrieval stores (L6).* Reuse the database or search engine the firm already operates. Every new store is another copy of confidential content to secure, retain and exit [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *AI security tooling (C7).* Secrets brokers, scanners and runtime detectors are specialist products. What the firm builds is the architecture that makes any one detector non-critical [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The build column.** The build list is short, and none of it is large software [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}:

- *Tool servers (L4).* Read-only servers owned by each system's team, exposed over MCP or, as the independent alternative to that Anthropic-originated protocol, as OpenAPI-described tools behind the same gateway [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Tools encode access to systems of record; they are the firm's integration surface [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Memory service (L5).* Thin, and later: write through a policy gate, recall, forget by subject, snapshot. The governance is unique to the firm; the storage is not [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Configuration of record (C5).* A release manifest schema, an evaluation gate in continuous integration and pull-request approval rules. Prompt text and approvals are intellectual property and model-risk evidence [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *AI FinOps (C6).* A thin cost dataset in the open FOCUS shape, with allocation rules in source control [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S096: FOCUS Specification licence (FinOps Foundation (FOCUS Working Group)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/FinOps-Open-Cost-and-Usage-Spec/FOCUS_Spec/working_draft/license.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. Allocation rules are finance policy [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Evidence store (C8).* Immutable and keyed by trace ID. A governance workflow tool such as ValidMind, watsonx.governance, Collibra or Credo AI is optional and replaceable on top of it [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The evidence must outlive any tool [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**The hybrid column.** Most of the stack sits here, and the split inside each row is specific [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}:

- *Orchestration (L3).* Adopt open frameworks, build the workflows. LangGraph, Microsoft Agent Framework, Google ADK and Pydantic AI are open and self-hostable [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S001: PyPI JSON metadata: langgraph (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/langgraph/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S067: Microsoft Agent Framework LICENSE <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/microsoft/agent-framework/main/LICENSE> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S114: Agent Development Kit (ADK) 2.0 README (GitHub) (Google) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/google/adk-python/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S003: PyPI JSON metadata: pydantic-ai (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/pydantic-ai/json> accessed 2026-10-07]; the process is the firm's [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Ingestion (L8).* Adopt the parsers, build the envelope: the approved-source register, parse manifest, metadata envelope and lineage events. No ingestion product emits lineage, so the envelope cannot be bought [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S094: unstructured-ingest CHANGELOG (Unstructured Technologies (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Unstructured-IO/unstructured-ingest/main/CHANGELOG.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A1-S096: OpenLineage README (OpenLineage (LF AI & Data)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OpenLineage/OpenLineage/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07].
- *Evaluation (L9).* The datasets and scorers live in source control; the platform of record is replaceable if instrumentation is portable [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S058: Semantic conventions for generative AI systems (status: Development) (OpenTelemetry (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-telemetry/semantic-conventions-genai/main/docs/gen-ai/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A1-S059: GenAI events incl. gen_ai.evaluation.result (OpenTelemetry (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-telemetry/semantic-conventions-genai/main/docs/gen-ai/gen-ai-events.md> accessed 2026-10-07].
- *Gateway (C1).* Gateways are commodity; routes, fallback lists and budgets are the firm's control. Proprietary policy dialects are the main switching cost [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S053: AI gateway capabilities in Azure API Management (Microsoft Learn source, ms.date 29 May 2026) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-docs/main/articles/api-management/genai-gateway-capabilities.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S024: Apigee AI solutions: gateway to enterprise-ready AI (Google Cloud) <https://cloud.google.com/solutions/apigee-ai> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S016: Kong AI Gateway 2.0 Is Now GA — And It's Already Moving Faster (Kong Inc.) <https://konghq.com/blog/product-releases/kong-ai-gateway-2-0-ga> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *Guardrails (C2).* The numeric comparator is domain logic no vendor sells, while detectors churn through acquisitions [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S028: Guardrails AI Joins Harvey <https://www.harvey.ai/blog/guardrails-ai-joins-harvey> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S012: Check Point to Acquire Lakera, Redefining Security for the AI era (Check Point Software) <https://blog.checkpoint.com/security/check-point-to-acquire-lakera-redefining-security-for-the-ai-era/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *Privacy (C3).* One firm API over an adopted detection engine; a vendor-held token map is a hard exit [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S081: skyflow - PyPI JSON metadata (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/skyflow/json> accessed 2026-10-07].
- *Identity (C4).* Buy the identity provider's agent features, such as Entra Agent ID or Okta for AI Agents, and build the policy [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V2-S032: Microsoft Entra releases and announcements (with What's new in Entra Agent ID; Entra blog 'Build AI agents for production with secure identities from day one';  <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/fundamentals/whats-new> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; A6-S100: Okta for AI Agents is Now Generally Available; Okta announces new blueprint for the secure agentic enterprise (Okta, Inc.) <https://www.okta.com/blog/ai/okta-for-ai-agents-general-availability/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].

**"Adopt open source" carries its own diligence.** The review found that "open source" often is not. Arize Phoenix is under ELv2, a source-available licence [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S048: Arize Phoenix LICENSE (Elastic License 2.0) (Arize AI (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Arize-ai/phoenix/main/LICENSE> accessed 2026-10-07]. Firecrawl's server is AGPL-3.0 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S053: Firecrawl README (Firecrawl (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/firecrawl/firecrawl/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. Jina's weights are CC-BY-NC-4.0 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S024: Legal Information - Jina AI (Jina AI (Elastic)) <https://jina.ai/legal/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The Claude Agent SDK is governed by Anthropic's Commercial Terms [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S092: Claude Agent SDK (Python) LICENSE (Anthropic) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anthropics/claude-agent-sdk-python/main/LICENSE> accessed 2026-10-07], and the review names LangGraph or Pydantic AI as the independent alternatives [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Licence review is therefore an architectural gate on the buy and hybrid columns, not a procurement afterthought [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Fine-tuning, the explicit "not yet".** It sits outside the three columns. Under Article 25 of the EU AI Act, a deployer becomes a provider if it substantially modifies a high-risk system or changes an AI system's intended purpose so that it becomes high-risk [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S011: Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) amending Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (EUR-Lex / Official Journal of the EU) <https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2026/1744/oj/eng> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Most asset-management uses are not high-risk; the post's "in some cases" is deliberate [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The other cost applies everywhere: a fine-tuned model is another model to validate, and PRA SS1/23 expects validation and ongoing monitoring that extends to vendor models [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS123: PRA Supervisory Statement SS1/23 'Model risk management principles for banks' (published with PS6/23) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S008: SS1/23 Model risk management principles for banks (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2023/may/model-risk-management-principles-for-banks-ss> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].

**In the worked example.** For the attribution commentary from Chapter 19, the split is concrete. The firm buys the hosted models and the gateway product. It builds the read-only tool onto the attribution engine, the numeric check that compares every figure in the draft against that engine, the regression suite of past commentaries and the evidence pack [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Nothing it builds would interest a vendor, and nothing it buys holds the firm's records [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is **configuration coverage**: the share of production model calls whose prompt, model, tool and retrieval settings all resolve to an approved version identifier the firm holds [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It measures whether the build column is real. If a prompt or a model pin lives only in a vendor console, the firm has bought something it should have built [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Measure it from the evidence the stack already produces: version identifiers on gateway logs and traces, reconciled each day to the release manifest in source control [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Registries in observability platforms can link each trace to the prompt version that produced it; Langfuse does this, for example [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S071: Langfuse docs: Prompt Management overview (Langfuse (ClickHouse)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/langfuse/langfuse-docs/main/content/docs/prompt-management/overview.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07]. Two companion measures make the number honest: pin compliance, the share of model and embedding references pinned to a dated version rather than a moving alias, and trace-to-version linkage [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The starting target is 100% for regulated outputs, with pin compliance also at 100% [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Treat both as a starting point for the firm's own standard, not as an industry benchmark. A falling number usually means a team has found a faster path around the manifest, and that path is worth understanding before it is closed [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 21: build or buy.** A build, hybrid or buy decision for each of the seventeen components: build the evidence store, configuration and tools; buy models, serving and security tooling; hybrid elsewhere. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** Spend goes where the firm differentiates. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** Fine-tuning stays off the list. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** The decision and rationale per component. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Configuration coverage: 100% of settings under the manifest. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"Hybrid doubles the work: we pay for a product and still maintain an interface."** Sometimes true, which is the post's caveat. The defence is to keep the interface thin: one routing contract, one retrieval call, one privacy API [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. If the interface starts to replicate the product's features, the firm is rebuilding the product and should either buy it outright or build it outright [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Chapter 22 sets out where that line falls.

**"Our vendor already holds prompts, datasets and evidence well; why copy them?"** Because those records are what an exit or a regulator needs, and they must outlive the vendor relationship [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The review treats evaluation datasets held only in a vendor interface, or an evidence store held only by a vendor, as unacceptable lock-in for regulated work [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Keeping the canonical copy in source control or a firm archive does not stop the vendor's tool from being useful; it stops it from being indispensable [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Building the numeric check and evaluation datasets is expensive specialist work."** It is the most valuable work in the stack, and no vendor can do it for the firm, because it encodes the firm's own data and judgement [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The regression suite is also the evidence a validator re-performs, so the cost is paid once and used repeatedly [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"A single managed platform would be simpler than seventeen decisions."** For a small firm it may be, and the review's cloud-native stack exists for that case [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Even there, the review keeps the evidence store and the configuration manifest in the firm's hands so that exit remains possible [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Simplicity in sourcing is fine; simplicity that leaves the firm's records inside one supplier is a concentration decision, and should be taken as one [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- For each layer and control, have we written down one decision, build, buy or hybrid, with its reason? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which of our prompts, datasets or approval records exist only in a vendor's tool?
- What share of production calls resolves to an approved, pinned version we hold?
- Where has a hybrid interface grown into a second product?
- Which "open source" components have had a licence review, and which have not?
- Who owns the numeric or domain checks that no vendor sells?
- Is fine-tuning on our not-yet list, with a named trigger for revisiting it?

### In one line

Build the small things that are your control statement, evidence and domain logic, and buy the rest behind an interface you own.


## 22. Portability lives in the artefacts you own

*Putting it together: abstraction strategy, what to abstract and what not to, from Post 22 of the series.*

### The post

Abstraction is how an architecture stays reversible. Too much of it is how an architecture stops moving.

A firm-owned interface in front of anything replaceable is the right default. The trap is applying it everywhere. The classic case is a firm-wide wrapper around agent frameworks: frameworks on top of frameworks. It falls behind every upstream release, hides the features teams chose the framework for, and becomes a second product to maintain. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Abstract where switching is likely and the interface is small. Model routing through one API contract at the gateway. Observability through an open telemetry collector. Evaluation datasets in source control. Credentials through workload identity. Policy as code. Retrieval behind a thin interface. Embeddings with a version on every vector. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Do not abstract the framework. Keep the workflow specification, prompts, tools and evaluations outside it, and rewriting a well-specified workflow becomes bounded work. That is the real portability. Do not build a plug-in architecture for one workflow with one model step. Do not put a layer between every query and the database. And do not build an abstraction to hide a model you never pinned. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The signal is framework currency: days behind the pinned framework's latest security fix, held inside the patch window, for example 30 days. Wrappers make that number drift. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to ask, of every proposed abstraction, what switching would cost without it. If the answer is a bounded rewrite, skip the abstraction. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: this is a judgement, and reasonable architects draw the line differently. A timed switch of one component settles most arguments.

Portability lives in the artefacts you own, not in the layers you add.

![Portability lives in the artefacts you own](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P22.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 22. Abstract where switching is likely and the interface is small; do not wrap the framework. Ask what switching would cost without the layer. Generic: no vendor names.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

Chapter 21 argued for a thin firm-owned interface in front of anything the firm may replace. This chapter is the counterweight. Every abstraction has a running cost: it must be maintained, documented, secured and kept current with whatever sits beneath it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The question is never whether abstraction is good in general, but whether a particular interface earns that cost [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What earns an abstraction.** The review supports ten firm-owned interfaces. They share three properties: switching is likely within the planning horizon, the interface is small, and the content behind it belongs to the firm [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

- *Model routing.* One OpenAI-compatible contract to the gateway, with no provider SDKs in application code. Every gateway assessed exposes or accepts the format [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S015: LiteLLM docs: MCP Overview, Agent Gateway (A2A), Caching, Budgets/Rate Limits, v1.89.0 release notes (BerriAI (LiteLLM)) <https://docs.litellm.ai/docs/mcp> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S049: Portkey AI Gateway README (GitHub) (Portkey (Palo Alto Networks)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Portkey-AI/gateway/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S053: AI gateway capabilities in Azure API Management (Microsoft Learn source, ms.date 29 May 2026) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-docs/main/articles/api-management/genai-gateway-capabilities.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S061: agentgateway README (GitHub) (agentgateway project (Linux Foundation)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/agentgateway/agentgateway/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S063: Envoy AI Gateway / Agent Router README (GitHub) (Envoy AI Gateway project (Agentic AI Foundation)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/envoyproxy/ai-gateway/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07].
- *Observability.* A firm-owned OpenTelemetry Collector with redaction and a pinned semantic-convention version. Every evaluation and observability product assessed ingests OpenTelemetry or OpenInference [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S032: OpenTelemetry (OTEL) for LLM Observability - Langfuse (Langfuse (ClickHouse)) <https://langfuse.com/integrations/native/opentelemetry> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A1-S037: Introducing End-to-End OpenTelemetry Support in LangSmith (LangChain, Inc.) <https://www.langchain.com/blog/end-to-end-opentelemetry-langsmith> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A1-S042: OpenTelemetry (OTel) - Braintrust docs (Braintrust Data, Inc.) <https://www.braintrust.dev/docs/integrations/opentelemetry> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A1-S049: OpenInference LICENSE (Apache-2.0) (Arize AI (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Arize-ai/openinference/main/LICENSE> accessed 2026-10-07].
- *Evaluation.* Datasets and scorers versioned in source control, with results emitted as standard evaluation events [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S059: GenAI events incl. gen_ai.evaluation.result (OpenTelemetry (CNCF)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-telemetry/semantic-conventions-genai/main/docs/gen-ai/gen-ai-events.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. The datasets are the regression baseline and the validation evidence [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Credentials.* Workload identity, a vault and on-behalf-of tokens, with no standing secrets in agents [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S033: Vault release notes 2.x; IBM Vault Self-Managed 2.x.x lifecycle page (HashiCorp (IBM)) <https://developer.hashicorp.com/vault/docs/updates/release-notes> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *Policy.* OPA (Rego) or Cedar in source control, with tests; both are open source [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S088: Open Policy Agent LICENSE (Open Policy Agent project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-policy-agent/opa/main/LICENSE> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S045: Cedar README (GitHub) (Cedar project) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/cedar-policy/cedar/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07].
- *Retrieval.* One thin call taking a query, filters, tenant and top-k, and returning identifiers and scores. Proprietary query APIs differ [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S051: pinecone (Python SDK) - PyPI (PyPI / Pinecone Systems, Inc.) <https://pypi.org/project/pinecone/> accessed 2026-10-07; A2-S056: turbopuffer (Python SDK) - PyPI (PyPI / turbopuffer) <https://pypi.org/project/turbopuffer/> accessed 2026-10-07; A2-S092: Amazon S3 Vectors (feature page) (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/s3/features/vectors/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *Privacy.* One privacy-service API to detect, transform and re-identify, so detectors can be replaced if recognisers and tests are the firm's [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Embedding and reranking.* One service, with the model version recorded on every vector, because switching the model forces re-embedding [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Memory.* Write, recall, forget by subject and snapshot. Memory semantics differ by product [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S081: Mem0 docs source: Open Source migration to the new memory algorithm (Mem0 (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mem0ai/mem0/main/docs/migration/oss-v2-to-v3.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S003: PyPI JSON metadata: graphiti-core (Python Package Index (PyPI)) <https://pypi.org/pypi/graphiti-core/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S109: Vertex AI Agent Engine Memory Bank overview / generate memories / Memory Bank REST reference (docs.cloud.google.com, now 'Agent Platform Memory Bank') (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/vertex-ai/generative-ai/docs/agent-engine/memory-bank/overview> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *Configuration.* One fetch call with a file-based fallback, because each registry has its own SDK [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S072: Langfuse docs: Prompt version control (Langfuse (ClickHouse)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/langfuse/langfuse-docs/main/content/docs/prompt-management/features/prompt-version-control.mdx> accessed 2026-10-07; A7-S004: promptlayer on PyPI (JSON API) (PyPI / PromptLayer) <https://pypi.org/pypi/promptlayer/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A7-S089: LaunchDarkly Server-Side AI SDK for Python README <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/launchdarkly/python-server-sdk-ai/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07].

None of these is a framework. Most are a single function signature, a schema or a configuration file [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**What does not earn one.** The review names five places where adding a layer is the mistake [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}:

- *Agent frameworks.* Do not wrap LangGraph, Microsoft Agent Framework or Google ADK in a firm abstraction [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The frameworks are open source and self-hostable [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S001: PyPI JSON metadata: langgraph (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/langgraph/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S067: Microsoft Agent Framework LICENSE <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/microsoft/agent-framework/main/LICENSE> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S114: Agent Development Kit (ADK) 2.0 README (GitHub) (Google) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/google/adk-python/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S003: PyPI JSON metadata: pydantic-ai (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/pydantic-ai/json> accessed 2026-10-07], so the lock-in is already classed as acceptable [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Their APIs are framework-specific [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S039: LangGraph Persistence / Durable execution / Interrupts - Docs by LangChain <https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/durable-execution> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], but rewriting a well-specified workflow is bounded work [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Single-workflow plug-ins.* A deterministic workflow with one model step does not need a plug-in architecture [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Plain database access.* pgvector is reached through SQL joined to entitlement tables; the thin retrieval interface sits above it for agents, not between every query and the database [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Store-bundled features.* Several stores now run embedding models themselves, among them MongoDB Automated Embedding, Elastic's `semantic_text` and Pinecone's integrated inference [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S078: Unlocking AI Search: Introducing Automated Embedding in MongoDB Vector Search <https://www.mongodb.com/company/blog/product-release-announcements/unlocking-ai-search-introducing-automated-embedding-in-mongodb-vector-search> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S133: Elastic 9.5: Columnar, VectorDB index mode & auto-calibration, and AI-driven alert triage <https://www.elastic.co/blog/whats-new-elastic-9-5-0> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A2-S051: pinecone (Python SDK) - PyPI (PyPI / Pinecone Systems, Inc.) <https://pypi.org/project/pinecone/> accessed 2026-10-07]. Use them only if the model is pinned and the raw text is kept outside the store; an abstraction that hides an unpinned model hides the problem, not the dependency [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Vendor model extras.* Prompt-caching formats and vendor tool-use extensions belong behind adapters with a feature-free fallback path [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why wrapping frameworks fails now.** The frameworks are moving quickly. Microsoft Agent Framework reached general availability on 2 April 2026 as the successor to Semantic Kernel and AutoGen, with AutoGen in maintenance mode [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S008: PyPI JSON metadata: agent-framework (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/agent-framework/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S021: PyPI JSON metadata: autogen-agentchat (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/autogen-agentchat/json> accessed 2026-10-07; V1-S050: PyPI JSON metadata: agent-framework (1.0.0 uploaded 2026-04-02; latest 1.20.0) (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/agent-framework/json> accessed 2026-10-08]. Google ADK 2.0 added a Workflow Runtime [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S114: Agent Development Kit (ADK) 2.0 README (GitHub) (Google) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/google/adk-python/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. LangGraph has published three checkpoint deserialisation advisories since 2025, all patched [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L3-S007: LangGraph security advisories (GitHub) and NVD entries (LangChain / GitHub / NIST NVD) <https://github.com/langchain-ai/langgraph/security> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. A firm-wide wrapper sits between each of those changes and the teams that need them [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Every security fix has to pass through the wrapper's own release, and every new capability waits for the wrapper to expose it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The portable assets are elsewhere: the workflow specification as a reviewed document, prompts under configuration control, tool contracts in the firm's repository, and evaluation suites [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. With those in hand, a rewrite onto a different framework is bounded work, and the evaluation suite proves the result [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

A related change helps. The hyperscalers now sell framework-agnostic agent runtimes; Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, for example, hosts open frameworks [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S116: Bedrock AgentCore SDK README (GitHub) (AWS) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/aws/bedrock-agentcore-sdk-python/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07]. The runtime decision and the framework decision can be taken separately, which further reduces the case for a firm wrapper [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Where multi-vendor is necessary, and where it only adds complexity.** Abstraction and multi-vendor redundancy are often argued together, and the review separates them [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Five things genuinely need two:

- *Two unrelated model vendors*, qualified on the same suite. A single vendor's suspension or retirement stops the service: Anthropic's top tier, Claude Fable 5, was made unavailable from 12 June to 1 July 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V2-S004: Claude Fable - Anthropic <https://www.anthropic.com/claude/fable> accessed 2026-10-08], and Gemini Flash versions live for months [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L1-S003: Gemini 3.7 Flash model page and related lifecycle pages (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/models/gemini/3-7-flash> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The review pairs any Claude route with GPT-6.1 Sol, Gemini 3.8 Flash or Mistral Medium 3.5 as a qualified alternative [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *An open-weight model qualified on vLLM*, the credible stressed exit that PRA SS2/21 expects firms to plan for [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S048: SS2/21 Outsourcing and third party risk management (and PS7/26, March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/supervisory-statement/2021/ss221-march-21.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *Two red-team tools*, one independent of the model vendor under test; Promptfoo's announced owner is a model vendor [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S024: OpenAI to acquire Promptfoo <https://openai.com/index/openai-to-acquire-promptfoo/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *Two guardrail detectors* from different owners, because detectors are being absorbed by security vendors [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S012: Check Point to Acquire Lakera, Redefining Security for the AI era (Check Point Software) <https://blog.checkpoint.com/security/check-point-to-acquire-lakera-redefining-security-for-the-ai-era/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S014: Palo Alto Networks Completes Acquisition of Protect AI <https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/company/press/2025/palo-alto-networks-completes-acquisition-of-protect-ai> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A6-S028: Guardrails AI Joins Harvey <https://www.harvey.ai/blog/guardrails-ai-joins-harvey> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *Two gateway deployments* of the same product, because the gateway is a single point of failure by design [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Five things only add complexity: two orchestration frameworks in one language estate, two vector stores for one corpus, two observability platforms of record, two identity providers for agents, and a second cloud's agent stack for the same use case [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Each doubles the work of securing, retaining and evidencing, with no resilience gain [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The signal is **framework currency**: the number of days the pinned orchestration framework is behind its latest security fix [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It is the most direct measure of whether the framework layer is being kept, or merely kept running.

Measure it through dependency scanning in the security pipeline, recording for each workflow the pinned framework version, the date of the latest upstream security fix, and the gap between them [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Report the maximum across workflows, not the average, because one stale workflow is the exposure [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The review's example target is the patch window, for example 30 days [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Treat that as a starting point set by the firm's own vulnerability-management policy, not as a benchmark. If a wrapper exists, measure the gap at the wrapper as well as the framework; the difference between the two is the cost the wrapper adds [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A companion check, from the caveat in the post, is a timed switch: move one component, such as the vector store behind the retrieval interface, to an alternative in a test environment, and record how long it took [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 22: thin interfaces.** Thin firm-owned interfaces where switching is likely (routing contract, telemetry, datasets, privacy, retrieval) and none around the agent framework. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** Changing a vendor is a contained change. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** No firm-wide wrapper around frameworks. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** The interface inventory. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Framework currency: patches within an agreed window. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"Without a wrapper, every team will use the framework differently."** That is a standards problem, not an abstraction problem. A reference workflow, a code-review checklist and shared evaluation suites constrain usage without hiding the framework [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Standardising on one framework per language estate removes most of the variation on its own [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"A rewrite is never as bounded as architects claim."** It is bounded only if the portable assets exist [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A workflow whose prompts are embedded in code, whose tools are framework-specific functions and which has no regression suite is expensive to move with or without a wrapper [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The timed switch in the caveat is the honest test; if the rewrite estimate cannot be demonstrated on one component, the claim should not be relied on [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Thin interfaces become thick ones over time."** They do, which is why each one needs an owner and a scope written down [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A retrieval interface that starts to add ranking, caching and query rewriting has become a product [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The review's guidance on the memory and retrieval interfaces is deliberately minimal for that reason: a handful of operations, returning identifiers and scores [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Supervisors expect exit plans, and an abstraction layer shows we have one."** PRA SS2/21 expects documented exit plans that are tested, including for stressed exit [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S048: SS2/21 Outsourcing and third party risk management (and PS7/26, March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/supervisory-statement/2021/ss221-march-21.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. A layer is a design intention; a drill is evidence [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A gateway route moved to a second vendor by configuration, or an index rebuilt from source in a measured time, says more to a supervisor than any interface diagram [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- For each abstraction we maintain, what would switching cost without it? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- How many days behind its latest security fix is each production framework?
- Are our prompts, tool contracts, workflow specifications and evaluations stored outside the framework?
- Where do we run two of something that only needs one?
- Where do we run one of something that needs two?
- Has any team ever timed a switch of one component?
- Who owns each thin interface, and what is out of its scope?

### In one line

Abstract the small, likely-to-change interfaces, keep the framework unwrapped, and put portability into artefacts the firm owns.


## 23. Some lock-in is a good trade

*Putting it together: lock-in by layer and control, from Post 23 of the series.*

### The post

Some lock-in is a good trade. The skill is knowing which.

Total avoidance is not a strategy. It produces the lowest common denominator everywhere, and the firm pays for portability it will never use. The better question, layer by layer, is what it would cost to leave, and whether that cost is acceptable, manageable with an abstraction, or unacceptable for a regulated workload. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Acceptable: open-weight models the firm holds, open serving engines, open orchestration frameworks, a stateless reranker, a vector index that can be rebuilt from source. Leaving costs little. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Manageable: a proprietary model behind the gateway with a qualified second vendor, a gateway product, the workforce identity provider, a managed agent runtime. Each is fine with the right interface and a rehearsed exit. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

Unacceptable: a single proprietary model vendor behind an important business service; evaluation datasets that exist only in a vendor console; prompts and change history held only in a vendor registry; credentials held in a third party's multi-tenant cloud; an evidence store held only by a vendor. Each turns an exit into a reconstruction. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The pattern is consistent. Lock-in to a product is usually tolerable. Lock-in of the firm's own records, meaning its evidence, configuration, datasets and credentials, is not. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The retrieval layer shows the signal: rebuild time from approved sources with a pinned embedding model, inside the exit-plan tolerance and tested twice a year. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The leadership move is to classify each layer once, record it in the exit plan, and review it whenever ownership changes. [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

The honest caveat: the classification moves. A tolerable dependency becomes a concern when its owner changes.

Choose dependencies deliberately. The accidental ones are expensive.

![Some lock-in is a good trade](Enterprise_GenAI_Stack_Oct2026/08_Graphic/linkedin/P23.png){width=4.2in}

*Figure 23. Lock-in to a product is usually tolerable; lock-in of the firm's own records is not. Classify each layer once, record it in the exit plan, and review it when ownership changes. Generic: no vendor names.* [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Behind the post

**Three categories, defined by the cost of leaving.** The review consolidates every layer and control chapter's lock-in assessment into one table. "Acceptable" means the switching cost is low. "Manageable" means acceptable with a stated abstraction. "Unacceptable" means the arrangement should not be adopted for a regulated workload without that abstraction [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The categories describe arrangements, not products: the same product can sit in two columns depending on how it is used [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. A vector index is acceptable if it can be rebuilt from source, and part of an unacceptable arrangement if the store also holds the only copy of the entitlement logic [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Acceptable: where leaving costs little.** The common thread is that the firm holds what it needs to run without the supplier [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

- *Models and serving (L1, L2).* Apache-2.0 or MIT open weights held by the firm, and open serving engines. vLLM and SGLang are Apache-2.0 and OpenAI-compatible [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S009: PyPI JSON metadata: vllm (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/vllm/json> accessed 2026-10-07; A4-S010: PyPI JSON metadata: sglang (Python Package Index) <https://pypi.org/pypi/sglang/json> accessed 2026-10-07].
- *Orchestration (L3).* Open frameworks such as LangGraph, Microsoft Agent Framework, Google ADK and Pydantic AI [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Tools (L4).* MCP and A2A as open specifications under the Agentic AI Foundation [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A3-S018: MCP joins the Agentic AI Foundation (9 Dec 2025) (Model Context Protocol project (blog)) <https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2025-12-09-mcp-joins-agentic-ai-foundation/> accessed 2026-10-07; A3-S116: A2A joins AAIF's open agentic stack (17 Aug 2026) (Agentic AI Foundation (AAIF)) <https://aaif.io/blog/a2a-joins-aaif> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. MCP originated at Anthropic; the independent alternative is OpenAPI-described tools behind the same gateway [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Retrieval (L6, L7).* A vector index rebuildable from source, and a stateless reranker [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Controls.* Open policy languages, model signing, and lineage on OpenLineage, an open specification under neutral governance [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S041: OpenLineage README (OpenLineage (LF AI & Data)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OpenLineage/OpenLineage/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07].

**Manageable: fine with an interface and a rehearsed exit.** These are the dependencies most firms will, and should, accept [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

- *A proprietary model behind the gateway with a qualified second vendor.* Switching becomes re-qualification rather than re-engineering, provided prompts and schemas are neutral [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *A gateway product.* Replaceable if routes, budgets and policies are documented as data. Proprietary policy dialects are the main switching effort [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A6-S053: AI gateway capabilities in Azure API Management (Microsoft Learn source, ms.date 29 May 2026) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-docs/main/articles/api-management/genai-gateway-capabilities.md> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S024: Apigee AI solutions: gateway to enterprise-ready AI (Google Cloud) <https://cloud.google.com/solutions/apigee-ai> accessed 2026-10-07; A6-S016: Kong AI Gateway 2.0 Is Now GA — And It's Already Moving Faster (Kong Inc.) <https://konghq.com/blog/product-releases/kong-ai-gateway-2-0-ga> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *The workforce identity provider*, with agents following the humans' directory [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *A managed agent runtime.* Proprietary APIs, but the runtimes host open frameworks [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S116: Bedrock AgentCore SDK README (GitHub) (AWS) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/aws/bedrock-agentcore-sdk-python/main/README.md> accessed 2026-10-07].
- *Hyperscaler services*, with concentration noted. The UK designated AWS, Google Cloud, Microsoft and Oracle as critical third parties [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-UK-CTP: UK Critical Third Parties regime (FSMA 2023 / FSMA 2000 s312L; PRA/FCA/BoE CTP rules) and the Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S023: The Critical Third Parties (Designation) Regulations 2026 (SI 2026/777); BoE/FCA/HMT announcements (HM Treasury / legislation.gov.uk / Bank of England) <https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2026/777/made> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], and S3 Vectors, for example, is AWS-only [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S092: Amazon S3 Vectors (feature page) (Amazon Web Services) <https://aws.amazon.com/s3/features/vectors/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].

**Unacceptable: where an exit becomes a reconstruction.** Here the evidence is specific, and recent.

- *A single proprietary model vendor for an important business service.* Anthropic's top tier, Claude Fable 5, was made unavailable on 12 June 2026 and restored from 1 July 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V2-S004: Claude Fable - Anthropic <https://www.anthropic.com/claude/fable> accessed 2026-10-08]; a Gemini Flash version released on 13 August 2026 retires on 28 January 2027 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L1-S003: Gemini 3.7 Flash model page and related lifecycle pages (Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform) (Google Cloud) <https://docs.cloud.google.com/gemini-enterprise-agent-platform/models/gemini/3-7-flash> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The review therefore treats any Claude route as one leg of a portfolio, with GPT-6.1 Sol, Gemini 3.8 Flash or Mistral Medium 3.5 as the qualified alternative [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Vendor-hosted agent state and visual builders.* OpenAI's Agent Builder shuts down on 30 November 2026 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S054: OpenAI Deprecations (Agent Builder) and Introducing AgentKit update <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/deprecations> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S051: OpenAI API Deprecations (Agent Builder row); Introducing AgentKit (updated) <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/deprecations> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. The Mistral Agents API, OpenAI's hosted Agents API and Claude Managed Agents keep state and transcripts in the vendor's cloud by default [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A4-S057: Build AI agents with the Mistral Agents API (and Agents & Conversations / Handoffs docs) (Mistral AI) <https://mistral.ai/news/agents-api/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A4-S055: OpenAI API Changelog (Agents API public beta, Sep 2026) and Introducing the Agents API <https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/changelog> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A4-S123: API and data retention - Claude Platform Docs (Anthropic) <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/zero-data-retention> accessed 2026-10-07]; for regulated workflows the review keeps state in the firm's estate, on an open framework's checkpointer [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.
- *Credentials in a third party's multi-tenant cloud.* Composio, a broker that holds users' OAuth tokens for third-party applications, disclosed a May 2026 incident in which, by its own account, about 0.3% of active connections leaked and 5,241 API keys were flagged as possibly exposed [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — B-L4-S007: Composio May 2026 security incident update <https://composio.dev/blog/composio-may-2026-security-incident> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]].
- *A knowledge engine as the only home of curated knowledge.* Such engines compile data into vendor-specific artefacts queried in a proprietary language [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S073: General Availability of Pinecone Nexus Proves Knowledge Drives Real Outcomes for Agentic AI <https://www.pinecone.io/newsroom/general-availability-of-pinecone-nexus-proves-knowledge-drives-real-outcomes-for-agentic-ai/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].
- *The firm's records held only by a vendor:* evaluation datasets, prompts and change history, guardrail policy and test sets, a token map and its keys, cost-allocation rules, and the evidence store. The evidence store matters most, because it is the regulatory record and must meet retention duties such as Article 26 of the EU AI Act where it applies [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-EUAIA: Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (Artificial Intelligence Act), as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (Digital Omnibus on AI) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S016: AI Act Article 26 Obligations of deployers; Article 25 Responsibilities along the AI value chain (European Commission AI Act Service Desk) <https://ai-act-service-desk.ec.europa.eu/en/ai-act/article-26> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]].

**The pattern.** Read down the unacceptable column and most of its entries are not products at all. They are the firm's own records, held in someone else's system [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. In the figure, the darker cells mark them: credentials, canonical memory, evaluation datasets, guardrail policy, the privacy token map, prompts, allocation rules and evidence [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. That is the post's central claim. Lock-in to a product is usually tolerable, because a product can be replaced if the records survive. Lock-in of the records is not, because they cannot be re-created after the fact [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why the classification moves.** The caveat in the post is not a hedge. Ownership changes reclassify dependencies. Palo Alto Networks now owns both Portkey and Protect AI within Prisma AIRS [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A7-S014: Palo Alto Networks Completes Acquisition of Protect AI <https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/company/press/2025/palo-alto-networks-completes-acquisition-of-protect-ai> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A7-S016: Palo Alto Networks Inc Form 10-Q for quarter ended 30 April 2026 (Palo Alto Networks / US SEC) <https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0001327567/000132756726000015/panw-20260430.htm> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]; for a firm using that gateway, a security platform bundled with the gateway without an exit plan becomes a live concern, which the review classes as unacceptable [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Stripe has agreed to acquire OpenRouter, with closing pending [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — V1-S059: Stripe agrees to acquire OpenRouter to help businesses optimize token routing and usage <https://stripe.com/newsroom/news/stripe-agrees-to-acquire-openrouter> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]; V1-S060: Stripe to buy OpenRouter as fintech expands deeper into AI (and TechCrunch 16 Aug 2026 report) (CNBC / TechCrunch) <https://www.cnbc.com/2026/08/19/stripe-openrouter-fintech-ai-model-marketplace-.html> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]. Dynatrace now owns Arize, and ClickHouse owns Langfuse [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S045: Dynatrace Completes Acquisition of Arize, Extending AI Observability Across the Full Development Lifecycle (Dynatrace, Inc.) <https://ir.dynatrace.com/news-events/press-releases/detail/439/dynatrace-completes-acquisition-of-arize-extending-ai-observability-across-the-full-development-lifecycle> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A1-S021: ClickHouse raises $400M Series D, acquires Langfuse, launches Postgres (ClickHouse, Inc.) <https://clickhouse.com/blog/clickhouse-raises-400-million-series-d-acquires-langfuse-launches-postgres> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. Each event should trigger a review of the affected row, not a reflexive switch [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**Why it matters to a regulated firm.** PRA SS2/21 expects documented exit plans, tested, including for stressed exit [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S048: SS2/21 Outsourcing and third party risk management (and PS7/26, March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/-/media/boe/files/prudential-regulation/supervisory-statement/2021/ss221-march-21.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. DORA requires a register of information for ICT third-party arrangements, with exit strategies for critical or important functions [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-DORA: Regulation (EU) 2022/2554 Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S021: Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA) pages and RoI reporting FAQ (ESMA / EBA / EIOPA) <https://www.esma.europa.eu/esmas-activities/digital-finance-and-innovation/digital-operational-resilience-act-dora> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. From 18 March 2027, PS7/26 and FCA PS26/2 require notification of material third-party arrangements [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-PRA-SS221: PRA SS2/21 'Outsourcing and third party risk management' (revised by PS7/26, March 2026) (see regulatory_facts.json); R-FCA-SYSC8: FCA Handbook SYSC 8 (Outsourcing); FG16/5 'Guidance for firms outsourcing to the cloud and other third-party IT services'; PS26/2 operational incident and third-party reporting (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S062: PS7/26 Operational incident and third-party reporting (PRA) and FCA PS26/2; revised SS2/21 (March 2026) (Bank of England / PRA; FCA) <https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/prudential-regulation/publication/2026/march/operational-incident-and-third-party-reporting-policy-statement> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. IOSCO flags concentration risk from reliance on a few AI technology providers [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — R-INTL-AI-ASSETMGMT: IOSCO Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (FR/02/2026) and ESMA Public Statement on AI and investment services (30 May 2024) (see regulatory_facts.json); A8-S058: IOSCO FR/02/2026 Supervisory Toolkit for AI Use in Capital Markets (May 2026) <https://www.iosco.org/library/pubdocs/pdf/IOSCOPD823.pdf> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. The lock-in table is the working document behind all four: it says which exits are cheap, which need rehearsal, and which must be designed out before go-live [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### What good looks like

The post picks one signal to make the classification testable: **index rebuild time** at the retrieval layer [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. It is defined as the time to rebuild the retrieval index from the source of truth, the approved corpus, with the pinned embedding model [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

Measure it with a timed dry run: take the approved-source register, re-parse or reuse the stored parses, re-embed with the pinned model version, rebuild the index in a clean environment, and run the retrieval regression set against it [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Record the elapsed time and the regression result together; a fast rebuild that fails the regression set has not passed [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

The review's starting target is a rebuild time inside the exit-plan tolerance, tested twice a year [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The tolerance is the firm's own, set by the important business service the index supports; it is a starting point, not a benchmark. The same drill proves three things at once: the backup works, the exit works, and the embedding pin is real [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Equivalent drills exist for other rows, such as the gateway's time to switch a route to a pre-qualified alternative [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### In the worked example

**Step 23: exit tested.** Lock-in accepted knowingly per component, with the firm's own records (evidence, configuration, datasets, credentials) kept out of any vendor's hands, and an index rebuild drilled. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

- **Now works:** The agent survives the loss of any single vendor. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Must never:** No vendor-held store is ever the only copy of the firm's evidence. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Evidence added:** Exit and rebuild drill results. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- **Signal:** Index rebuild time, tested twice a year. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

### Objections worth taking seriously

**"Classifying seventeen layers and controls is a bureaucratic exercise."** It is done once, and most rows take minutes, because the review's table provides a starting position for each [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. The work that matters is the handful of rows that land in "unacceptable" for the firm's actual estate, and those are usually obvious once the question is asked [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Our proprietary vendor is more capable; a second vendor dilutes quality."** The second vendor does not need to be equal on every task; it needs to pass the same qualification suite for the routes it may serve [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Capability differences then become a measured trade-off rather than an assumed one. A single-vendor arrangement behind an important business service remains the review's clearest unacceptable case, whichever vendor it is [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Keeping canonical copies of our records duplicates what the vendor stores."** It does, deliberately. The copy in source control or a firm archive is the canonical one; the vendor's tool becomes a cache or a convenience [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}. Free tiers of evaluation platforms keep data for between 15 and 60 days [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S047: Arize AX Pricing: AI agent observability and evaluation (Arize AI) <https://arize.com/pricing/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A1-S031: Pricing - Langfuse (Langfuse (ClickHouse)) <https://langfuse.com/pricing> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; A1-S123: Comet pricing (Opik plans) (Comet ML, Inc.) <https://www.comet.com/site/pricing/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]], so evidence held only there may simply no longer exist when it is needed [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

**"Open source is acceptable by definition."** Not quite. The acceptable column assumes a licence the firm has read. Arize Phoenix is under ELv2, a source-available licence [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A1-S048: Arize Phoenix LICENSE (Elastic License 2.0) (Arize AI (GitHub)) <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Arize-ai/phoenix/main/LICENSE> accessed 2026-10-07]; Weaviate is moving to open core [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S115: weaviate/weaviate (README and licence files; PR #13360) (Weaviate) <https://github.com/weaviate/weaviate> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]; V1-S070: weaviate/weaviate README (Community vs Enterprise Edition), releases, PR #13360, PR #12942 (Weaviate (GitHub)) <https://github.com/weaviate/weaviate> accessed 2026-10-08 [search-tool extract]]; Jina's weights are CC-BY-NC-4.0 [VF]{custom-style="Claim Label"}^[Verified fact — A2-S024: Legal Information - Jina AI (Jina AI (Elastic)) <https://jina.ai/legal/> accessed 2026-10-07 [search-tool extract]]. An open component lands in the acceptable column only after licence review, and Weaviate's case shows that a licence can change after adoption [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}.

### Questions for your team

- Have we classified each layer and control as acceptable, manageable or unacceptable, and recorded it in the exit plan? [Rec]{custom-style="Claim Label"}
- Which of our records (evidence, prompts, datasets, credentials, policies) exist only in a vendor's system?
- When did we last time a rebuild of our retrieval index from source?
- Which important business service depends on a single proprietary model vendor?
- Which suppliers in our stack have changed owner this year, and did anyone review the affected rows?
- Where do we hold agent state or credentials outside our own estate?

### In one line

Accept lock-in to products deliberately, and never accept lock-in of the firm's own records.


## 24. Select the control plane first

*Putting it together: the recommended stack, and what to leave out, from Post 24 of the series.*

### The post

Twelve weeks ago this series started from a popular diagram of the AI stack. On this review, 39 of its 80 product tiles were out of date. The diagram was a catalogue. The enterprise problem is a control system. [AJ]{custom-style="Claim Label"}

So here is the selection, on one page.

First, a firm-owned control and evidence plane: one gateway of record for every model, tool and agent call; evaluation and observability from day one; an evidence store the firm owns; one privacy service; agent identities in the workforce directory; source control as the configuration of record.
